License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2606.00643v1 [stat.ML] 30 May 2026 
Taming the Loss Landscape of PINNs with Noisy Feynman–Kac Supervision: Operator Preconditioning and Non-Asymptotic Error Bounds 
Nathanael Tepakbong Hanyu Hu Chengyu Liu Xiang Zhou Abstract 
Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) often train slowly or fail to converge on challenging partial differential equations (PDEs), a behavior recently linked to severely ill-conditioned loss landscapes inherited from the underlying differential operator. We study PINNs augmented with a pointwise data-fidelity term, added at a few points in the domain to the standard residual and boundary losses. We show that this supervision term acts as an operator-level preconditioner: for suitable weights, our comparison bounds guarantee a substantially smaller condition number than under the standard PINN loss, independently of how the pointwise labels are obtained. For a broad class of PDEs admitting a Feynman-Kac (FK) representation, we generate such labels by Monte Carlo averages of the FK functional, resulting in what we call “FK-PINNs”, and using the excess risk decomposition approach, we derive non-asymptotic L 2 ​ ( Ω ) L^{2}(\Omega) -error bounds for FK-PINNs with tanh \tanh activation trained by finitely many steps of gradient descent. Along the way, we establish pseudo-dimension bounds for first- and second-order derivatives of tanh \tanh neural networks, which are of independent interest and, to the best of our knowledge, new. Numerical experiments on Poisson, Schrödinger, mean exit time, and committor problems corroborate the theory, and show that FK-PINNs can successfully solve PDEs for which standard PINNs exhibit severe failure modes. 
Physics-informed neural networks, Feynman–Kac, Monte Carlo 
1 Introduction 

Partial differential equations (PDEs) underpin much of computational science and engineering. While classical numerical methods, such as finite elements, are mature and accurate, their cost grows quickly with dimension and geometric complexity, making large-scale or high-resolution simulations prohibitively difficult in numerous practical scenarios. Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) offer a mesh-free alternative: a neural network u θ u_{\theta} approximates the solution, and training is driven by a loss that penalizes PDE residuals and boundary-condition violations at sampled collocation points (Raissi et al. , 2019 ) . PINNs have been investigated in a variety of settings, including forward and inverse problems, parameter estimation, and coupled multi-physics models (Cuomo et al. , 2022 ) . 

Although the idea of enforcing physical constraints within expressive neural networks is conceptually appealing, many studies report severe training pathologies. For moderately stiff or high-frequency problems, optimization can be extremely slow and may fail to reduce the residual even with expressive architectures (Krishnapriyan et al. , 2021 ; Wang et al. , 2021 ) . Moreover, the training dynamics are highly sensitive to hyperparameters, sampling strategies, and loss weights, hence small changes in either of these can lead to vastly different outcomes. Various heuristics, such as adaptive sampling, curriculum training, residual/gradient-based re-weighting or domain decomposition have been proposed to alleviate these issues (Wu et al. , 2023 ; Krishnapriyan et al. , 2021 ) . Although such approaches have been shown to help in specific cases, there is still no generally reliable way to obtain well-conditioned, robust PINN objectives. 

1.1 An Operator-Conditioning Perspective on PINN Training 

Recent work investigates this issue through an operator-conditioning and loss landscape lens. In particular, De Ryck et al. ( 2024 ) analyze gradient descent on PINN objectives and relate the dynamics to the spectrum of the Hermitian square ℒ ∗ ​ ℒ \mathcal{L}^{\ast}\mathcal{L} of the underlying PDE operator ℒ \mathcal{L} . Closely related analyses likewise tie PINN training difficulty to the conditioning of PDE-induced operators, their associated kernels, or PINN loss Hessians, and explain through theoretical and numerical results the resulting optimization pathologies (Rathore et al. , 2024 ; Wang et al. , 2022 ; Chen et al. , 2024 ; Wang et al. , 2026 ) . In this view, a “Hessian-like” operator governs training: when it is ill-conditioned—with widely spread eigenvalues and nearly null modes—some parameter directions evolve orders of magnitude more slowly than others, leading to stagnation and failure to fit the residual even when the target solution lies well within the hypothesis class. 

This operator-centric view suggests that successful training of PINNs is not just a matter of expressive networks or dense collocation sampling, but also of constructing losses and optimization schemes whose associated operators are well conditioned. Several prior works have indeed proposed to modify the traditional gradient-based neural network training algorithms by schemes which take into account the natural function space geometry associated with PINNs (Müller and Zeinhofer, 2023 ; Schwencke and Furtlehner, 2025 ; Chen et al. , 2025 ; Wang et al. , 2022 , 2026 ) . In many cases, these modified training schemes can be interpreted as applying a preconditioning on the operator (De Ryck et al. , 2024 ) . 

Complementary to these optimizer-based approaches, we instead propose to modify the training objective itself: we design preconditioned PINN losses that augment the standard residual and boundary terms with lightweight “mass” or data-anchoring penalties, which effectively precondition the operator governing the training dynamics. Such data could be obtained in a number of manners, through e.g. coarse FEM computations, experimental data or any kind of numerical simulation. In this work, we propose constructing these penalties using probabilistic representations of PDE solutions via the Feynman–Kac formula, yielding a simple, scalable, and mesh-free modification of the PINN loss that is completely agnostic to architecture and optimizer choice. 

1.2 Feynman–Kac Supervision as Operator Preconditioning 

For a broad class of linear second-order elliptic and parabolic PDEs, the Feynman–Kac (FK) formula represents the solution u ⋆ ​ ( x ) u^{\star}(x) as the expectation of a functional of a stochastic process ( X t ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t})_{t\geq 0} solving a certain SDE (Øksendal, 2003 ; Karatzas and Shreve, 2014 ) . For an appropriate choice of SDE and stopping time, u ⋆ ​ ( x ) u^{\star}(x) is given by an expectation over trajectories of X t X_{t} started at x x , with the functional involving source terms, potentials, and boundary data along the path. Approximating this expectation by simulating finitely many time-discretized trajectories from x x and averaging the corresponding FK functional yields a natural Monte Carlo estimator of u ⋆ ​ ( x ) u^{\star}(x) . 

Classical probabilistic numerical methods for PDEs exploit this idea by simulating many trajectories at spatial points of interest to approximate the solution field (Sabelfeld, 1991 ; Billaud-Friess et al. , 2018 ) . In contrast, we use FK only as a sparse source of supervision: we incorporate a small number of Monte Carlo FK estimates into the PINN loss as anchors, augmenting the standard residual and boundary terms with a penalty on discrepancies between u θ u_{\theta} and these estimates at a few interior points. We refer to the resulting model as a Feynman–Kac-Preconditioned Physics-Informed Neural Network (FK-PINN), and show that the added term acts as an operator preconditioner, contributing a mass-like component that improves conditioning and stabilizes gradient descent even for relatively small FK budgets. 

1.3 Contributions 

This paper makes the following contributions: 

1. 
Feynman–Kac-Preconditioned PINN objective. We introduce FK-PINNs, which augment the standard PINN loss with a data-fidelity term based on Monte Carlo FK estimates of the solution at a small set of interior domain points. The objective is mesh-free and acts as a simple drop-in replacement compatible with standard PINN architectures and training pipelines. 


2. 
Operator-preconditioning analysis. Building on the operator-conditioning framework of De Ryck et al. ( 2024 ) and Rathore et al. ( 2024 ) , we rigorously show in Theorem 5.4 that adding a suitable data-fidelity term to the standard PINN loss leads to a bounded and substantially improved condition number for the resulting loss. As a consequence, we deduce vastly improved convergence guarantees for gradient descent performed on this augmented objective. This regularizing effect is agnostic to the source of “supervised data” and is thus applicable to FK supervision as well as any other such method. 


3. 
Learning-theoretic error decomposition. We develop in Theorem 6.2 a learning-theoretic analysis of FK-PINNs trained by gradient descent. Under standard regularity assumptions, we derive high-probability L 2 L^{2} -error bounds between the true solution u ⋆ u^{\star} and an FK-PINN after T T steps of gradient descent. The bounds are non-asymptotic and depend explicitly on the problem parameters. 


4. 
Empirical illustrations on canonical PDE problems. We complement our theory with experiments on Poisson, Schrödinger, mean exit time, and committor problems. FK-PINNs exhibit improved training dynamics, better-conditioned operator spectra, lower solution error, and remain stable on instances where baseline PINNs suffer catastrophic optimization failures, all while using relatively small FK budgets. Detailed numerical results are reported in Appendix E . 


2 Background and Problem Setup 

2.1 PDE Setting and Function Spaces 

We consider linear second-order elliptic and parabolic PDEs on a bounded Lipschitz domain Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} with Dirichlet boundary conditions. Let ℒ \mathcal{L} be a linear second-order differential operator with bounded measurable coefficients, and consider 

ℒ ​ u ​ ( x ) = f ​ ( x ) , x ∈ Ω , \mathcal{L}u(x)=f(x),\quad x\in\Omega, (2.1) 

with boundary condition 

u ​ ( x ) = g ​ ( x ) , x ∈ ∂ Ω . u(x)=g(x),\quad x\in\partial\Omega. (2.2) 

We assume that ℒ \mathcal{L} is uniformly elliptic or parabolic in the usual sense, and that g g is sufficiently regular. Under these standard assumptions, there exists a unique weak solution u ⋆ ∈ H 1 ​ ( Ω ) u^{\star}\in H^{1}(\Omega) with u ⋆ | ∂ Ω = g u^{\star}|_{\partial\Omega}=g (Evans, 2022 ) . 

In later sections, we will restrict our attention to operators ℒ \mathcal{L} for which the solution u ⋆ u^{\star} admits a Feynman–Kac representation in terms of an associated stochastic differential equation (SDE) (Øksendal, 2003 ) . This class covers many important PDE models used in applications (E et al. , 2021 ) . We also note that our construction has natural analogues for broader classes of nonlinear PDEs that admit similar stochastic representations (E et al. , 2017 ) . We defer a more detailed discussion to Section 3 and Appendix A . 

2.2 Standard PINN Approximation of PDE Solutions 

Let { u θ : θ ∈ Θ } \{u_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\} be a parametric class of functions implemented by a fully connected neural network with depth L L , layer-wise widths ( m ℓ ) ℓ = 0 L (m_{\ell})_{\ell=0}^{L} and activation σ ≡ tanh \sigma\equiv\tanh , mapping x ∈ Ω ⊂ ℝ d x\in\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} to u θ ​ ( x ) ∈ ℝ u_{\theta}(x)\in\mathbb{R} . Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) approximate u ⋆ u^{\star} by minimizing a loss that penalizes PDE residuals in the interior and violations of boundary conditions (Raissi et al. , 2019 ) . 

Let { x i int } i = 1 N int ⊂ Ω \{x_{i}^{\mathrm{int}}\}_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{int}}}\subset\Omega be interior collocation points and { x j ∂ Ω } j = 1 N ∂ Ω ⊂ ∂ Ω \{x_{j}^{\partial\Omega}\}_{j=1}^{N_{\partial\Omega}}\subset\partial\Omega boundary collocation points, drawn i.i.d. from the uniform distribution on Ω \Omega and ∂ Ω \partial\Omega . We define the empirical PDE and boundary losses 

ℛ PDE ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta) := 1 N int ​ ∑ i = 1 N int | ℒ ​ u θ ​ ( x i int ) − f ​ ( x i int ) | 2 , \displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{int}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{int}}}\bigl|\mathcal{L}u_{\theta}(x_{i}^{\mathrm{int}})-f(x_{i}^{\mathrm{int}})\bigr|^{2}, (2.3) 

ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(\theta) := 1 N ∂ Ω ​ ∑ j = 1 N ∂ Ω | u θ ​ ( x j ∂ Ω ) − g ​ ( x j ∂ Ω ) | 2 . \displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N_{\partial\Omega}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\partial\Omega}}\bigl|u_{\theta}(x_{j}^{\partial\Omega})-g(x_{j}^{\partial\Omega})\bigr|^{2}. (2.4) 

The standard PINN objective is 

ℛ PINN ​ ( θ ) := ℛ PDE ​ ( θ ) + λ ∂ Ω ​ ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( θ ) , \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PINN}}(\theta):=\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)+\lambda_{\partial\Omega}\,\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(\theta), (2.5) 

where λ ∂ Ω > 0 \lambda_{\partial\Omega}>0 balances PDE residuals and boundary accuracy. In practice, ℒ PINN \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{PINN}} is minimized using (stochastic) gradient-based optimizers (e.g., Adam), with automatic differentiation to compute ℒ ​ u θ ​ ( x ) \mathcal{L}u_{\theta}(x) . 

2.3 Operator-Conditioning View of PINN Training 

Recent work analyzes PINN training through the conditioning of the local quadratic model induced by the underlying differential operator (Rathore et al. , 2024 ; De Ryck et al. , 2024 ) . We follow this viewpoint. Let H H denote a positive semidefinite curvature matrix for ℛ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PINN}} at a local minimizer θ ⋆ \theta^{\star} (for instance the empirical Gauss–Newton matrix). In a neighborhood of θ ⋆ \theta^{\star} , the loss is approximately quadratic and gradient descent is well approximated by a linear iteration governed by H H , so components along eigendirections with small eigenvalues decay very slowly, leading to the optimization pathologies observed in practice (Wang et al. , 2022 ; De Ryck et al. , 2024 ; Rathore et al. , 2024 ) . 

In this work, we augment the standard PINN objective with a (noisy) pointwise data-fidelity term. This adds an additional positive semidefinite curvature contribution M M , so that the resulting curvature becomes 

H FK := H + λ FK ​ M , H_{\mathrm{FK}}:=H+\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}M, (2.6) 

with λ FK > 0 \lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}>0 a weight. Building on the operator-conditioning analyses of Rathore et al. ( 2024 ); De Ryck et al. ( 2024 ) , we study in Section 5 how the spectrum and conditioning of H FK H_{\mathrm{FK}} compare to those of H H . 

2.4 Related Work 
Mitigating the failure modes of PINNs. 
Ever since their introduction by Raissi et al. ( 2019 ) , a growing body of work documents a number of severe training difficulties when training PINNs, including spectral bias, failure to minimize residuals, and sensitivity to sampling and loss weights (Krishnapriyan et al. , 2021 ; Cuomo et al. , 2022 ) . Finding methods to mitigate these issues is an active field of research, and proposed methods include adaptive collocation, residual-based reweighting, domain decomposition, and specialized architectures (Wang et al. , 2021 ; Wu et al. , 2023 ; Wang et al. , 2024 ) . While the augmentation of the PINN loss with supervised data has been shown empirically to help mitigate these failure modes (Heldmann et al. , 2023 ; Bensoussan et al. , 2023 ) , the theoretical underpinnings of this phenomenon have remained largely unexplored. 
Operator- and loss-landscape-based analyses. 
A complementary line of work analyzes PINN training through the operators induced by the PDE and the loss, relating gradient descent dynamics and convergence rates to the spectrum and conditioning of ℒ ∗ ​ ℒ \mathcal{L}^{\ast}\mathcal{L} or NTK-like operators (Wang et al. , 2022 ; De Ryck et al. , 2024 ) . Recent such studies (Rathore et al. , 2024 ; Chen et al. , 2024 ; Wang et al. , 2026 ) further show how ill-conditioned differential operators translate into ill-conditioned PINN objectives and motivate preconditioned or second-order optimization schemes. Our work adopts this operator-centric viewpoint but improves conditioning by modifying the PINN objective itself. 
Feynman-Kac and Monte Carlo methods for PDEs. 
Classical probabilistic numerical methods exploit Feynman–Kac representations to compute PDE solutions via Monte Carlo simulation of associated SDEs (Sabelfeld, 1991 ; Billaud-Friess et al. , 2018 ; Yu and Mascagni, 2022 ) . Recent works have combined these ideas with the expressive power of neural networks, resulting in FK-based neural PDE solvers (Han et al. , 2020 ; Beck et al. , 2021 ; Han et al. , 2026 ) . All of these approaches aim at directly computing pointwise estimates of u ⋆ ​ ( x ) u^{\star}(x) , with controllable bias and variance. This differs to the way we use the Feynman–Kac Monte Carlo data only as a supervision term for PINNs, and analyze its effect on both the conditioning of the loss landscape and the resulting generalization error. 

3 Feynman–Kac Representation and Monte Carlo Supervision 

3.1 Feynman–Kac Representation for Linear PDEs 

We briefly recall the celebrated Feynman–Kac (FK) representation at the core of our Monte Carlo supervision. Consider the boundary value problem ( 2.1 )–( 2.2 ) for the operator ℒ \mathcal{L} introduced in Section 2 . Under conditions ensuring that ( 2.1 )–( 2.2 ) admits a Feynman–Kac representation (Øksendal, 2003 ; Karatzas and Shreve, 2014 ) , there exists a diffusion process ( X t ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t})_{t\geq 0} with generator ℒ \mathcal{L} and exit time τ := inf { t ≥ 0 : X t ∉ Ω } , \tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\notin\Omega\}, such that for every x ∈ Ω x\in\Omega , 

u ⋆ ​ ( x ) = 𝔼 x ​ [ ∫ 0 τ r ​ ( X t ) ​ 𝑑 t + h ​ ( X τ ) ] , u^{\star}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\!\bigg[\int_{0}^{\tau}r(X_{t})\,dt+h\bigl(X_{\tau}\bigr)\bigg], (3.1) 

where r r is a running functional and h h is a terminal functional determined by the coefficients of ℒ \mathcal{L} and the data ( f , g ) (f,g) . Explicit expressions for r r and h h , together with analogous stochastic representation formulas for more general PDE families are detailed in Appendix A . 

This probabilistic representation is classical in the theory of diffusion processes and underlies many probabilistic numerical methods for elliptic and parabolic PDEs (Pavliotis, 2014 ) . It naturally covers, for example, mean exit time problems and committor functions describing transition probabilities between metastable sets in stochastic dynamics (E and Vanden-Eijnden, 2006 ) . 

3.2 Monte Carlo Estimator of the FK Functional 

To obtain pointwise supervision values for the PINN, we approximate the FK functional in ( 3.1 ) by Monte Carlo (MC) simulation of the associated diffusion. Starting from a point x ∈ Ω x\in\Omega , we simulate N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} independent trajectories with a time step Δ ​ t > 0 \Delta t>0 , using a standard Euler–Maruyama scheme (Gobet, 2016 ) , and stop each trajectory when it exits Ω \Omega or when a truncation time T max > 0 T_{\max}>0 is reached. 

Let X n ( m ) X^{(m)}_{n} denote the n n -th point along the m m -th simulated trajectory, and let τ ^ ( m ) \hat{\tau}^{(m)} be the corresponding (discrete) stopping index. Our Monte Carlo approximation of u ⋆ ​ ( x ) u^{\star}(x) is 

u ^ MC ​ ( x ) = 1 N MC ​ ∑ m = 1 N MC ( ∑ n = 0 τ ^ ( m ) − 1 r ​ ( X n ( m ) ) ​ Δ ​ t + h ​ ( X τ ^ ( m ) ( m ) ) ) . \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x)=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\hat{\tau}^{(m)}-1}r\bigl(X^{(m)}_{n}\bigr)\,\Delta t+h\bigl(X^{(m)}_{\hat{\tau}^{(m)}}\bigr)\bigg). (3.2) 

In words, along each trajectory we approximate the FK integral by a simple time Riemann sum of r r , add the terminal term h h at the (possibly truncated) exit point, and then average across all trajectories. Algorithm 1 summarizes the simulation procedure. 

Under mild conditions on the diffusion and on ( Δ ​ t , T max ) (\Delta t,T_{\max}) , the estimator u ^ MC ​ ( x ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x) has variance Var ⁡ [ u ^ MC ​ ( x ) ] = 𝒪 ​ ( 1 / N MC ) \operatorname{Var}[\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x)]=\mathcal{O}(1/N_{\mathrm{MC}}) and a bias controlled by Δ ​ t \Delta t and T max T_{\max} . Precise bias–variance bounds and tail estimates for u ^ MC ​ ( x ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x) are proved in Appendix C (see in particular Propositions C.11 and 6.1 ). 

Algorithm 1 Monte Carlo Feynman–Kac estimator at a point x x 
0: Point x ∈ Ω x\in\Omega ; number of trajectories N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} ; time step Δ ​ t \Delta t ; truncation time T max T_{\max} ; drift b b and diffusion σ \sigma ; running and terminal functionals r , h r,h . 1: for m = 1 , … , N MC m=1,\dots,N_{\mathrm{MC}} do 2: Initialize X 0 ( m ) ← x X^{(m)}_{0}\leftarrow x , S ( m ) ← 0 S^{(m)}\leftarrow 0 , n ← 0 n\leftarrow 0 . 3: while X n ( m ) ∈ Ω X^{(m)}_{n}\in\Omega and n ​ Δ ​ t < T max n\Delta t<T_{\max} do 4: Sample Δ ​ W n ( m ) ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , Δ ​ t ​ 𝑰 ) \Delta W^{(m)}_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Delta t\,\bm{I}) . 5: Update X n + 1 ( m ) ← X n ( m ) + b ​ ( X n ( m ) ) ​ Δ ​ t + σ ​ ( X n ( m ) ) ​ Δ ​ W n ( m ) X^{(m)}_{n+1}\leftarrow X^{(m)}_{n}+b\bigl(X^{(m)}_{n}\bigr)\,\Delta t+\sigma\bigl(X^{(m)}_{n}\bigr)\,\Delta W^{(m)}_{n} . 6: Update S ( m ) ← S ( m ) + r ​ ( X n ( m ) ) ​ Δ ​ t S^{(m)}\leftarrow S^{(m)}+r\bigl(X^{(m)}_{n}\bigr)\,\Delta t . 7: Increment n ← n + 1 n\leftarrow n+1 . 8: end while 9: Set τ ^ ( m ) ← n \hat{\tau}^{(m)}\leftarrow n and update S ( m ) ← S ( m ) + h ​ ( X τ ^ ( m ) ( m ) ) S^{(m)}\leftarrow S^{(m)}+h\bigl(X^{(m)}_{\hat{\tau}^{(m)}}\bigr) . 10: end for 11: return u ^ MC ​ ( x ) ← 1 N MC ​ ∑ m = 1 N MC S ( m ) \displaystyle\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x)\leftarrow\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}S^{(m)} . 
3.3 Selection of Supervision Points and Budget 

We evaluate ( 3.2 ) at a relatively small set of Feynman–Kac supervision points { x k FK } k = 1 N FK ⊂ Ω \{x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}\}_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\subset\Omega . These points can be selected by simple generic strategies, such as uniform sampling over Ω \Omega , low-discrepancy sequences, or an adaptive second pass that concentrates points in regions where an initial PINN exhibits large residuals. 

Given a fixed computational budget, there is a natural trade-off between the number of supervision points N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} and the number of trajectories per point N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} : increasing N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} improves spatial coverage, whereas increasing N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} reduces the Monte Carlo variance at each point. Concrete parameter choices and additional implementation details for our numerical experiments are deferred to Appendix E . 

4 Feynman–Kac-Preconditioned PINNs (FK-PINNs) 

We now formulate explicitly how supervised data obtained from Algorithm 1 is used to train PINNs in our framework. Naturally, one could apply this same methodology with supervised data coming from any other source. Indeed, as we will see in Section 5 below, the improved conditioning due to supervised data is agnostic to the data source. 

4.1 Augmented Loss with FK Supervision 

Given FK supervision points { x k FK } k = 1 N FK ⊂ Ω \{x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}\}_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\subset\Omega and corresponding Monte Carlo estimates u ^ Γ MC ​ ( x k FK ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}_{\Gamma}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}) of the Feynman–Kac functional ( 3.1 ), we define the FK supervision loss 

ℛ FK ​ ( θ ) = 1 N FK ​ ∑ k = 1 N FK ( u θ ​ ( x k FK ) − u ^ Γ MC ​ ( x k FK ) ) 2 . \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(\theta)=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\bigl(u_{\theta}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})-\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}_{\Gamma}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})\bigr)^{2}. (4.1) 

Here Γ \Gamma collects the Monte Carlo parameters (number of trajectories, time step, truncation time, etc.), as described in Algorithm 1 . 

The full FK-PINN objective augments the standard PINN loss with ( 4.1 ): 

ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN ​ ( θ , ϕ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}}(\theta,\phi) = λ PDE ​ ( ϕ ) ​ ℛ PDE ​ ( θ ) + λ ∂ Ω ​ ( ϕ ) ​ ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle=\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\phi)\,\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)+\lambda_{\partial\Omega}(\phi)\,\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(\theta) (4.2) 

+ λ FK ​ ( ϕ ) ​ ℛ FK ​ ( θ ) , \displaystyle\qquad+\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}(\phi)\,\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(\theta), 

where ℛ PDE \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}} and ℛ ∂ Ω \mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega} are the empirical PDE residual and boundary losses defined in Section 2, and ϕ \phi denotes the task-weighting parameters introduced below. When λ FK ​ ( ϕ ) = 0 \lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}(\phi)=0 and the remaining weights are fixed, ( 4.2 ) reduces to the standard PINN objective. 

4.2 Adaptive Weighting of Loss Components 

The objective ( 4.2 ) couples three losses with different noise levels: ℛ PDE \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}} , ℛ ∂ Ω \mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega} , and ℛ FK \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}} . We adopt the uncertainty-based weighting scheme of Kendall et al. ( 2018 ) , in the same form used for PINNs by Niu et al. ( 2025 ) . In their formulation, for task losses { ℛ j } \{\mathcal{R}_{j}\} with respective variances { σ j 2 } \{\sigma_{j}^{2}\} , the joint objective is 

ℛ multi = ∑ j ( 1 2 ​ σ j 2 ​ ℛ j + log ⁡ σ j ) . \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{multi}}=\sum_{j}\biggl(\frac{1}{2\sigma_{j}^{2}}\,\mathcal{R}_{j}+\log\sigma_{j}\biggr). (4.3) 

Motivated by this, for our three tasks j ∈ { PDE , ∂ Ω , FK } j\in\{\mathrm{PDE},\,\partial\Omega,\,\mathrm{FK}\} we introduce log-variances s j := log ⁡ σ j 2 s_{j}:=\log\sigma_{j}^{2} and define trainable weights λ j ​ ( ϕ ) := exp ⁡ ( − s j ) \lambda_{j}(\phi):=\exp(-s_{j}) with ϕ := { s j } j \phi:=\{s_{j}\}_{j} . The three scalars in ( 4.2 ), λ PDE ​ ( ϕ ) \lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\phi) , λ ∂ Ω ​ ( ϕ ) \lambda_{\partial\Omega}(\phi) , and λ FK ​ ( ϕ ) \lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}(\phi) , are obtained in this way and are optimized jointly with θ \theta . In practice, we initialize the s j s_{j} so that the initial weights are of comparable magnitude and clip s j s_{j} to a bounded interval to avoid degenerate solutions in which one loss (especially the noisy FK term) is effectively ignored. 

4.3 Training Algorithm 

FK-PINN training consists of an offline stage that generates FK supervision and an online stage that trains the network with the augmented loss ( 4.2 ). The full procedure is summarized in Algorithm 2 . 

Algorithm 2 Training a Feynman–Kac-Preconditioned PINN (FK-PINN) 
0: Domain Ω \Omega and boundary ∂ Ω \partial\Omega ; PDE operator ℒ \mathcal{L} and data ( f , g ) (f,g) ; neural network u θ u_{\theta} ; task-weight parameters ϕ \phi ; FK Monte Carlo parameters Γ \Gamma ; number of FK points N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} ; numbers of interior and boundary collocation points. 1: Stage 1: Offline FK supervision generation 2: Choose FK supervision points { x k FK } k = 1 N FK ⊂ Ω \{x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}\}_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\subset\Omega (e.g., uniform or low-discrepancy sampling). 3: for k = 1 , … , N FK k=1,\dots,N_{\mathrm{FK}} do 4: Approximate u ⋆ ​ ( x k FK ) u^{\star}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}) by a Monte Carlo FK estimate u ^ Γ MC ​ ( x k FK ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}_{\Gamma}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}) using Algorithm 1 with parameters Γ \Gamma . 5: end for 6: Form the FK dataset 𝒟 FK = { ( x k FK , u ^ Γ MC ​ ( x k FK ) ) } k = 1 N FK \mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{FK}}=\{(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}},\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}_{\Gamma}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}))\}_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}} . 7: Stage 2: PINN training with FK supervision 8: while stopping criterion not met do 9: Sample interior collocation points in Ω \Omega and boundary points on ∂ Ω \partial\Omega . 10: Compute ℛ PDE ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta) and ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(\theta) from the current collocation batches. 11: Sample a minibatch from 𝒟 FK \mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{FK}} and compute ℛ FK ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(\theta) via ( 4.1 ). 12: Evaluate the total loss ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN ​ ( θ , ϕ ) \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}}(\theta,\phi) in ( 4.2 ). 13: Update ( θ , ϕ ) (\theta,\phi) using (stochastic) gradient descent. 14: end while 15: return trained parameters ( θ , ϕ ) (\theta,\phi) . 
5 Theoretical guarantees for supervision-based operator preconditioning 

We now give an overview of our theoretical framework for supervision-based operator preconditioning. As we will see, the regularizing effect of supervised data on the loss landscape is completely agnostic to the source of such data, and our results are thus stated for generic data terms. Our presentation follows the PL ∗ framework of Liu et al. ( 2022 ) and its adaptation to finite-width PINNs by Rathore et al. ( 2024 ) . Full assumptions and proofs are deferred to Appendix B . 

5.1 Setup and PL ∗ framework 

We analyze gradient descent on the PINN loss augmented with a squared supervision term of the form ( 4.1 ), with fixed task weights, where the supervision targets may come from the Feynman–Kac construction or from any other data-labeling procedure. Throughout this section we fix constants λ PDE > 0 \lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}>0 , λ ∂ Ω > 0 \lambda_{\partial\Omega}>0 , and λ FK ≥ 0 \lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}\geq 0 , and we consider the objectives 

ℛ PINN ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PINN}}(\theta) := λ PDE ​ ℛ PDE ​ ( θ ) + λ ∂ Ω ​ ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( θ ) , \displaystyle=\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}\,\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)+\lambda_{\partial\Omega}\,\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(\theta), 

ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}}(\theta) := ℛ PINN ​ ( θ ) + λ FK ​ ℛ FK ​ ( θ ) , \displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PINN}}(\theta)+\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}\,\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(\theta), 

where ℛ PDE \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}} and ℛ ∂ Ω \mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega} are defined in Section 2.2 , and, slightly abusing notation, ℛ FK \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}} is a data supervision loss of the form ( 4.1 ), but with supervised data coming from any source. The standard PINN corresponds to λ FK = 0 \lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}=0 . 

To carry out our analysis, we will assume that the following holds: 
Assumption 5.1 (Interpolation regime) . 
Both ℛ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PINN}} and ℛ FK \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}} are optimized in an interpolation regime: each loss has minimum value 0 , and every global minimizer attains zero loss. 

Assumption 5.1 is an expressivity assumption on the hypothesis class. It requires that each empirical loss can attain the value zero for at least one choice of network parameters, meaning that the training constraints are realizable. This is the standard interpolation setting used in (Liu et al. , 2022 ; Rathore et al. , 2024 ) , and has been shown to hold even for moderately over-parametrized networks (Vardi et al. , 2022 ; Madden, 2024 ) . 
Definition 5.1 (PL ∗ condition (Karimi et al. , 2016 ) ) . 
Let 𝒥 \mathcal{J} be a loss function and let θ ⋆ \theta^{\star} be any global minimizer of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} . We say that 𝒥 \mathcal{J} satisfies the PL ∗ condition in a neighborhood 𝒮 \mathcal{S} of θ ⋆ \theta^{\star} with constant μ > 0 \mu>0 if 

𝒥 ​ ( θ ) − 𝒥 ​ ( θ ⋆ ) ≤ 1 2 ​ μ ​ ‖ ∇ 𝒥 ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 for all θ ∈ 𝒮 . \mathcal{J}(\theta)-\mathcal{J}(\theta^{\star})\;\leq\;\frac{1}{2\mu}\,\|\nabla\mathcal{J}(\theta)\|^{2}\quad\text{for all $\theta\in\mathcal{S}$.} 

This is the Polyak–Łojasiewicz (PL) inequality (Polyak, 1963 ) , adapted to possibly non-isolated minimizers as in Karimi et al. ( 2016 ); Liu et al. ( 2022 ) . Under Assumption 5.1 we have 𝒥 ​ ( θ ⋆ ) = 0 \mathcal{J}(\theta^{\star})=0 , so the PL ∗ condition directly controls the loss in terms of the gradient norm. 
Definition 5.2 (Condition number for PL ∗ functions) . 
Let 𝒥 \mathcal{J} be differentiable and L L -smooth 1 1 1 That is, ∇ 𝒥 \nabla\mathcal{J} is L L -Lipschitz on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , meaning that ‖ ∇ 𝒥 ​ ( θ ) − ∇ 𝒥 ​ ( θ ′ ) ‖ ≤ L ​ ‖ θ − θ ′ ‖ \|\nabla\mathcal{J}(\theta)-\nabla\mathcal{J}(\theta^{\prime})\|\leq L\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\| holds for all θ , θ ′ ∈ 𝒮 \theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S} . on a set 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , and assume that 𝒥 \mathcal{J} satisfies a PL ∗ condition on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} with constant μ > 0 \mu>0 . We define the PL-based condition number of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} as 

κ PL ​ ( 𝒥 ; 𝒮 ) := L μ . \kappa_{\mathrm{PL}}(\mathcal{J};\mathcal{S}):=\frac{L}{\mu}. 

For the two objectives of interest, we fix a neighborhood 𝒮 \mathcal{S} of the interpolation manifold and write κ PINN := κ PL ​ ( ℛ PINN ; 𝒮 ) \kappa_{\mathrm{PINN}}:=\kappa_{\mathrm{PL}}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PINN}};\mathcal{S}) and κ FK := κ PL ​ ( ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN ; 𝒮 ) \kappa_{\mathrm{FK}}:=\kappa_{\mathrm{PL}}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}};\mathcal{S}) , omitting the dependence on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} when clear from context. 

For the losses considered here, the constants L L and the PL ∗ constant μ \mu are controlled by the largest and smallest nonzero eigenvalues of the linearized Gauss–Newton matrix, so κ PL \kappa_{\mathrm{PL}} is comparable to the usual spectral condition number. As in Liu et al. ( 2022 ); Rathore et al. ( 2024 ) , we assume that the empirical PINN objectives ℛ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PINN}} and ℛ FK \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}} are L L -smooth and satisfy a PL ∗ condition on a neighborhood 𝒮 \mathcal{S} of the interpolation manifold. 
Theorem 5.3 ( Rathore et al. ( 2024 ) , Theorem 8.4) . 
Consider the standard PINN loss ℛ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PINN}} for a linear PDE and an interpolating minimizer θ ⋆ \theta^{\star} as in Assumption 5.1 . Let 𝒯 K ​ ( θ ⋆ ) \mathcal{T}_{K}(\theta^{\star}) be the associated kernel integral operator with eigenvalues ( λ j ) j ≥ 1 (\lambda_{j})_{j\geq 1} satisfying λ j ≤ C ​ j − β \lambda_{j}\leq Cj^{-\beta} for some C > 0 C>0 and β > 1 \beta>1 . Let κ PINN \kappa_{\mathrm{PINN}} denote the PL-based condition number of Definition 5.2 on a PL ∗ neighborhood of θ ⋆ \theta^{\star} . Then there exists a constant c > 0 c>0 such that, with high probability for all sufficiently large number N N of collocation points, 

κ PINN ≥ c ​ N β / 2 . \kappa_{\mathrm{PINN}}\;\geq\;c\,N^{\beta/2}. 

Thus, in the PDE-only case the PL-based condition number of the PINN loss deteriorates polynomially as the residual (collocation) mesh is refined. 

5.2 Effect of supervision on conditioning 

Augmenting the loss with a data term adds curvature in parameter space, which can prevent the conditioning from deteriorating as the residual mesh is refined. The following theorem makes this effect precise by controlling the local PL ∗ and smoothness constants of the data-augmented objective. Before stating our result, we also require the following compatibility condition ensuring that the supervised data term is not “blind” to parameter directions that are relevant for satisfying the PDE constraints. 
Assumption 5.2 . 
Consider small parameter perturbations around the parameters of interest. Split such perturbations into those that change the supervised loss term and those that do not. We assume that the physics part of the objective remains well conditioned on the second class of perturbations, and that the interaction between the two classes is not too large. 

A precise formulation is given in Section B as Assumption B.1 . We can now formulate our main result of this section: 
Theorem 5.4 (Informal) . 
Assume the setting of Theorem 5.3 , the regularity assumptions on the supervision term ℛ FK \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}} in ( 4.1 ) stated in Section B , and the compatibility condition of Assumption B.1 . Then there exist constants μ 0 , L 0 , C > 0 \mu_{0},L_{0},C>0 , which may depend on the task weights and on the supervised data distribution but are independent of the number N N of PDE collocation points, such that with high probability, the data-augmented objective ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}} is L 0 L_{0} -smooth and satisfies a PL ∗ condition with constant μ 0 \mu_{0} on a neighborhood 𝒮 \mathcal{S} of the interpolation manifold. In particular, 

κ FK = κ PL ​ ( ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN ; 𝒮 ) ≤ C \kappa_{\mathrm{FK}}\;=\;\kappa_{\mathrm{PL}}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}};\mathcal{S})\;\leq\;C 

uniformly in N N . 

A detailed statement and proof are given in Section B (see in particular Theorem B.9 ). 

Combining Theorems 5.3 and 5.4 yields a sharp contrast between the two objectives: while for the standard PINN loss, the PL-based condition number κ PINN \kappa_{\mathrm{PINN}} deteriorates polynomially with the number of collocation points , the data-augmented loss’ condition number κ FK \kappa_{\mathrm{FK}} remains uniformly bounded as the mesh is refined, provided the supervised data points satisfy the compatibility requirements stated in Assumption 5.2 . This explains why the data-supervision term can effectively enable PINNs to overcome training pathologies due to large collocation sample size. 
Implications for gradient descent. 
Under the PL ∗ framework, Liu et al. ( 2022 ) show that gradient descent on an L L -smooth loss 𝒥 \mathcal{J} satisfying a PL ∗ condition with constant μ \mu converges linearly to a global minimizer, with iteration complexity 

T 𝒥 ​ ( ε ) = 𝒪 ​ ( κ PL ​ ( 𝒥 ; 𝒮 ) ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ) , κ PL ​ ( 𝒥 ; 𝒮 ) = L ​ ( 𝒥 ; 𝒮 ) μ , T_{\mathcal{J}}(\varepsilon)=\mathcal{O}\bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm{PL}}(\mathcal{J};\mathcal{S})\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr),\,\kappa_{\mathrm{PL}}(\mathcal{J};\mathcal{S})=\frac{L(\mathcal{J};\mathcal{S})}{\mu}, 

where L ​ ( 𝒥 ; 𝒮 ) L(\mathcal{J};\mathcal{S}) is the Lipschitz constant of ∇ 𝒥 \nabla\mathcal{J} on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} . For the standard PINN loss this gives T PINN ​ ( ε ) = 𝒪 ​ ( κ PINN ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ) T_{\mathrm{PINN}}(\varepsilon)=\mathcal{O}\bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm{PINN}}\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr) , and Theorem 5.3 suggests that T PINN ​ ( ε ) T_{\mathrm{PINN}}(\varepsilon) grows like N β / 2 ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) N^{\beta/2}\log(1/\varepsilon) as N N increases. In contrast, Theorem 5.4 shows that κ FK \kappa_{\mathrm{FK}} stays bounded in N N , so T FK ​ ( ε ) = 𝒪 ​ ( log ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ) T_{\mathrm{FK}}(\varepsilon)=\mathcal{O}\bigl(\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr) , i.e., reaching error ε \varepsilon costs about a factor N β / 2 N^{\beta/2} more iterations for the standard PINN than for the data-enhanced PINN. 

6 Error Analysis of FK-PINNs 

6.1 Setting and regime 

We complement the operator-level PL ∗ analysis from Section 5 with learning-theoretic error bounds for FK-PINNs. The analysis, which relies in part on explicit control of the error and noise induced by the Monte Carlo approximation scheme, is specific to FK-PINNs, and an extension to other supervised data generation processes would require a separate case-specific study. As per Section 2 , we consider the hypothesis class ℱ \mathcal{F} of fully connected networks u 𝜽 u_{\bm{\theta}} with depth L L , layer-wise widths ( m ℓ ) ℓ = 0 L (m_{\ell})_{\ell=0}^{L} and tanh \tanh activation in all layers except for a linear output neuron. As described in Algorithm 2 , the neural networks in ℱ \mathcal{F} are trained to minimize the loss ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}} ( 4.1 ), which serves as an empirical risk. 

We sample N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} FK points x i FK x_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}} , N int N_{\mathrm{int}} interior points x i int x_{i}^{\mathrm{int}} , and N ∂ Ω N_{\partial\Omega} boundary points x i ∂ Ω x_{i}^{\partial\Omega} independently and uniformly on their respective domains. At each FK point x i FK x_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}} , a Monte Carlo approximation of the FK functional provides a supervision label u ^ MC ​ ( x i FK ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}) , as described in Algorithm 1 . Under the assumptions collected in Appendix D , we establish non-asymptotic L 2 ​ ( Ω ) L^{2}(\Omega) error bounds for trained FK-PINNs via an approximation–estimation–optimization decomposition. To our knowledge, these are the first non-asymptotic learning-error guarantees for PINNs with tanh \tanh activation—a widely used choice in practice—contrasting with, e.g., earlier asymptotic convergence results in similar settings (Doumèche et al. , 2025 ) , data-independent a posteriori error bounds (Hillebrecht and Unger, 2023 ) , or non-asymptotic results restricted to piecewise polynomial activations (Jiao et al. , 2022 ; Lu et al. , 2022 ; Lei et al. , 2025 ) . 

6.2 Non-asymptotic error bound 

Our first result establishes that, with the Monte Carlo estimator described by Algorithm 1 , each fidelity label in ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}} is a pointwise approximation of u ⋆ u^{\star} with a deterministic bias of order Δ ​ t \sqrt{\Delta t} plus an exponentially small term in T max T_{\max} and with sub-exponential Monte Carlo noise. 
Proposition 6.1 (Sub-exponential FK noise) . 
Suppose that the FK data in ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}} is generated by simulating the diffusion with the Euler–Maruyama scheme described in Algorithm 1 , with time step 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} , truncation horizon T max ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ] T_{\max}\in(0,\infty] and a fixed number of Monte Carlo paths N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} per point, where Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 is a constant depending only on the problem data (as in Appendix C ). Then each FK datum admits a decomposition 

Y i FK = u ⋆ ​ ( X i FK ) + b ​ ( X i FK ) + ζ i , Y_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}=u^{\star}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}})+b(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}})+\zeta_{i}, 

where { ( X i FK , Y i FK ) } i = 1 N FK \{(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}},Y_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}})\}_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}} are i.i.d., the bias satisfies 

| b ​ ( x ) | ≤ C bias ​ Δ ​ t + C T ​ e − κ ​ T max for all ​ x ∈ Ω , \lvert b(x)\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t}+C_{T}e^{-\kappa T_{\max}}\quad\text{for all }x\in\Omega, 

for constants C bias , C T , κ > 0 C_{\mathrm{bias}},C_{T},\kappa>0 independent of Δ ​ t \Delta t , N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} and N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} , with the convention e − κ ​ T max = 0 e^{-\kappa T_{\max}}=0 when T max = ∞ T_{\max}=\infty , and, conditional on X i FK X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}} , the fluctuation ζ i \zeta_{i} is mean-zero and sub-exponential with parameters that do not depend on N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} or N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} . A proof is given in Appendix C . 

We can now state our main learning-theoretic estimates. 
Theorem 6.2 (Error analysis for depth- 3 3 FK-PINNs) . 
Assume the setting of Section 6 and the PDE, regularity and PL ∗ conditions of Appendix D . Let s ≥ 3 s\geq 3 and suppose that u ⋆ ∈ W s , ∞ ​ ( Ω ) u^{\star}\in W^{s,\infty}(\Omega) and set β := ( s − 2 ) / ( 2 ​ d + 4 ​ ( s − 2 ) ) \beta:=(s-2)/(2d+4(s-2)) . Consider FK-PINNs with depth L = 3 L=3 and width m m , trained, according to Algorithm 2 , by T T steps of gradient descent on ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}} with sufficiently small step size. Denote by ε bias := C bias ​ Δ ​ t + C T ​ e − κ ​ T max \varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}:=C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t}+C_{T}e^{-\kappa T_{\mathrm{max}}} the bias term from Proposition 6.1 . Lastly, suppose that the max layer width m ≍ N FK d / ( 4 ​ d + 8 ​ ( s − 2 ) ) m\asymp N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{d/(4d+8(s-2))} , and that N int , N ∂ Ω ≳ N FK N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega}\gtrsim N_{\mathrm{FK}} . Then, for any δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) , there exist constants C , c opt > 0 C,c_{\mathrm{opt}}>0 , independent of ( N int , N ∂ Ω , N FK , T ) (N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega},N_{\mathrm{FK}},T) , such that with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , 

∥ u 𝜽 T − u ⋆ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) ≤ C ​ ( N FK − β + e − c opt ​ T + ε bias + ε bias 1 / 2 ) , \lVert u_{{\bm{\theta}}_{T}}-u^{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\bigl(N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-\beta}\!+e^{-c_{\mathrm{opt}}T}\!+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}^{1/2}\bigr), (6.1) 

up to logarithmic factors in N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} and in the sample sizes. 
Corollary 6.3 (Rate for the FK-PINN algorithm) . 
In the setting of Theorem 6.2 , define ε bias \varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}} and choose the width m m as in the theorem, and run gradient descent for 

T ≳ log ⁡ N FK T\gtrsim\log N_{\mathrm{FK}} 

iterations. Let u ^ \hat{u} denote the resulting depth- 3 3 FK-PINN. Then, up to logarithmic factors in N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} and in the sample sizes, there exists a constant C > 0 C>0 , independent of N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} , such that with probability at least 1 − N FK − 10 1-N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-10} , 

∥ u ^ − u ⋆ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) ≤ C ( N FK − β + ε bias + ε bias 1 / 2 ) , \lVert\hat{u}-u^{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\,\bigr(N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-\beta}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}^{1/2}\bigr), (6.2) 

where β := ( s − 2 ) / ( 2 ​ d + 4 ​ ( s − 2 ) ) \beta:=(s-2)/(2d+4(s-2)) . See Appendix D , Corollary D.25 for proofs. 

This result shows that, when N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} grows and the width is tuned as in Theorem 6.2 , FK-PINNs achieve the expected rate in terms of N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} , in line with the convergence theory of PINNs with dense physics sampling (Doumèche et al. , 2025 ) . In contrast, in the genuinely sparse FK regime where N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} is fixed while N int N_{\mathrm{int}} and N ∂ Ω N_{\partial\Omega} increase, the error bound is saturated by the FK term and cannot vanish uniformly, which quantifies the statistical “cost” paid for the improved conditioning brought by FK supervision. 

7 Numerical experiments 

7.1 An Illustrative Example 

We now illustrate our theoretical results by comparing the FK-PINNs against the standard PINN baseline on a representative PDE: a steady-state Schrödinger-type equation. Further extensive numerical experiments on a wider range of problems, together with implementation details are provided in Appendix E . 

For a stationary system defined on a bounded domain Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} , we consider the following non-homogeneous Schrödinger-type equation: 

− 1 2 ​ Δ ​ ψ ​ ( x ) + V ​ ( x ) ​ ψ ​ ( x ) = f ​ ( x ) \displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Delta\psi\left(x\right)+V\left(x\right)\psi\left(\begin{gathered}x\end{gathered}\right)=f\left(x\right) x ∈ Ω , \displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ x\in\Omega, (7.1) 

ψ ​ ( x ) = 0 \displaystyle\psi\left(x\right)=0 x ∈ ∂ Ω , \displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ x\in\partial\Omega, 

where ψ ​ ( x ) \psi(x) represents the response wavefunction, V ​ ( x ) V(x) denotes a prescribed potential function, and f ​ ( x ) f(x) constitutes a given source term. The multi-scale nature of this Schrödinger-type equation, notably for oscillatory, high-frequency potentials, poses a significant challenge for conventional PINNs. 

We set the domain Ω \Omega as Ω = [ − 1 , 1 ] × [ − 1 , 1 ] \Omega=[-1,1]\times[-1,1] , while the potential V ​ ( x ) V(x) is that of a periodic crystalline lattice, defined as 

V ​ ( x ) = − V 0 ​ [ cos ⁡ ( k ​ x 1 ) + cos ⁡ ( k ​ x 2 ) ] , V(x)=-V_{0}[\cos(kx_{1})+\cos(kx_{2})], (7.2) 

where V 0 = 5 V_{0}=5 is the potential depth and k = 2 ​ π / a k=2\pi/a with lattice constant a = 0.5 a=0.5 . This periodic structure induces strong local confinement and sharp gradients in the wavefunction ψ ​ ( x ) \psi(x) . W further introduce a high-frequency external forcing term: 

f ​ ( x ) = A ​ sin ⁡ ( K ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( K ​ x 2 ) , f(x)=A\sin(Kx_{1})\sin(Kx_{2}), (7.3) 

where A = 50 A=50 and K = 5 K=5 . 

Figure 1 illustrates the potential V V , forcing term f f and corresponding wavefunction ψ \psi . We report respectively in Figures 2 and 3 the wavefunctions learned by a standard PINN, and an FK-PINN, with both identical architecture training parameters and schedule, after 30000 + 15000 30000+15000 steps of Adam + L-BFGS. As the results clearly illustrate, while the standard PINN completely fails at learning the solution structure, due to the high-frequency oscillations making the loss landscape intractable, the proposed FK-PINNs effectively mitigates the underlying stiffness of the PDE and succesfully recovers the intended solution profile. 

Figure 1 : Data for the Schrödinger-type equation ( 7.1 ). From left to right: potential V ​ ( 𝐱 ) V(\mathbf{x}) , source f ​ ( 𝐱 ) f(\mathbf{x}) , and FEM reference ψ \psi . 

Figure 2 : Numerical results for the standard PINN on the Schrödinger-type equation ( 7.1 ). Left side: PINN prediction Right side: absolute errors | ψ − u θ | |\psi-u_{\theta}| 

Figure 3 : Numerical results for the FK-PINN on the Schrödinger-type equation ( 7.1 ). Left side: FK-PINN prediction Right side: absolute errors | ψ − u θ | |\psi-u_{\theta}|

We now showcase the performance of FK-PINNs when compared to standard PINNs on a number of canonical PDEs. As we can see, the ability of this supervised approach to overcome the failure modes of PINNs is clear. We refer the reader to Appendix E for further experimental details and background on the PDEs under consideration.

8 Conclusion 

In this work, we’ve established that enriching the standard PINN loss with pointwise approximate solution data can provably regularize the loss landscape and enhance the convergence of gradient descent. Furthermore, we’ve proposed an easily implementable and flexible algorithm based on a Monte Carlo approximation of the Feynman–Kac representation of certain elliptic and parabolic PDEs to compute such approximate solution data, resulting in what we’ve referred to as FK-PINNs. Using a learning-theoretic framework together with tight error bounds on the Monte Carlo tails, we have derived non-asymptotic, end-to-end L 2 ​ ( Ω ) L^{2}(\Omega) a priori error bounds for FK-PINNs using tanh \tanh activation trained with finitely many steps of gradient descent. Those estimates are based on pseudo-dimension bounds for first- and second-order derivatives of tanh \tanh networks, which are of independent interest. Numerical experiments confirm our theoretical findings, showing more stable optimization, faster training, and lower solution error than baseline PINNs, even with modest Monte Carlo budgets.

Impact Statement 

This paper presents work whose goal is to advance the field of Machine Learning. There are many potential societal consequences of our work, none which we feel must be specifically highlighted here.

Appendix A More on Feynman–Kac Representations and Probabilistic PDE Solutions 

This appendix collects standard probabilistic representations for linear and nonlinear parabolic and elliptic PDEs. By a Feynman–Kac (FK) representation we mean a formula of the form ( 3.1 ) expressing the solution u u as the expectation of a running and terminal functional of a suitable Markov process with generator ℒ \mathcal{L} . This structure underlies the Monte Carlo supervision term and FK-PINN loss introduced in Section 3 , but the discussion here is self-contained. The following subsections present representative examples—diffusion and jump–diffusion generators, semilinear and fully nonlinear equations, branching diffusions, and stochastic control/HJB problems—to illustrate that PDEs admitting such stochastic (Feynman–Kac-type) representations form a large and mathematically rich class. We refer the interested readers to (Dynkin, 1965 ; Ethier and Kurtz, 2009 ; Karatzas and Shreve, 2014 ) for background on Markov processes and classical FK formulas. 

A.1 Linear parabolic and elliptic equations with diffusion generators 

Let B B be a d d -dimensional Brownian motion and consider the Itô SDE 

d ​ X s = b ​ ( X s ) ​ d ​ s + σ ​ ( X s ) ​ d ​ B s , X t = x ∈ ℝ d , dX_{s}=b(X_{s})\,ds+\sigma(X_{s})\,dB_{s},\qquad X_{t}=x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, (A.1) 

where b : ℝ d → ℝ d b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d} and σ : ℝ d → ℝ d × d \sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} satisfy standard Lipschitz and linear growth conditions (Karatzas and Shreve, 2014 ) . The associated generator ℒ \mathcal{L} acts on smooth test functions φ \varphi as 

( ℒ ​ φ ) ​ ( x ) = b ​ ( x ) ⋅ ∇ φ ​ ( x ) + 1 2 ​ Tr ⁡ ( a ​ ( x ) ​ D 2 ​ φ ​ ( x ) ) , a ​ ( x ) := σ ​ ( x ) ​ σ ​ ( x ) ⊤ . (\mathcal{L}\varphi)(x)=b(x)\cdot\nabla\varphi(x)+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(a(x)D^{2}\varphi(x)\bigr),\qquad a(x):=\sigma(x)\sigma(x)^{\top}. (A.2) 

A prototypical linear parabolic Cauchy problem on [ 0 , T ] × ℝ d [0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d} is 

{ ∂ t u ​ ( t , x ) + ( ℒ ​ u ​ ( t , ⋅ ) ) ​ ( x ) − V ​ ( t , x ) ​ u ​ ( t , x ) + f ​ ( t , x ) = 0 , ( t , x ) ∈ [ 0 , T ) × ℝ d , u ​ ( T , x ) = g ​ ( x ) , x ∈ ℝ d , \begin{cases}\partial_{t}u(t,x)+(\mathcal{L}u(t,\cdot))(x)-V(t,x)u(t,x)+f(t,x)=0,&(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[2.5pt] u(T,x)=g(x),&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases} (A.3) 

with bounded (or suitably integrable) data V , f , g V,f,g . Under standard regularity assumptions on b , σ , V , f , g b,\sigma,V,f,g (e.g. Dynkin, 1965 ; Karatzas and Shreve, 2014 ) , the classical Feynman–Kac formula yields 

u ​ ( t , x ) = 𝔼 t , x ​ [ e − ∫ t T V ​ ( s , X s ) ​ 𝑑 s ​ g ​ ( X T ) + ∫ t T e − ∫ t s V ​ ( r , X r ) ​ 𝑑 r ​ f ​ ( s , X s ) ​ 𝑑 s ] , u(t,x)=\mathbb{E}^{t,x}\Bigl[e^{-\int_{t}^{T}V(s,X_{s})\,ds}\,g(X_{T})+\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\int_{t}^{s}V(r,X_{r})\,dr}f(s,X_{s})\,ds\Bigr], (A.4) 

where X X solves ( A.1 ) with initial condition X t = x X_{t}=x . 

Elliptic boundary value problems such as Poisson, mean exit time, or committor equations arise by dropping the time derivative in ( A.3 ). For a bounded C 2 C^{2} domain D ⊂ ℝ d D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} , define the exit time τ D := inf { s ≥ 0 : X t + s ∉ D } \tau_{D}:=\inf\{s\geq 0:X_{t+s}\notin D\} . Under standard conditions, the Dirichlet problem 

{ ( ℒ ​ u ) ​ ( x ) − V ​ ( x ) ​ u ​ ( x ) + f ​ ( x ) = 0 , x ∈ D , u ​ ( x ) = g ​ ( x ) , x ∈ ∂ D , \begin{cases}(\mathcal{L}u)(x)-V(x)u(x)+f(x)=0,&x\in D,\\[1.99997pt] u(x)=g(x),&x\in\partial D,\end{cases} (A.5) 

admits the representation 

u ​ ( x ) = 𝔼 x ​ [ ∫ 0 τ D e − ∫ 0 s V ​ ( X r ) ​ 𝑑 r ​ f ​ ( X s ) ​ 𝑑 s + e − ∫ 0 τ D V ​ ( X r ) ​ 𝑑 r ​ g ​ ( X τ D ) ] , u(x)=\mathbb{E}^{x}\Bigl[\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}e^{-\int_{0}^{s}V(X_{r})\,dr}f(X_{s})\,ds+e^{-\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}V(X_{r})\,dr}g(X_{\tau_{D}})\Bigr], (A.6) 

and analogous formulas hold for Neumann and Robin boundary conditions in terms of reflected diffusions and boundary local time (cf. Dynkin, 1965 ; Fleming and Soner, 2006 ) . 

For the boundary value problem ( 2.1 )–( 2.2 ) considered in Section 3 , the operator ℒ \mathcal{L} and data ( f , g ) (f,g) fit into the elliptic template ( A.5 ) (with V V encoding any zeroth-order term). In particular, in the Poisson, mean exit time, and committor benchmarks of the main text one typically has V ≡ 0 V\equiv 0 , so that ( A.6 ) reduces to 

u ​ ( x ) = 𝔼 x ​ [ ∫ 0 τ D f ​ ( X s ) ​ 𝑑 s + g ​ ( X τ D ) ] , u(x)=\mathbb{E}^{x}\Bigl[\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}f(X_{s})\,ds+g\bigl(X_{\tau_{D}}\bigr)\Bigr], 

which is of the form ( 3.1 ), with running and terminal functionals r r and h h determined (up to the sign convention for ℒ \mathcal{L} ) by the source term f f and boundary data g g . In the algorithms of the main text, expectations such as ( A.4 ) and ( A.6 ) are approximated by simulating time-discretized trajectories of ( A.1 ) (e.g. Euler–Maruyama) and averaging over samples. Weak and strong error estimates for such schemes can be found in (Kloeden and Platen, 1992 ; Glasserman, 2004 ) . 

A.2 Linear equations with nonlocal generators 

Let X X be a Lévy process in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} with Lévy triplet ( b , a , ν ) (b,a,\nu) , where b ∈ ℝ d b\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , a ∈ ℝ d × d a\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} is symmetric nonnegative definite, and ν \nu is a Lévy measure. Its generator ℒ \mathcal{L} acts on C c 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) as 

( ℒ ​ φ ) ​ ( x ) \displaystyle(\mathcal{L}\varphi)(x) = b ⋅ ∇ φ ​ ( x ) + 1 2 ​ Tr ⁡ ( a ​ D 2 ​ φ ​ ( x ) ) \displaystyle=b\cdot\nabla\varphi(x)+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(aD^{2}\varphi(x)\bigr) 

+ ∫ ℝ d ∖ { 0 } [ φ ​ ( x + y ) − φ ​ ( x ) − 𝟏 { | y | ≤ 1 } ​ y ⋅ ∇ φ ​ ( x ) ] ​ ν ​ ( d ​ y ) , \displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\Bigl[\varphi(x+y)-\varphi(x)-\mathbf{1}_{\{|y|\leq 1\}}\,y\cdot\nabla\varphi(x)\Bigr]\nu(dy), (A.7) 

see, for instance, (Applebaum, 2009 ) . 

Linear parabolic and elliptic equations with the nonlocal operator ℒ \mathcal{L} in ( A.7 ) have the same abstract form as ( A.3 ) and ( A.5 ). Under suitable conditions on ( b , a , ν ) (b,a,\nu) and the data, the solution u u admits a Feynman–Kac representation identical to ( A.4 ), with X X now a Lévy (or more generally a Lévy-type) process (see, e.g., Dynkin, 1965 ; Applebaum, 2009 ) . Monte Carlo approximation then proceeds by simulating jump–diffusion paths (using standard jump-adapted schemes) and averaging the corresponding functionals (Glasserman, 2004 ) . In principle, FK-PINNs could be extended to this setting by supervising the network with such Lévy paths instead of pure diffusions. 

A.3 Semilinear parabolic equations and BSDE representations 

Consider the semilinear parabolic PDE 

{ ∂ t u ​ ( t , x ) + ( ℒ ​ u ​ ( t , ⋅ ) ) ​ ( x ) + F ​ ( t , x , u ​ ( t , x ) , σ ​ ( x ) ⊤ ​ ∇ u ​ ( t , x ) ) = 0 , ( t , x ) ∈ [ 0 , T ) × ℝ d , u ​ ( T , x ) = g ​ ( x ) , x ∈ ℝ d , \begin{cases}\partial_{t}u(t,x)+(\mathcal{L}u(t,\cdot))(x)+F\bigl(t,x,u(t,x),\sigma(x)^{\top}\nabla u(t,x)\bigr)=0,&(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[2.5pt] u(T,x)=g(x),&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases} (A.8) 

where ℒ \mathcal{L} is the diffusion generator ( A.2 ), F F is Lipschitz in ( y , z ) (y,z) with linear growth, and g g is bounded and Lipschitz. Let X X solve ( A.1 ) and consider the forward–backward system 

d ​ X s \displaystyle dX_{s} = b ​ ( X s ) ​ d ​ s + σ ​ ( X s ) ​ d ​ B s , X t = x , \displaystyle=b(X_{s})\,ds+\sigma(X_{s})\,dB_{s},\qquad X_{t}=x, (A.9) 

Y s \displaystyle Y_{s} = g ​ ( X T ) + ∫ s T F ​ ( r , X r , Y r , Z r ) ​ 𝑑 r − ∫ s T Z r ​ 𝑑 B r , s ∈ [ t , T ] . \displaystyle=g(X_{T})+\int_{s}^{T}F\bigl(r,X_{r},Y_{r},Z_{r}\bigr)\,dr-\int_{s}^{T}Z_{r}\,dB_{r},\qquad s\in[t,T]. (A.10) 

Under the above assumptions, there exists a unique adapted solution ( Y , Z ) (Y,Z) to ( A.10 ) (Pardoux and Peng, 1990 ; El Karoui et al. , 1997 ) , and the nonlinear Feynman–Kac formula identifies 

u ​ ( t , x ) = Y t t , x , u(t,x)=Y_{t}^{t,x}, (A.11) 

where ( X t , x , Y t , x , Z t , x ) (X^{t,x},Y^{t,x},Z^{t,x}) denotes the solution of ( A.9 )–( A.10 ) with X t t , x = x X_{t}^{t,x}=x . Conversely, if u u is a sufficiently regular solution of ( A.8 ), then ( Y s , Z s ) = ( u ​ ( s , X s ) , σ ​ ( X s ) ⊤ ​ ∇ u ​ ( s , X s ) ) (Y_{s},Z_{s})=(u(s,X_{s}),\sigma(X_{s})^{\top}\nabla u(s,X_{s})) solves ( A.10 ) (Pardoux and Peng, 1990 ; El Karoui et al. , 1997 ) . 

Monte Carlo approximations for ( A.8 ) discretize the forward SDE and the BSDE and estimate the conditional expectations in the backward recursion by regression-based methods (Bouchard and Touzi, 2004 ) . In the present paper we only need the linear case where F ​ ( t , x , y , z ) = − V ​ ( t , x ) ​ y + f ​ ( t , x ) F(t,x,y,z)=-V(t,x)\,y+f(t,x) , which reduces to the classical FK form in Section 3.1 , but the nonlinear Feynman–Kac formula suggests a natural path to extending FK-PINNs to general semilinear problems by coupling PINNs with Monte Carlo BSDE solvers. 

A.4 Semilinear equations with polynomial nonlinearities and branching diffusions 

Branching diffusion processes provide representations for semilinear equations of reaction–diffusion type. A prototype is 

∂ t u ​ ( t , x ) = ( ℒ ​ u ​ ( t , ⋅ ) ) ​ ( x ) + λ ​ ( u ​ ( t , x ) p − u ​ ( t , x ) ) , u ​ ( 0 , x ) = u 0 ​ ( x ) , \partial_{t}u(t,x)=(\mathcal{L}u(t,\cdot))(x)+\lambda\bigl(u(t,x)^{p}-u(t,x)\bigr),\qquad u(0,x)=u_{0}(x), (A.12) 

with λ > 0 \lambda>0 , integer p ≥ 2 p\geq 2 , and ℒ \mathcal{L} the diffusion generator ( A.2 ). Under appropriate integrability and non-explosion conditions, the solution of ( A.12 ) can be written as 

u ​ ( t , x ) = 𝔼 x ​ [ ∏ i = 1 N t u 0 ​ ( X t ( i ) ) ] , u(t,x)=\mathbb{E}^{x}\Bigl[\prod_{i=1}^{N_{t}}u_{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{(i)}\bigr)\Bigr], (A.13) 

where ( X t ( i ) ) 1 ≤ i ≤ N t (X_{t}^{(i)})_{1\leq i\leq N_{t}} are the positions of particles in a branching diffusion system with spatial motion governed by ℒ \mathcal{L} and binary or p p -ary branching at rate λ \lambda (see, e.g., Dynkin, 1991 ) . More general semilinear PDEs with polynomial nonlinearities in u u and ∇ u \nabla u admit representations in terms of marked branching diffusions (Henry-Labordère et al. , 2019 ; Agarwal and Claisse, 2020 ) . 

Monte Carlo evaluation of ( A.13 ) proceeds by simulating the associated random branching tree and averaging the weight functional, provided the variance is controlled (Henry-Labordère et al. , 2019 ) . 

A.5 Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equations from stochastic control 

Let A A be a compact metric control set. For an A A -valued progressively measurable control process α \alpha , consider the controlled diffusion 

d ​ X s t , x , α = b ​ ( X s t , x , α , α s ) ​ d ​ s + σ ​ ( X s t , x , α , α s ) ​ d ​ B s , X t t , x , α = x , dX_{s}^{t,x,\alpha}=b\bigl(X_{s}^{t,x,\alpha},\alpha_{s}\bigr)\,ds+\sigma\bigl(X_{s}^{t,x,\alpha},\alpha_{s}\bigr)\,dB_{s},\qquad X_{t}^{t,x,\alpha}=x, (A.14) 

with running cost ℓ : [ 0 , T ] × ℝ d × A → ℝ \ell:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A\to\mathbb{R} and terminal cost g : ℝ d → ℝ g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} . The value function is 

u ​ ( t , x ) := sup α 𝔼 t , x , α ​ [ ∫ t T ℓ ​ ( s , X s t , x , α , α s ) ​ 𝑑 s + g ​ ( X T t , x , α ) ] , u(t,x):=\sup_{\alpha}\mathbb{E}^{t,x,\alpha}\Bigl[\int_{t}^{T}\ell\bigl(s,X_{s}^{t,x,\alpha},\alpha_{s}\bigr)\,ds+g\bigl(X_{T}^{t,x,\alpha}\bigr)\Bigr], (A.15) 

where the supremum ranges over all admissible controls. Writing ℒ a \mathcal{L}^{a} for the diffusion generator associated with the frozen control a ∈ A a\in A , the corresponding Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation is 

{ ∂ t u ​ ( t , x ) + sup a ∈ A { ( ℒ a ​ u ​ ( t , ⋅ ) ) ​ ( x ) + ℓ ​ ( t , x , a ) } = 0 , ( t , x ) ∈ [ 0 , T ) × ℝ d , u ​ ( T , x ) = g ​ ( x ) , x ∈ ℝ d . \begin{cases}\partial_{t}u(t,x)+\displaystyle\sup_{a\in A}\bigl\{(\mathcal{L}^{a}u(t,\cdot))(x)+\ell(t,x,a)\bigr\}=0,&(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[3.00003pt] u(T,x)=g(x),&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{cases} (A.16) 

Under standard compactness, measurability, and regularity assumptions, u u is the unique bounded viscosity solution of ( A.16 ) and coincides with ( A.15 ) (see Fleming and Soner, 2006 ) . 

Stochastic representations of the form ( A.15 ) can be approximated numerically by simulation-based dynamic programming and related Monte Carlo methods for controlled diffusions; see (Fleming and Soner, 2006 ) for an overview. For high-dimensional parabolic PDEs and BSDEs, including HJB-type equations from stochastic control, deep learning-based methods can approximate the gradient of the value function (interpreted as a feedback control or policy) by a neural network and train it via Monte Carlo simulation of the underlying diffusion; see, for example, (E et al. , 2017 ) . While FK-PINNs in the present work are restricted to linear operators, the HJB representation suggests a possible extension in which the neural network is supervised by Monte Carlo estimates of value functions under sampled controls. 

A.6 Fully nonlinear parabolic equations and second-order BSDEs 

Consider a fully nonlinear parabolic PDE of the form 

− ∂ t u ​ ( t , x ) + F ​ ( t , x , u ​ ( t , x ) , D ​ u ​ ( t , x ) , D 2 ​ u ​ ( t , x ) ) = 0 , u ​ ( T , x ) = g ​ ( x ) , -\partial_{t}u(t,x)+F\bigl(t,x,u(t,x),Du(t,x),D^{2}u(t,x)\bigr)=0,\qquad u(T,x)=g(x), (A.17) 

where F F is nonlinear in the Hessian D 2 ​ u D^{2}u and satisfies suitable ellipticity, monotonicity, and regularity conditions ensuring a comparison principle and well-posedness in the viscosity sense. Second-order backward SDEs (2BSDEs) provide pathwise representations of solutions to ( A.17 ) (Cheridito et al. , 2007 ) . Roughly speaking, given a forward diffusion X X , one considers an adapted quadruple ( Y , Z , Γ , A ) (Y,Z,\Gamma,A) solving a 2BSDE whose first component satisfies 

Y t t , x = u ​ ( t , X t t , x ) , Y_{t}^{t,x}=u(t,X_{t}^{t,x}), (A.18) 

and conversely any such 2BSDE solution identifies the unique viscosity solution u u of ( A.17 ) (Cheridito et al. , 2007 ) . Numerical schemes discretize both the forward diffusion and the 2BSDE and approximate conditional expectations by Monte Carlo in an enlarged state space, providing a probabilistic route to numerical solutions of fully nonlinear equations (Cheridito et al. , 2007 ) . Extending FK-PINNs to this 2BSDE setting is an interesting direction for future research but lies beyond the scope of this paper. 

A.7 Summary table 

For convenience, Table 2 summarizes the PDE classes discussed above, the corresponding operators, the probabilistic objects, and some standard references.

Table 2 : PDE classes covered by standard probabilistic representations. Here ℒ \mathcal{L} denotes a (possibly nonlocal) Markov generator, X X the corresponding Markov or controlled process, and expectations 𝔼 t , x ​ [ ⋅ ] \mathbb{E}^{t,x}[\cdot] are taken with respect to the law of X X started from ( t , x ) (t,x) . The FK-PINNs studied in the main text use the first (linear diffusion) row, but the same Monte Carlo supervision principle could in principle be adapted to the other classes. 

Appendix B Preconditioning Effect of the FK data term 

We now prove the conditioning result stated informally as Theorem 5.4 . The idea of the proof is to compare the empirical Gauss–Newton matrix of the PDE-only objective with that of the FK-augmented objective, and to show that the supervised FK term prevents the smallest nonzero eigenvalues from collapsing as the residual mesh is refined. 

We write L PDE + BC ​ ( θ ) L_{\mathrm{PDE+BC}}(\theta) for the empirical PDE residual plus Dirichlet boundary loss and L data ​ ( θ ) L_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) for a supervised data loss, and we consider 

L λ ​ ( θ ) := L PDE + BC ​ ( θ ) + λ ​ L data ​ ( θ ) . L_{\lambda}(\theta):=L_{\mathrm{PDE+BC}}(\theta)+\lambda\,L_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta). 

This is the same structure as the fixed-weight FK-PINN objective in Section 5 , with L data L_{\mathrm{data}} playing the role of the FK supervision term and λ \lambda corresponding to λ FK \lambda_{\mathrm{FK}} . 

The proof combines the residual ill-conditioning mechanism in (De Ryck et al. , 2024 ; Rathore et al. , 2024 ) described by Proposition B.2 , with the local PL ∗ control for supervised least squares from (Liu et al. , 2022 ) . In an overparameterized regime, the data Gauss–Newton matrix is typically low rank, so adding it does not automatically bound the smallest eigenvalue of the sum. We therefore impose a compatibility condition, Assumption B.1 , which ensures that every direction relevant to the PDE and boundary terms is also seen by the data term. Under this condition, the smallest nonzero eigenvalues of the combined Gauss–Newton matrix are uniformly bounded below, which yields the PL ∗ constant and the condition-number bound in Theorem B.9 . 

B.1 PINN objective with noisy data 

We consider a linear PDE with Dirichlet boundary conditions on a bounded domain Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} , 

ℒ ​ [ u ] ​ ( x ) \displaystyle\mathcal{L}[u](x) = f ​ ( x ) , \displaystyle=f(x), x ∈ Ω , \displaystyle x\in\Omega, (B.1) 

u ​ ( x ) \displaystyle u(x) = g ​ ( x ) , \displaystyle=g(x), x ∈ ∂ Ω , \displaystyle x\in\partial\Omega, 

where ℒ \mathcal{L} is a linear differential operator, and f f and g g are given functions. We assume that ( B.1 ) is well-posed in the sense that there exists a unique solution u ⋆ u^{\star} in an appropriate function space. 

Let θ ∈ ℝ p \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p} denote the PINN parameter vector, and let u ​ ( ⋅ ; θ ) u(\cdot;\theta) be a neural network parameterization of u u . We distinguish three types of training points: 

• 
Residual points { x i res } i = 1 n res ⊂ Ω \{x_{i}^{\mathrm{res}}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{res}}}\subset\Omega . 


• 
Boundary points { x j bc } j = 1 n bc ⊂ ∂ Ω \{x_{j}^{\mathrm{bc}}\}_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{bc}}}\subset\partial\Omega . 


• 
Data points { x i d } i = 1 n d ⊂ Ω \{x_{i}^{\mathrm{d}}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{d}}}\subset\Omega . 


Residual and boundary terms 

The empirical residual and boundary losses are defined by 

L PDE + BC ​ ( θ ) := 1 2 ​ n res ​ ∑ i = 1 n res ( ℒ ​ [ u ​ ( x i res ; θ ) ] − f ​ ( x i res ) ) 2 + 1 2 ​ n bc ​ ∑ j = 1 n bc ( u ​ ( x j bc ; θ ) − g ​ ( x j bc ) ) 2 . L_{\mathrm{PDE+BC}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2n_{\mathrm{res}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{res}}}\bigl(\mathcal{L}[u(x_{i}^{\mathrm{res}};\theta)]-f(x_{i}^{\mathrm{res}})\bigr)^{2}+\frac{1}{2n_{\mathrm{bc}}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{bc}}}\bigl(u(x_{j}^{\mathrm{bc}};\theta)-g(x_{j}^{\mathrm{bc}})\bigr)^{2}. (B.2) 

Noisy data term 

We additionally observe (possibly noisy) function values at the data points, 

y i = u ⋆ ​ ( x i d ) + ξ i , i = 1 , … , n d , y_{i}=u^{\star}(x_{i}^{\mathrm{d}})+\xi_{i},\quad i=1,\dots,n_{\mathrm{d}}, 

where the noise variables ξ i \xi_{i} are independent across i i and satisfy 𝔼 ​ [ ξ i ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}]=0 . 

The data-supervision term with weight λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0 is 

L data ​ ( θ ) := 1 2 ​ n d ​ ∑ i = 1 n d ( u ​ ( x i d ; θ ) − y i ) 2 . L_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2n_{\mathrm{d}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{d}}}\bigl(u(x_{i}^{\mathrm{d}};\theta)-y_{i}\bigr)^{2}. (B.3) 

The full PINN objective is then 

L λ ​ ( θ ) := L PDE + BC ​ ( θ ) + λ ​ L data ​ ( θ ) . L_{\lambda}(\theta):=L_{\mathrm{PDE+BC}}(\theta)+\lambda\,L_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta). (B.4) 

We denote by Θ λ ⋆ \Theta_{\lambda}^{\star} the set of global minimizers of L λ L_{\lambda} : 

Θ λ ⋆ := arg ⁡ min θ ∈ ℝ p ⁡ L λ ​ ( θ ) . \Theta_{\lambda}^{\star}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}L_{\lambda}(\theta). 

Stacked feature map and Gauss–Newton matrix. 

To streamline notation, define the residual, boundary, and data feature maps 

F res ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle F_{\mathrm{res}}(\theta) := 1 n res ​ ( ℒ ​ [ u ​ ( x 1 res ; θ ) ] − f ​ ( x 1 res ) , … , ℒ ​ [ u ​ ( x n res res ; θ ) ] − f ​ ( x n res res ) ) ⊤ , \displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{res}}}}\bigl(\mathcal{L}[u(x_{1}^{\mathrm{res}};\theta)]-f(x_{1}^{\mathrm{res}}),\dots,\mathcal{L}[u(x_{n_{\mathrm{res}}}^{\mathrm{res}};\theta)]-f(x_{n_{\mathrm{res}}}^{\mathrm{res}})\bigr)^{\top}, 

F bc ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle F_{\mathrm{bc}}(\theta) := 1 n bc ​ ( u ​ ( x 1 bc ; θ ) − g ​ ( x 1 bc ) , … , u ​ ( x n bc bc ; θ ) − g ​ ( x n bc bc ) ) ⊤ , \displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{bc}}}}\bigl(u(x_{1}^{\mathrm{bc}};\theta)-g(x_{1}^{\mathrm{bc}}),\dots,u(x_{n_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\mathrm{bc}};\theta)-g(x_{n_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\mathrm{bc}})\bigr)^{\top}, 

F data ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle F_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) := 1 n d ​ ( u ​ ( x 1 d ; θ ) − y 1 , … , u ​ ( x n d d ; θ ) − y n d ) ⊤ . \displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{d}}}}\bigl(u(x_{1}^{\mathrm{d}};\theta)-y_{1},\dots,u(x_{n_{\mathrm{d}}}^{\mathrm{d}};\theta)-y_{n_{\mathrm{d}}}\bigr)^{\top}. 

Then 

L PDE + BC ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ ‖ F res ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 + 1 2 ​ ‖ F bc ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 , L data ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ ‖ F data ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 . L_{\mathrm{PDE+BC}}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl\|F_{\mathrm{res}}(\theta)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\bigl\|F_{\mathrm{bc}}(\theta)\bigr\|_{2}^{2},\quad L_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl\|F_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}. 

We introduce the stacked feature map 

F λ ​ ( θ ) := [ F res ​ ( θ ) F bc ​ ( θ ) λ ​ F data ​ ( θ ) ] ∈ ℝ n λ , n λ := n res + n bc + n d , F_{\lambda}(\theta):=\begin{bmatrix}F_{\mathrm{res}}(\theta)\\ F_{\mathrm{bc}}(\theta)\\ \sqrt{\lambda}\,F_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\lambda}},\quad n_{\lambda}:=n_{\mathrm{res}}+n_{\mathrm{bc}}+n_{\mathrm{d}}, (B.5) 

so that L λ ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ ‖ F λ ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 L_{\lambda}(\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|F_{\lambda}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2} . Let J F λ ​ ( θ ) ∈ ℝ n λ × p J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\lambda}\times p} denote the Jacobian of F λ F_{\lambda} at θ \theta . The associated empirical Gauss–Newton matrix is 

G λ ​ ( θ ) := J F λ ​ ( θ ) ⊤ ​ J F λ ​ ( θ ) ∈ ℝ p × p . G_{\lambda}(\theta):=J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)^{\top}J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}. 

Its eigenvalues describe the local curvature of L λ L_{\lambda} in parameter space, and will provide the link between operator conditioning and PL-based optimization guarantees. From this point on, the conditioning question is reduced to bounding the smallest and largest eigenvalues of G λ ​ ( θ ) G_{\lambda}(\theta) in the region where the PL ∗ argument is applied. 
Definition B.1 . 
Let A ∈ ℝ p × p A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p} be symmetric and positive semidefinite. We write λ min + ​ ( A ) \lambda_{\min}^{+}(A) for the smallest strictly positive eigenvalue of A A . 

B.2 Tangent function space and population Gauss–Newton (PDE-only) 

We now recall the operator-theoretic interpretation of the PDE-only population Gauss–Newton matrix from De Ryck et al. ( 2024 ) . This section mostly summarizes their construction in the finite-width setting, to make our later use of their results self-contained. The only reason to introduce the tangent space ℋ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{H}(\theta) , the map T ​ ( θ ) T(\theta) , and the operator 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta) is to express Gauss–Newton curvature in a form where the differential operator ℒ \mathcal{L} appears as 𝒜 = ℒ ∗ ​ ℒ \mathcal{A}=\mathcal{L}^{\ast}\mathcal{L} . This will allow us to directly see which “directions” are poorly controlled by residual sampling and how a supervised term changes those directions. 

Let θ ∈ ℝ p \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p} be a parameter vector. For j = 1 , … , p j=1,\dots,p , we define the tangent functions 

ϕ j ​ ( x ; θ ) := ∂ ∂ θ j ​ u ​ ( x ; θ ) , x ∈ Ω . \phi_{j}(x;\theta):=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{j}}u(x;\theta),\quad x\in\Omega. 

The ϕ j ​ ( ⋅ ; θ ) \phi_{j}(\cdot;\theta) ’s span the corresponding tangent function space 

ℋ ​ ( θ ) := span ⁡ { ϕ 1 ​ ( ⋅ ; θ ) , … , ϕ p ​ ( ⋅ ; θ ) } ⊂ L 2 ​ ( Ω , μ ) , \mathcal{H}(\theta):=\operatorname{span}\{\phi_{1}(\cdot;\theta),\dots,\phi_{p}(\cdot;\theta)\}\subset L^{2}(\Omega,\mu), 

where μ \mu is the reference sampling measure on Ω \Omega (i.e., the distribution used to draw residual and data points). 

We then introduce the linear map 

T ​ ( θ ) : ℝ p → ℋ ​ ( θ ) , T ​ ( θ ) ​ v := ∑ j = 1 p v j ​ ϕ j ​ ( ⋅ ; θ ) , T(\theta):\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathcal{H}(\theta),\quad T(\theta)v:=\sum_{j=1}^{p}v_{j}\phi_{j}(\cdot;\theta), 

and its adjoint 

T ​ ( θ ) ∗ : L 2 ​ ( Ω , μ ) → ℝ p , ( T ​ ( θ ) ∗ ​ f ) j := ∫ Ω f ​ ( x ) ​ ϕ j ​ ( x ; θ ) ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( x ) . T(\theta)^{\ast}:L^{2}(\Omega,\mu)\to\mathbb{R}^{p},\quad\bigl(T(\theta)^{\ast}f\bigr)_{j}:=\int_{\Omega}f(x)\,\phi_{j}(x;\theta)\,d\mu(x). 

We then define the naturally associated (population) kernel integral operator as: 

𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) := T ​ ( θ ) ​ T ​ ( θ ) ∗ : L 2 ​ ( Ω , μ ) → ℋ ​ ( θ ) . \mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta):=T(\theta)T(\theta)^{\ast}:L^{2}(\Omega,\mu)\to\mathcal{H}(\theta). (B.6) 

Here ℋ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{H}(\theta) is the tangent function space spanned by { ϕ j ​ ( ⋅ ; θ ) } \{\phi_{j}(\cdot;\theta)\} , and 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) = T ​ ( θ ) ​ T ​ ( θ ) ∗ \mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta)=T(\theta)T(\theta)^{\ast} is the corresponding (finite-width) neural tangent kernel operator in the sense of De Ryck et al. ( 2024 ); Rathore et al. ( 2024 ) . 

Population PDE objective and Gauss–Newton matrix. 

For simplicity, we ignore the boundary term in this subsection. It contributes an additional positive semidefinite Gauss–Newton term, so keeping it separate can only strengthen the spectral lower bounds used later. With this simplification, the population counterpart of the residual loss is given by: 

L ∞ , PDE ​ ( θ ) := 1 2 ​ ∫ Ω ( ℒ ​ [ u ​ ( x ; θ ) ] − f ​ ( x ) ) 2 ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( x ) . L_{\infty,\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\mathcal{L}[u(x;\theta)]-f(x)\bigr)^{2}\,d\mu(x). (B.7) 

Define 𝒜 := ℒ ∗ ​ ℒ \mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{L}^{\ast}\mathcal{L} , where ℒ ∗ \mathcal{L}^{\ast} denotes the adjoint of ℒ \mathcal{L} with respect to the L 2 ​ ( Ω , μ ) L^{2}(\Omega,\mu) inner product. 

The population Gauss–Newton matrix for ( B.7 ) is 

G ∞ , PDE ​ ( θ ) := ∫ Ω ℒ ​ [ ∇ θ u ​ ( x ; θ ) ] ​ ℒ ​ [ ∇ θ u ​ ( x ; θ ) ] ⊤ ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( x ) , G_{\infty,\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta):=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{L}[\nabla_{\theta}u(x;\theta)]\,\mathcal{L}[\nabla_{\theta}u(x;\theta)]^{\top}\,d\mu(x), 

where ∇ θ u ​ ( x ; θ ) ∈ ℝ p \nabla_{\theta}u(x;\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{p} is the gradient of u u with respect to θ \theta . A direct computation shows that 

G ∞ , PDE ​ ( θ ) = T ​ ( θ ) ∗ ​ 𝒜 ​ T ​ ( θ ) . G_{\infty,\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)=T(\theta)^{\ast}\mathcal{A}T(\theta). (B.8) 

By (De Ryck et al. , 2024 ) , the finite-width population Gauss–Newton matrix G ∞ , PDE ​ ( θ ) G_{\infty,\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta) has the same non-zero eigenvalues as the compact, self-adjoint operator 

𝒜 ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) : ℋ ​ ( θ ) → ℋ ​ ( θ ) , ( 𝒜 ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) ) ​ f := 𝒜 ​ ( T ​ ( θ ) ​ T ​ ( θ ) ∗ ​ f ) , \mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta):\mathcal{H}(\theta)\to\mathcal{H}(\theta),\quad(\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta))f:=\mathcal{A}\bigl(T(\theta)T(\theta)^{\ast}f\bigr), 

and hence 

λ j ​ ( G ∞ , PDE ​ ( θ ) ) = λ j ​ ( 𝒜 ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) ) , κ ​ ( G ∞ , PDE ​ ( θ ) ) = κ ​ ( 𝒜 ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) ) , \lambda_{j}\bigl(G_{\infty,\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)\bigr)=\lambda_{j}\bigl(\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta)\bigr),\quad\kappa\bigl(G_{\infty,\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)\bigr)=\kappa\bigl(\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta)\bigr), (B.9) 

for j = 1 , … , p j=1,\dots,p . We refer to De Ryck et al. ( 2024 , Thm. 2.4) for the derivation, which is a finite-dimensional analogue of the fact that S ∗ ​ S S^{\ast}S and S ​ S ∗ SS^{\ast} have the same non-zero eigenvalues for any bounded operator S S between Hilbert spaces. 

B.3 Population Gauss–Newton with FK data 

We now incorporate the Feynman-Kac supervision term into the population analysis. For simplicity, we assume that residual and data points are sampled from the same measure μ \mu on Ω \Omega , but the more general case follows from the same argument. 

The population counterpart of the full loss ( B.4 ) is 

L ∞ , λ ​ ( θ ) := 1 2 ​ ∫ Ω ( ℒ ​ [ u ​ ( x ; θ ) ] − f ​ ( x ) ) 2 ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( x ) + λ 2 ​ ∫ Ω ( u ​ ( x ; θ ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x ) ) 2 ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( x ) . L_{\infty,\lambda}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\mathcal{L}[u(x;\theta)]-f(x)\bigr)^{2}\,d\mu(x)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(u(x;\theta)-u^{\star}(x)\bigr)^{2}\,d\mu(x). (B.10) 

The first term yields the Gauss–Newton matrix T ​ ( θ ) ∗ ​ 𝒜 ​ T ​ ( θ ) T(\theta)^{\ast}\mathcal{A}T(\theta) as in ( B.8 ). The data term contributes 

G ∞ , data ​ ( θ ) := λ ​ ∫ Ω ∇ θ u ​ ( x ; θ ) ​ ∇ θ u ​ ( x ; θ ) ⊤ ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( x ) = λ ​ T ​ ( θ ) ∗ ​ T ​ ( θ ) , G_{\infty,\mathrm{data}}(\theta):=\lambda\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{\theta}u(x;\theta)\,\nabla_{\theta}u(x;\theta)^{\top}\,d\mu(x)=\lambda\,T(\theta)^{\ast}T(\theta), 

since T ​ ( θ ) ​ v = ∑ j = 1 p v j ​ ϕ j ​ ( ⋅ ; θ ) T(\theta)v=\sum_{j=1}^{p}v_{j}\phi_{j}(\cdot;\theta) and ∇ θ u ​ ( x ; θ ) = ( ϕ 1 ​ ( x ; θ ) , … , ϕ p ​ ( x ; θ ) ) ⊤ \nabla_{\theta}u(x;\theta)=(\phi_{1}(x;\theta),\dots,\phi_{p}(x;\theta))^{\top} . 

Thus the combined population Gauss–Newton matrix for L ∞ , λ L_{\infty,\lambda} is 

G ∞ , λ ​ ( θ ) := G ∞ , PDE ​ ( θ ) + G ∞ , data ​ ( θ ) = T ​ ( θ ) ∗ ​ ( 𝒜 + λ ​ I ) ​ T ​ ( θ ) . G_{\infty,\lambda}(\theta):=G_{\infty,\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)+G_{\infty,\mathrm{data}}(\theta)=T(\theta)^{\ast}(\mathcal{A}+\lambda I)\,T(\theta). (B.11) 

Exactly as in the PDE-only case, G ∞ , λ ​ ( θ ) G_{\infty,\lambda}(\theta) is the matrix representation (in the basis { ϕ j ​ ( ⋅ ; θ ) } j = 1 p \{\phi_{j}(\cdot;\theta)\}_{j=1}^{p} ) of the compact, self-adjoint operator
