( 𝒜 + λ ​ I ) ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) : ℋ ​ ( θ ) → ℋ ​ ( θ ) , (\mathcal{A}+\lambda I)\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta):\mathcal{H}(\theta)\to\mathcal{H}(\theta), 

and we obtain from De Ryck et al. ( 2024 , Thm. 2.4) that their non-zero spectra and condition numbers coincide: 

λ j ​ ( G ∞ , λ ​ ( θ ) ) = λ j ​ ( ( 𝒜 + λ ​ I ) ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) ) , κ ​ ( G ∞ , λ ​ ( θ ) ) = κ ​ ( ( 𝒜 + λ ​ I ) ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) ) . \lambda_{j}\bigl(G_{\infty,\lambda}(\theta)\bigr)=\lambda_{j}\bigl((\mathcal{A}+\lambda I)\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta)\bigr),\quad\kappa\bigl(G_{\infty,\lambda}(\theta)\bigr)=\kappa\bigl((\mathcal{A}+\lambda I)\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta)\bigr). (B.12) 

Intuitively, adding the data term simply replaces 𝒜 \mathcal{A} by 𝒜 + λ ​ I \mathcal{A}+\lambda I in the operator-theoretic framework of De Ryck et al. ( 2024 ) , thereby lifting the small eigenvalues associated with ill-conditioned PDE directions. This is the population analogue of Tikhonov regularization or ridge regression, but applied at the level of the PDE operator 𝒜 \mathcal{A} composed with the tangent kernel 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta) . This population calculation motivates what we look for empirically: if the data term contributes a matrix with eigenvalues bounded below independently of the number of residual points, then the sum G λ = G res + G bc + λ ​ G data G_{\lambda}=G_{\mathrm{res}}+G_{\mathrm{bc}}+\lambda G_{\mathrm{data}} should inherit a uniform lower bound once λ > 0 \lambda>0 is fixed. 

B.4 Finite-sample Gauss–Newton matrices and effective conditioning 

We now move from the population operators in ( B.8 ) and ( B.11 ) to their empirical counterparts, and study how the data term affects the spectrum of G λ G_{\lambda} built from finitely many residual, boundary, and data points. 

Empirical Gauss–Newton matrices 

The empirical residual and data Gauss–Newton matrices at a parameter θ \theta are given by 

G res ​ ( θ ) := 1 n res ​ ∑ i = 1 n res ℒ ​ [ ∇ θ u ​ ( x i res ; θ ) ] ​ ℒ ​ [ ∇ θ u ​ ( x i res ; θ ) ] ⊤ , G_{\mathrm{res}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{res}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{res}}}\mathcal{L}[\nabla_{\theta}u(x_{i}^{\mathrm{res}};\theta)]\,\mathcal{L}[\nabla_{\theta}u(x_{i}^{\mathrm{res}};\theta)]^{\top}, (B.13) 

G data ​ ( θ ) := 1 n d ​ ∑ i = 1 n d ∇ θ u ​ ( x i d ; θ ) ​ ∇ θ u ​ ( x i d ; θ ) ⊤ . G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{d}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{d}}}\nabla_{\theta}u(x_{i}^{\mathrm{d}};\theta)\,\nabla_{\theta}u(x_{i}^{\mathrm{d}};\theta)^{\top}. (B.14) 

The boundary Gauss–Newton term G bc ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{bc}}(\theta) is defined analogously to G res ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{res}}(\theta) , replacing ℒ ​ [ u ] − f \mathcal{L}[u]-f by u − g u-g on ∂ Ω \partial\Omega . 

The full empirical Gauss–Newton matrix for L λ L_{\lambda} at θ \theta is then 

G λ ​ ( θ ) = G res ​ ( θ ) + G bc ​ ( θ ) + λ ​ G data ​ ( θ ) . G_{\lambda}(\theta)=G_{\mathrm{res}}(\theta)+G_{\mathrm{bc}}(\theta)+\lambda\,G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta). (B.15) 

In what follows, we focus on the behavior of G λ ​ ( θ ) G_{\lambda}(\theta) at a global minimizer θ λ ⋆ ∈ Θ λ ⋆ \theta_{\lambda}^{\star}\in\Theta_{\lambda}^{\star} , although the analysis applies more generally to any parameter θ \theta in a region of interest. 

Polynomial spectral decay for the PDE part 

We assume that at the parameter of interest θ λ ⋆ \theta_{\lambda}^{\star} the population operator 𝒜 ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) \mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star}) has polynomially decaying eigenvalues: 

λ j ​ ( 𝒜 ∘ 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ) ≤ C ​ j − 2 ​ α , j = 1 , 2 , … , \lambda_{j}\bigl(\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\bigr)\;\leq\;C\,j^{-2\alpha},\quad j=1,2,\dots, (B.16) 

for some constants C > 0 C>0 and α > 1 / 2 \alpha>1/2 . This type of decay is typical for elliptic operators on smooth domains, possibly combined with smoothness properties of the tangent kernel 𝒦 ∞ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) \mathcal{K}_{\infty}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star}) (see (De Ryck et al. , 2024 ) for some examples). 

Theorem 5.3 in the main text is proved in (Rathore et al. , 2024 , Theorem 8.4) in the same linear PDE and interpolation setting considered here. We will use the following finite-sample spectral scaling for the residual Gauss–Newton matrix at θ λ ⋆ \theta_{\lambda}^{\star} , stated in the notation of Section B.4 . 
Proposition B.2 . 
There exist constants c res > 0 c_{\mathrm{res}}>0 , Λ PDE < ∞ \Lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}<\infty and α > 1 / 2 \alpha>1/2 such that, for every δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) , there is n res min ​ ( δ ) ∈ ℕ n_{\mathrm{res}}^{\min}(\delta)\in\mathbb{N} with the following property: for all n res ≥ n res min ​ ( δ ) n_{\mathrm{res}}\geq n_{\mathrm{res}}^{\min}(\delta) , with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta over the sampling of the residual points, 

λ min ​ ( G res ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ) ≥ c res ​ n res − α , λ max ​ ( G res ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ) ≤ Λ PDE . \lambda_{\min}\bigl(G_{\mathrm{res}}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\bigr)\;\geq\;c_{\mathrm{res}}\,n_{\mathrm{res}}^{-\alpha},\quad\lambda_{\max}\bigl(G_{\mathrm{res}}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\bigr)\;\leq\;\Lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}. 
Proof. 
This is a restatement of (Rathore et al. , 2024 , Theorem 8.4) in the notation of ( B.13 ), with N = n res N=n_{\mathrm{res}} . ∎ 

Proposition B.2 captures the deterioration of the residual contribution under mesh refinement, as established in (Rathore et al. , 2024 ) . We now turn to the supervised term and its effect on the combined spectrum. 

Data PL ∗ \mathrm{PL}^{\ast} condition and finite-sample concentration 

The beneficial effect of the data term on conditioning requires that the tangent features at the data points are sufficiently rich and non-degenerate. Following the PL ∗ \mathrm{PL}^{\ast} viewpoint of (Liu et al. , 2022 ) , we encode this via a finite-width PL ∗ \mathrm{PL}^{\ast} result for the data term, Proposition B.3 , which yields a uniform spectral bound on the data tangent kernel and its associated empirical Gauss–Newton matrix. Informally, this proposition states that for a sufficiently wide network, the empirical data tangent kernel stays uniformly well conditioned in a ball around initialization, so that the supervised data loss satisfies a uniform PL ∗ inequality with constants independent of n d n_{\mathrm{d}} . 
Proposition B.3 . 
Consider the fully-connected network, initialization scheme, and fixed data points { x i d } i = 1 n d ⊂ Ω \{x_{i}^{\mathrm{d}}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{d}}}\subset\Omega in the setting of Liu et al. ( 2022 , Section 4) , and define the rescaled data feature map 

ℱ data ​ ( θ ) := 1 n d ​ ( u ​ ( x 1 d ; θ ) − y 1 , … , u ​ ( x n d d ; θ ) − y n d ) ∈ ℝ n d . \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{d}}}}\bigl(u(x_{1}^{\mathrm{d}};\theta)-y_{1},\ldots,u(x_{n_{\mathrm{d}}}^{\mathrm{d}};\theta)-y_{n_{\mathrm{d}}}\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathrm{d}}}. 

Let θ 0 \theta_{0} be the random initialization and define the associated tangent kernel K data K_{\mathrm{data}} by 

K data ​ ( θ ) := D ​ ℱ data ​ ( θ ) ​ D ​ ℱ data ​ ( θ ) ⊤ ∈ ℝ n d × n d , K_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta):=D\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\,D\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathrm{d}}\times n_{\mathrm{d}}}, 

where D ​ ℱ data ​ ( θ ) ∈ ℝ n d × p D\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathrm{d}}\times p} is the Jacobian matrix, and denote λ 0 := λ min ​ ( K data ​ ( θ 0 ) ) \lambda_{0}:=\lambda_{\min}\bigl(K_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta_{0})\bigr) . Fix any radius R > 0 R>0 , target μ data ∈ ( 0 , λ 0 ) \mu_{\mathrm{data}}\in(0,\lambda_{0}) , and confidence level δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) . Then there exists a universal constant C > 0 C>0 such that, if the neural network’s width m m satisfies 

m ≥ C ​ n d ​ R 6 ​ L + 2 ( λ 0 − μ data ) 2 ​ log ⁡ ( 2 ​ n d δ ) , m\;\geq\;C\,\frac{n_{\mathrm{d}}\,R^{6L+2}}{\bigl(\lambda_{0}-\mu_{\mathrm{data}}\bigr)^{2}}\,\log\!\Bigl(\frac{2n_{\mathrm{d}}}{\delta}\Bigr), (B.17) 

then the following holds with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta over the random initialization, simultaneously for all θ ∈ B ​ ( θ 0 , R ) \theta\in B(\theta_{0},R) : 

μ data ≤ λ min ​ ( K data ​ ( θ ) ) ≤ λ max ​ ( K data ​ ( θ ) ) ≤ M data , \mu_{\mathrm{data}}\;\leq\;\lambda_{\min}\bigl(K_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr)\;\leq\;\lambda_{\max}\bigl(K_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr)\;\leq\;M_{\mathrm{data}}, (B.18) 

for some M data < ∞ M_{\mathrm{data}}<\infty independent of n d n_{\mathrm{d}} . In particular, the empirical data loss L data ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ ‖ ℱ data ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 L_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl\|\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr\|_{2}^{2} satisfies a μ data \mu_{\mathrm{data}} – PL ∗ \mathrm{PL}^{\ast} inequality on B ​ ( θ 0 , R ) B(\theta_{0},R) . Moreover, G data ​ ( θ ) = D ​ ℱ data ​ ( θ ) ⊤ ​ D ​ ℱ data ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)=D\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)^{\top}D\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) and K data ​ ( θ ) K_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) have the same nonzero eigenvalues, hence the nonzero eigenvalues of G data ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) lie in [ μ data , M data ] [\mu_{\mathrm{data}},M_{\mathrm{data}}] . Equivalently, λ min + ​ ( G data ​ ( θ ) ) ≥ μ data \lambda_{\min}^{+}\bigl(G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr)\geq\mu_{\mathrm{data}} and λ max ​ ( G data ​ ( θ ) ) ≤ M data \lambda_{\max}\bigl(G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr)\leq M_{\mathrm{data}} . 
Proof sketch. 
This is the specialization of Liu et al. ( 2022 , Theorem 4) to ℱ = ℱ data \mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{data}} , together with the identification of the PL ∗ \mathrm{PL}^{\ast} constant with λ min ​ ( K data ​ ( θ ) ) \lambda_{\min}\bigl(K_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr) (Liu et al. , 2022 , Theorem 1) and the fact that G data ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) and K data ​ ( θ ) K_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) have the same non-zero eigenvalues. ∎ 

Fix a confidence level δ init ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta_{\mathrm{init}}\in(0,1) and choose the width m m so that ( B.17 ) holds with δ = δ init \delta=\delta_{\mathrm{init}} . Let ℰ init \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{init}} denote the corresponding high-probability initialization event. By Proposition B.3 , on ℰ init \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{init}} the following uniform spectral bounds hold on B ​ ( θ 0 , R ) B(\theta_{0},R) : 

λ min + ​ ( G data ​ ( θ ) ) ≥ μ data ​ and ​ λ max ​ ( G data ​ ( θ ) ) ≤ M data ​ for all ​ θ ∈ B ​ ( θ 0 , R ) . \lambda_{\min}^{+}\bigl(G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr)\geq\mu_{\mathrm{data}}\text{ and }\lambda_{\max}\bigl(G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr)\leq M_{\mathrm{data}}\text{ for all }\theta\in B(\theta_{0},R). (B.19) 

When p > n d p>n_{\mathrm{d}} , one typically has λ min ​ ( G data ​ ( θ ) ) = 0 \lambda_{\min}\bigl(G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr)=0 , so the relevant notion is the conditioning of G data ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) restricted to its range, quantified by λ min + ​ ( G data ​ ( θ ) ) \lambda_{\min}^{+}\bigl(G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)\bigr) . 

At this stage we have a uniform lower bound on the nonzero curvature provided by the data term through ( B.19 ). To ensure that this conditioning transfers to the combined Gauss–Newton matrix, we need a compatibility condition describing how the PDE term acts on directions that are poorly constrained (or unconstrained) by the data tangent features. In particular, we rule out nearly-flat PDE curvature on the data-orthogonal subspace and control the coupling between data-visible and data-invisible directions. This the object of Assumption B.1 that we now introduce. 

In what follows, we will let, for any θ ∈ ℝ p \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p} , P θ ∈ ℝ p × p P_{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p} denote the projection matrix onto the range of G data ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) , and I ∈ ℝ p × p I\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p} the identity. For x ∈ ℝ p x\in\mathbb{R}^{p} and A ∈ ℝ p × p A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p} , we will also slightly abuse notation and respectively denote by ‖ x ‖ := x 1 2 + … + x p 2 \|x\|:=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+\ldots+x_{p}^{2}} and ‖ A ‖ := sup v ∈ ℝ p : ‖ v ‖ = 1 ‖ A ​ v ‖ \|A\|:=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\|v\|=1}\|Av\| their Euclidean and operator norm. 
Assumption B.1 . 
Fix a set 𝒮 ⊂ B ​ ( θ 0 , R ) \mathcal{S}\subset B(\theta_{0},R) . There exist constants δ ′ , μ ⟂ > 0 \delta^{\prime},\mu_{\perp}>0 and ρ ≥ 0 \rho\geq 0 , such that with probability at least 1 − δ ′ 1-\delta^{\prime} over the initialization θ 0 \theta_{0} and the random sampling of the collocation, boundary and supervised data points, the following holds for every θ ∈ 𝒮 \theta\in\mathcal{S} : 

• 
λ min + ​ ( ( I − P θ ) ​ G PDE ​ ( θ ) ​ ( I − P θ ) ) ≥ μ ⟂ \displaystyle\lambda_{\min}^{+}\bigl((I-P_{\theta})G_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)(I-P_{\theta})\bigr)\geq\mu_{\perp} , (B.20) 


• 
‖ P θ ​ G PDE ​ ( θ ) ​ ( I − P θ ) ‖ ≤ ρ \displaystyle\left\|P_{\theta}G_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)(I-P_{\theta})\right\|\leq\rho . (B.21) 

Remark B.4 . 
If the supervised data is not random (e.g., coarse finite element grid), one can formulate Assumption B.1 in a completely analogous manner and reach the same conclusion. 

Assumption B.1 allows to bound from below the smallest eigenvalue of G λ ​ ( θ ) G_{\lambda}(\theta) on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} by an explicit quantity γ ​ ( λ ) \gamma(\lambda) , as we will now establish. 
Proposition B.5 . 
Assume that the conditions of Proposition B.3 are satisfied with radius R > 0 R>0 and confidence level δ init \delta_{\mathrm{init}} , and let 𝒮 ⊂ B ​ ( θ 0 , R ) \mathcal{S}\subset B(\theta_{0},R) be a set on the corresponding initialization event. Assume also that Assumption B.1 holds on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} . Then, with probability 1 − δ init − δ ′ ≡ 1 − δ 1-\delta_{\mathrm{init}}-\delta^{\prime}\equiv 1-\delta we have for every λ > 0 \lambda>0 and θ ∈ 𝒮 \theta\in\mathcal{S} , 

λ min + ​ ( G λ ​ ( θ ) ) ≥ γ ​ ( λ ) , \lambda_{\min}^{+}\bigl(G_{\lambda}(\theta)\bigr)\geq\gamma(\lambda), (B.22) 

where 

γ ​ ( λ ) ≔ ( λ ​ μ data + μ ⟂ ) − ( λ ​ μ data − μ ⟂ ) 2 + 4 ​ ρ 2 2 . \gamma(\lambda)\coloneqq\frac{(\lambda\mu_{\mathrm{data}}+\mu_{\perp})-\sqrt{(\lambda\mu_{\mathrm{data}}-\mu_{\perp})^{2}+4\rho^{2}}}{2}. 

In particular, if ρ 2 < λ ​ μ data ​ μ ⟂ \rho^{2}<\lambda\mu_{\mathrm{data}}\mu_{\perp} , then γ ​ ( λ ) > 0 \gamma(\lambda)>0 uniformly over θ ∈ 𝒮 \theta\in\mathcal{S} . 
Proof. 
Fix θ ∈ 𝒮 \theta\in\mathcal{S} . Decompose any v ∈ ℝ p v\in\mathbb{R}^{p} as v = v 1 + v 2 v=v_{1}+v_{2} with v 1 = P θ ​ v v_{1}=P_{\theta}v and v 2 = ( I − P θ ) ​ v v_{2}=(I-P_{\theta})v . Using Range ⁡ ( P θ ) = Range ⁡ ( G data ​ ( θ ) ) \operatorname{Range}(P_{\theta})=\operatorname{Range}(G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)) and ( B.19 ), 

v ⊤ ​ ( λ ​ G data ​ ( θ ) ) ​ v = λ ​ v 1 ⊤ ​ G data ​ ( θ ) ​ v 1 ≥ λ ​ μ data ​ ∥ v 1 ∥ 2 . v^{\top}(\lambda G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta))v=\lambda v_{1}^{\top}G_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)v_{1}\geq\lambda\mu_{\mathrm{data}}\lVert v_{1}\rVert^{2}. 

Similarly, by ( • ‣ B.1 ), for v 2 v_{2} orthogonal to the kernel of ( I − P θ ) ​ G PDE ​ ( θ ) ​ ( I − P θ ) (I-P_{\theta})G_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)(I-P_{\theta}) , we have 

v 2 ⊤ ​ G PDE ​ ( θ ) ​ v 2 = v ⊤ ​ ( I − P θ ) ​ G PDE ​ ( θ ) ​ ( I − P θ ) ​ v ≥ μ ⟂ ​ ∥ v 2 ∥ 2 . v_{2}^{\top}G_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)v_{2}=v^{\top}(I-P_{\theta})G_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)(I-P_{\theta})v\geq\mu_{\perp}\lVert v_{2}\rVert^{2}. 

For the cross term, by ( • ‣ B.1 ), 

2 ​ v 1 ⊤ ​ G PDE ​ ( θ ) ​ v 2 ≥ − 2 ​ ∥ P θ ​ G PDE ​ ( θ ) ​ ( I − P θ ) ∥ ​ ∥ v 1 ∥ ​ ∥ v 2 ∥ ≥ − 2 ​ ρ ​ ∥ v 1 ∥ ​ ∥ v 2 ∥ . 2v_{1}^{\top}G_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)v_{2}\geq-2\lVert P_{\theta}G_{\mathrm{PDE}}(\theta)(I-P_{\theta})\rVert\,\lVert v_{1}\rVert\,\lVert v_{2}\rVert\geq-2\rho\,\lVert v_{1}\rVert\,\lVert v_{2}\rVert. 

Putting these together, 

v ⊤ ​ G λ ​ ( θ ) ​ v ≥ [ ∥ v 1 ∥ ∥ v 2 ∥ ] ⊤ ​ [ λ ​ μ data − ρ − ρ μ ⟂ ] ​ [ ∥ v 1 ∥ ∥ v 2 ∥ ] . v^{\top}G_{\lambda}(\theta)v\geq\begin{bmatrix}\lVert v_{1}\rVert\\ \lVert v_{2}\rVert\end{bmatrix}^{\!\top}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda\mu_{\mathrm{data}}&-\rho\\ -\rho&\mu_{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert v_{1}\rVert\\ \lVert v_{2}\rVert\end{bmatrix}. 

Minimizing over ∥ v ∥ = 1 \lVert v\rVert=1 yields the smallest eigenvalue of the displayed 2 × 2 2\times 2 matrix, which equals γ ​ ( λ ) \gamma(\lambda) . By the variational formulation of the smallest non-zero eigenvalue, the claimed lower bound on λ min + ​ ( G λ ​ ( θ ) ) \lambda_{\min}^{+}(G_{\lambda}(\theta)) follows. ∎ 
Remark B.6 . 
Note that although we did not include it for simplicity, the boundary loss term has the same least-squares form as a supervised data term, so its Gauss–Newton contribution G bc ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{bc}}(\theta) is positive semidefinite and can be handled in an analogous manner. More specifically, in Proposition B.5 and Theorem B.9 we can include the boundary term while assuming that G bc ​ ( θ ) G_{\mathrm{bc}}(\theta) satisfies Assumption B.1 as well. The analogous conclusion then follows by mirroring the current argument. 

B.5 PL constant, condition number, and gradient descent 

We now translate the spectral bounds from Proposition B.5 into a Polyak–Łojasiewicz ( PL \mathrm{PL} ) constant and condition number for L λ L_{\lambda} , and derive implications for gradient descent. 

PL inequality and PL-based condition number 

Let 𝒮 ⊂ ℝ p \mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p} be a set containing θ λ ⋆ \theta_{\lambda}^{\star} . We say that L λ L_{\lambda} satisfies a PL ∗ inequality with constant μ λ > 0 \mu_{\lambda}>0 on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} if 

‖ ∇ L λ ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 2 ​ μ λ ≥ L λ ​ ( θ ) − L λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) , ∀ θ ∈ 𝒮 . \frac{\bigl\|\nabla L_{\lambda}(\theta)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}}{2\mu_{\lambda}}\;\geq\;L_{\lambda}(\theta)-L_{\lambda}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star}),\quad\forall\,\theta\in\mathcal{S}. (B.23) 

Let β L λ \beta_{L_{\lambda}} denote a smoothness constant on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , i.e. an upper bound on the operator norm of the Hessian, 

β L λ ≥ sup θ ∈ 𝒮 ‖ H L λ ​ ( θ ) ‖ . \beta_{L_{\lambda}}\;\geq\;\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{S}}\bigl\|H_{L_{\lambda}}(\theta)\bigr\|. 

The PL \mathrm{PL} -based condition number of L λ L_{\lambda} on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} is then defined by 

κ L λ ​ ( 𝒮 ) := β L λ μ λ . \kappa_{L_{\lambda}}(\mathcal{S}):=\frac{\beta_{L_{\lambda}}}{\mu_{\lambda}}. (B.24) 

We will use the following Lemma, which is nothing more than (Liu et al. , 2022 , Theorem 1) reformulated in our setup. 
Lemma B.7 . 
Let L ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ ‖ F ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 L(\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|F(\theta)\|_{2}^{2} , where F : ℝ p → ℝ n F:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{n} is differentiable on a set 𝒮 \mathcal{S} . Suppose there exists θ ⋆ ∈ 𝒮 \theta^{\star}\in\mathcal{S} with F ​ ( θ ⋆ ) = 0 F(\theta^{\star})=0 . Define the sample tangent kernel K F ​ ( θ ) := J F ​ ( θ ) ​ J F ​ ( θ ) ⊤ K_{F}(\theta):=J_{F}(\theta)\,J_{F}(\theta)^{\top} . If there exists μ > 0 \mu>0 such that λ min ​ ( K F ​ ( θ ) ) ≥ μ \lambda_{\min}(K_{F}(\theta))\geq\mu holds for every θ ∈ 𝒮 \theta\in\mathcal{S} , then L L satisfies the PL ∗ inequality on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} with constant μ \mu . 
Proof. 
For θ ∈ 𝒮 \theta\in\mathcal{S} , ∇ L ​ ( θ ) = J F ​ ( θ ) ⊤ ​ F ​ ( θ ) \nabla L(\theta)=J_{F}(\theta)^{\top}F(\theta) , hence ‖ ∇ L ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 = F ​ ( θ ) ⊤ ​ K F ​ ( θ ) ​ F ​ ( θ ) \|\nabla L(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}=F(\theta)^{\top}K_{F}(\theta)F(\theta) . The assumed bound gives ‖ ∇ L ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 ≥ μ ​ ‖ F ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 = 2 ​ μ ​ L ​ ( θ ) \|\nabla L(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\geq\mu\|F(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}=2\mu L(\theta) . Since L ​ ( θ ⋆ ) = 0 L(\theta^{\star})=0 , this is the claimed PL ∗ inequality. ∎ 

From basic linear algebra, we have the following relationship between the non-zero spectrum of K F K_{F} and that of G F G_{F} : 
Lemma B.8 . 
Let F : ℝ p → ℝ n F:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{n} be differentiable at θ \theta , with Jacobian J F ​ ( θ ) J_{F}(\theta) . Define G F ​ ( θ ) := J F ​ ( θ ) ⊤ ​ J F ​ ( θ ) G_{F}(\theta):=J_{F}(\theta)^{\top}J_{F}(\theta) and K F ​ ( θ ) := J F ​ ( θ ) ​ J F ​ ( θ ) ⊤ K_{F}(\theta):=J_{F}(\theta)J_{F}(\theta)^{\top} . Then G F ​ ( θ ) G_{F}(\theta) and K F ​ ( θ ) K_{F}(\theta) have the same nonzero eigenvalues. If J F ​ ( θ ) J_{F}(\theta) has full row rank, then K F ​ ( θ ) K_{F}(\theta) is positive definite and λ min ​ ( K F ​ ( θ ) ) = λ min + ​ ( G F ​ ( θ ) ) \lambda_{\min}\bigl(K_{F}(\theta)\bigr)=\lambda_{\min}^{+}\bigl(G_{F}(\theta)\bigr) . 

Bounding the PL constant and condition number 

Lemma B.7 applied to F λ F_{\lambda} shows that it suffices to control the smallest eigenvalue of the stacked sample kernel K F λ ​ ( θ ) K_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta) on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} to obtain a PL ∗ inequality. Lemma B.8 on the other hand shows that this smallest eigenvalue is equal to the non-zero such eigenvalue of G λ G_{\lambda} . Combining these facts with Proposition B.5 , which gives a lower bound on λ min + ​ ( G λ ) \lambda_{\min}^{+}(G_{\lambda}) yields the following uniform bound on the PL-based condition number, which is our main result. 
Theorem B.9 . 
Fix confidence levels δ init ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta_{\mathrm{init}}\in(0,1) and δ ′ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta^{\prime}\in(0,1) . Choose the width m m so that Proposition B.3 holds with δ = δ init \delta=\delta_{\mathrm{init}} , and let 𝒮 ⊂ B ​ ( θ 0 , R ) \mathcal{S}\subset B(\theta_{0},R) be any set containing a global minimizer θ λ ⋆ \theta_{\lambda}^{\star} of L λ L_{\lambda} satisfying F λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) = 0 F_{\lambda}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})=0 . Assume that Assumption B.1 holds on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} with confidence parameter δ ′ \delta^{\prime} , and let γ ​ ( λ ) \gamma(\lambda) be as in Proposition B.5 . 

Assume that J F λ J_{F_{\lambda}} is Lipschitz on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} with constant L J > 0 L_{J}>0 . Assume also that J F λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star}) has full row rank, and define 

s λ := σ min ​ ( J F λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ) , r λ := s λ 2 ​ L J , and ​ 𝒮 λ := 𝒮 ∩ B ​ ( θ λ ⋆ , r λ ) . s_{\lambda}:=\sigma_{\min}\bigl(J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\bigr),\ \ r_{\lambda}:=\frac{s_{\lambda}}{2L_{J}},\ \text{ and }\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{S}\cap B(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star},r_{\lambda}). 

Lastly, assume that there exist finite constants B F , B J > 0 B_{F},B_{J}>0 such that sup θ ∈ 𝒮 λ ‖ F λ ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 ≤ B F \sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}}\|F_{\lambda}(\theta)\|_{2}\leq B_{F} and sup θ ∈ 𝒮 λ ‖ J F λ ​ ( θ ) ‖ ≤ B J \sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}}\|J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)\|\leq B_{J} . 

Then, with probability at least 1 − δ init − δ ′ ≡ 1 − δ 1-\delta_{\mathrm{init}}-\delta^{\prime}\equiv 1-\delta , the loss L λ L_{\lambda} satisfies a PL ∗ \mathrm{PL}^{\ast} inequality on 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} with constant μ λ \mu_{\lambda} obeying 

μ λ ≥ γ ​ ( λ ) . \mu_{\lambda}\geq\gamma(\lambda). (B.25) 

Moreover, L λ L_{\lambda} is β L λ \beta_{L_{\lambda}} -smooth on 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} with β L λ ≤ B J 2 + L J ​ B F \beta_{L_{\lambda}}\leq B_{J}^{2}+L_{J}B_{F} , and its PL \mathrm{PL} -based condition number on 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} satisfies 

κ L λ ​ ( 𝒮 λ ) ≤ B J 2 + L J ​ B F γ ​ ( λ ) . \kappa_{L_{\lambda}}(\mathcal{S}_{\lambda})\leq\frac{B_{J}^{2}+L_{J}B_{F}}{\gamma(\lambda)}. (B.26) 
Proof. 
Work on the intersection of the initialization event from Proposition B.3 with δ = δ init \delta=\delta_{\mathrm{init}} and the event in Assumption B.1 on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} . This intersection event has probability at least 1 − δ init − δ ′ 1-\delta_{\mathrm{init}}-\delta^{\prime} , and on it Proposition B.5 gives λ min + ​ ( G λ ​ ( θ ) ) ≥ γ ​ ( λ ) \lambda_{\min}^{+}\bigl(G_{\lambda}(\theta)\bigr)\geq\gamma(\lambda) for every θ ∈ 𝒮 \theta\in\mathcal{S} . 

We next propagate full row rank from θ λ ⋆ \theta_{\lambda}^{\star} to 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} . For any θ ∈ 𝒮 λ \theta\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda} , Weyl’s inequalities for singular value perturbations (Horn and Johnson, 2012 ) yields 

σ min ​ ( J F λ ​ ( θ ) ) ≥ σ min ​ ( J F λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ) − ‖ J F λ ​ ( θ ) − J F λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ‖ ≥ s λ − L J ​ ‖ θ − θ λ ⋆ ‖ ≥ s λ 2 . \sigma_{\min}\bigl(J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)\bigr)\geq\sigma_{\min}\bigl(J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\bigr)-\bigl\|J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)-J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\bigr\|\geq s_{\lambda}-L_{J}\|\theta-\theta_{\lambda}^{\star}\|\geq\frac{s_{\lambda}}{2}. 

Therefore J F λ ​ ( θ ) J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta) has full row rank for every θ ∈ 𝒮 λ \theta\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda} . Lemma B.8 then implies that λ min ​ ( K F λ ​ ( θ ) ) = λ min + ​ ( G λ ​ ( θ ) ) \lambda_{\min}\bigl(K_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)\bigr)=\lambda_{\min}^{+}\bigl(G_{\lambda}(\theta)\bigr) holds for every θ ∈ 𝒮 λ \theta\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda} , hence λ min ​ ( K F λ ​ ( θ ) ) ≥ γ ​ ( λ ) \lambda_{\min}\bigl(K_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)\bigr)\geq\gamma(\lambda) on 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} . Applying Lemma B.7 to L λ ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ ‖ F λ ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 L_{\lambda}(\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|F_{\lambda}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2} and F λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) = 0 F_{\lambda}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})=0 yields the PL ∗ \mathrm{PL}^{\ast} inequality on 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} with constant μ λ ≥ γ ​ ( λ ) , \mu_{\lambda}\geq\gamma(\lambda), which is ( B.25 ). 

For smoothness on 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} , use ∇ L λ ​ ( θ ) = J F λ ​ ( θ ) ⊤ ​ F λ ​ ( θ ) \nabla L_{\lambda}(\theta)=J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)^{\top}F_{\lambda}(\theta) to write, for θ , θ ′ ∈ 𝒮 λ \theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda} , 

∇ L λ ​ ( θ ) − ∇ L λ ​ ( θ ′ ) = ( J F λ ​ ( θ ) − J F λ ​ ( θ ′ ) ) ⊤ ​ F λ ​ ( θ ) + J F λ ​ ( θ ′ ) ⊤ ​ ( F λ ​ ( θ ) − F λ ​ ( θ ′ ) ) . \nabla L_{\lambda}(\theta)-\nabla L_{\lambda}(\theta^{\prime})=\bigl(J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta)-J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta^{\prime})\bigr)^{\top}F_{\lambda}(\theta)+J_{F_{\lambda}}(\theta^{\prime})^{\top}\bigl(F_{\lambda}(\theta)-F_{\lambda}(\theta^{\prime})\bigr). 

The first term is bounded by L J ​ ‖ θ − θ ′ ‖ ​ ‖ F λ ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 ≤ L J ​ B F ​ ‖ θ − θ ′ ‖ L_{J}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\,\|F_{\lambda}(\theta)\|_{2}\leq L_{J}B_{F}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\| . For the second term, the mean value inequality gives ‖ F λ ​ ( θ ) − F λ ​ ( θ ′ ) ‖ 2 ≤ sup ξ ∈ 𝒮 λ ‖ J F λ ​ ( ξ ) ‖ ​ ‖ θ − θ ′ ‖ ≤ B J ​ ‖ θ − θ ′ ‖ \|F_{\lambda}(\theta)-F_{\lambda}(\theta^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}}\|J_{F_{\lambda}}(\xi)\|\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\leq B_{J}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\| , hence the second term is bounded by B J 2 ​ ‖ θ − θ ′ ‖ B_{J}^{2}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\| . Altogether, 

‖ ∇ L λ ​ ( θ ) − ∇ L λ ​ ( θ ′ ) ‖ ≤ ( B J 2 + L J ​ B F ) ​ ‖ θ − θ ′ ‖ ​ for all ​ θ , θ ′ ∈ 𝒮 λ . \|\nabla L_{\lambda}(\theta)-\nabla L_{\lambda}(\theta^{\prime})\|\leq(B_{J}^{2}+L_{J}B_{F})\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\text{ for all }\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}. 

Therefore L λ L_{\lambda} is β L λ \beta_{L_{\lambda}} -smooth on 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} with β L λ ≤ B J 2 + L J ​ B F \beta_{L_{\lambda}}\leq B_{J}^{2}+L_{J}B_{F} . Combining this with ( B.25 ) and the definition ( B.24 ) yields ( B.26 ). ∎ 

In the PDE-only case λ = 0 \lambda=0 , Proposition B.2 gives μ 0 ≍ n res − α \mu_{0}\asymp n_{\mathrm{res}}^{-\alpha} , so the PL \mathrm{PL} based condition number κ L 0 ​ ( 𝒮 ) \kappa_{L_{0}}(\mathcal{S}) grows like n res α n_{\mathrm{res}}^{\alpha} . In contrast, ( B.25 )–( B.26 ) show that if λ > ρ 2 / ( μ data ​ μ ⟂ ) \lambda>\rho^{2}/(\mu_{\mathrm{data}}\mu_{\perp}) is fixed independently of n res n_{\mathrm{res}} , then μ λ \mu_{\lambda} is bounded away from zero and κ L λ ​ ( 𝒮 ) \kappa_{L_{\lambda}}(\mathcal{S}) remains bounded as more residual points are added. 

Gradient descent convergence under uniform conditioning 

Consider gradient descent on L λ L_{\lambda} , 

θ k + 1 = θ k − η ​ ∇ L λ ​ ( θ k ) , \theta_{k+1}=\theta_{k}-\eta\,\nabla L_{\lambda}(\theta_{k}), 

with a fixed step size η ∈ ( 0 , 1 / β L λ ] \eta\in(0,1/\beta_{L_{\lambda}}] , and assume that the iterates stay in 𝒮 λ \mathcal{S}_{\lambda} . Under the setting of Theorem B.9 , the result of Liu et al. ( 2022 , Theorem 6) gives 

L λ ​ ( θ k + 1 ) − L λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ≤ ( 1 − η ​ μ λ ) ​ ( L λ ​ ( θ k ) − L λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ) . L_{\lambda}(\theta_{k+1})-L_{\lambda}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\;\leq\;\bigl(1-\eta\mu_{\lambda}\bigr)\,\bigl(L_{\lambda}(\theta_{k})-L_{\lambda}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\bigr). 

Choosing η = 1 / β L λ \eta=1/\beta_{L_{\lambda}} and using ( B.24 )–( B.26 ), we obtain 

L λ ​ ( θ k + 1 ) − L λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ≤ ( 1 − 1 κ L λ ​ ( 𝒮 ) ) ​ ( L λ ​ ( θ k ) − L λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ) . L_{\lambda}(\theta_{k+1})-L_{\lambda}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\;\leq\;\biggl(1-\frac{1}{\kappa_{L_{\lambda}}(\mathcal{S})}\biggr)\,\bigl(L_{\lambda}(\theta_{k})-L_{\lambda}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\bigr). 

Thus the number of iterations required to reach L λ ​ ( θ k ) − L λ ​ ( θ λ ⋆ ) ≤ ε L_{\lambda}(\theta_{k})-L_{\lambda}(\theta_{\lambda}^{\star})\leq\varepsilon is 

𝒪 ​ ( κ L λ ​ ( 𝒮 ) ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ) . \mathcal{O}\bigl(\kappa_{L_{\lambda}}(\mathcal{S})\,\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr). 

In particular, in virtue of Theorem B.9 , we see that this iteration complexity is bounded uniformly in n res n_{\mathrm{res}} when λ > 0 \lambda>0 is fixed. 

Appendix C Monte Carlo Feynman–Kac label noise 

This section provides the probabilistic error analysis for the Monte Carlo Feynman–Kac estimator used to construct the FK supervision labels in ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}} . Our goal is to justify Proposition 6.1 in the main text, namely that each FK datum can be written as a noisy point evaluation of the true solution u ⋆ u^{\star} with sub-exponential noise whose parameters do not depend on the number of FK points. 

Throughout this section, we fix a spatial point x ∈ Ω x\in\Omega and study the error of the Monte Carlo Feynman–Kac estimator at this point. All constants may depend on the model data (domain, coefficients, source and boundary terms) and, when a truncation horizon T max T_{\max} is present, on T max T_{\max} . They do not depend on the Monte Carlo sample size N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} and they do not depend on the time step Δ ​ t \Delta t once Δ ​ t \Delta t is restricted to be smaller than a suitable threshold. 

C.1 Setting and numerical scheme 

We recall the diffusion setting that underlies the Feynman–Kac representation. 

Let Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} be a bounded domain with C 2 C^{2} boundary ∂ Ω \partial\Omega . Let b : ℝ d → ℝ d b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d} and σ : ℝ d → ℝ d × m \sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times m} be globally Lipschitz functions. We assume that σ \sigma is uniformly elliptic, i.e. there exist constants 0 < λ ¯ ≤ λ ¯ < ∞ 0<\underline{\lambda}\leq\overline{\lambda}<\infty such that 

λ ¯ ​ | v | 2 ≤ v ⊤ ​ ( σ ​ σ ⊤ ) ​ ( x ) ​ v ≤ λ ¯ ​ | v | 2 , for all ​ x , v ∈ ℝ d . \underline{\lambda}\,\lvert v\rvert^{2}\;\leq\;v^{\top}(\sigma\sigma^{\top})(x)v\;\leq\;\overline{\lambda}\,\lvert v\rvert^{2},\ \text{ for all }x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}. 

Let ( W t ) t ≥ 0 (W_{t})_{t\geq 0} be an m m -dimensional Brownian motion on some filtered probability space ( Ω , ℱ , ( ℱ t ) t ≥ 0 , ℙ ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P}) . 

The diffusion process X = ( X t ) t ≥ 0 X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0} is the unique strong solution of 

d ​ X t = b ​ ( X t ) ​ d ​ t + σ ​ ( X t ) ​ d ​ W t , X 0 = x ∈ Ω . dX_{t}=b(X_{t})\,dt+\sigma(X_{t})\,dW_{t},\quad X_{0}=x\in\Omega. (C.1) 

We denote by 

τ := inf { t ≥ 0 : X t ∉ Ω } \tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\notin\Omega\} 

the first exit time of the diffusion from Ω \Omega , and assume τ < ∞ \tau<\infty almost surely for all starting points x ∈ Ω x\in\Omega . 

Let f : Ω ¯ → ℝ f:\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R} , g : ∂ Ω → ℝ g:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R} and V : Ω ¯ → ℝ V:\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R} be bounded measurable functions. In the main text these correspond to the source term, boundary data and zeroth-order potential of the elliptic boundary value problem. The associated (continuous-time) Feynman–Kac payoff is 

Y := ∫ 0 τ exp ⁡ ( − ∫ 0 t V ​ ( X s ) ​ 𝑑 s ) ​ f ​ ( X t ) ​ 𝑑 t + exp ⁡ ( − ∫ 0 τ V ​ ( X s ) ​ 𝑑 s ) ​ g ​ ( X τ ) . Y:=\int_{0}^{\tau}\exp\Bigl(-\int_{0}^{t}V(X_{s})\,ds\Bigr)\,f(X_{t})\,dt\;+\;\exp\Bigl(-\int_{0}^{\tau}V(X_{s})\,ds\Bigr)\,g(X_{\tau}). (C.2) 

Under standard regularity and well-posedness assumptions (Evans, 2022 ) , the elliptic boundary value problem admits a unique solution u ⋆ u^{\star} , and one has the Feynman–Kac representation 

u ⋆ ​ ( x ) = 𝔼 x ​ [ Y ] , u^{\star}(x)\;=\;\mathbb{E}^{x}[Y], 

where 𝔼 x \mathbb{E}^{x} denotes expectation for the diffusion ( C.1 ) started at X 0 = x X_{0}=x . 

For the numerical approximation we fix a time step Δ ​ t > 0 \Delta t>0 and define the Euler–Maruyama scheme ( X k Δ ​ t ) k ∈ ℕ 0 (X_{k}^{\Delta t})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}} by 

X 0 Δ ​ t = x , X k + 1 Δ ​ t = X k Δ ​ t + b ​ ( X k Δ ​ t ) ​ Δ ​ t + σ ​ ( X k Δ ​ t ) ​ Δ ​ W k , X_{0}^{\Delta t}=x,\quad X_{k+1}^{\Delta t}=X_{k}^{\Delta t}+b(X_{k}^{\Delta t})\,\Delta t+\sigma(X_{k}^{\Delta t})\,\Delta W_{k}, (C.3) 

where Δ ​ W k := W ( k + 1 ) ​ Δ ​ t − W k ​ Δ ​ t \Delta W_{k}:=W_{(k+1)\Delta t}-W_{k\Delta t} . The corresponding discrete exit time is 

τ Δ ​ t := inf { k ​ Δ ​ t : X k Δ ​ t ∉ Ω } , \tau_{\Delta t}:=\inf\{k\Delta t:X_{k}^{\Delta t}\notin\Omega\}, (C.4) 

with the convention inf ∅ = ∞ \inf\emptyset=\infty . Writing 

N Δ ​ t := inf { k ∈ ℕ 0 : X k Δ ​ t ∉ Ω } , N_{\Delta t}:=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:X_{k}^{\Delta t}\notin\Omega\}, 

we have τ Δ ​ t = N Δ ​ t ​ Δ ​ t \tau_{\Delta t}=N_{\Delta t}\Delta t . 

The discretized payoff functional is 

Y Δ ​ t := ∑ k = 0 N Δ ​ t − 1 exp ⁡ ( − Δ ​ t ​ ∑ j = 0 k − 1 V ​ ( X j Δ ​ t ) ) ​ f ​ ( X k Δ ​ t ) ​ Δ ​ t + exp ⁡ ( − Δ ​ t ​ ∑ j = 0 N Δ ​ t − 1 V ​ ( X j Δ ​ t ) ) ​ g ​ ( X N Δ ​ t Δ ​ t ) . Y^{\Delta t}:=\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\Delta t}-1}\exp\Bigl(-\Delta t\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}V(X_{j}^{\Delta t})\Bigr)f(X_{k}^{\Delta t})\,\Delta t\;+\;\exp\Bigl(-\Delta t\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\Delta t}-1}V(X_{j}^{\Delta t})\Bigr)g(X_{N_{\Delta t}}^{\Delta t}). (C.5) 

The associated time-discretized value at x x is 

u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) := 𝔼 x ​ [ Y Δ ​ t ] . u_{\Delta t}(x):=\mathbb{E}^{x}[Y^{\Delta t}]. 

Given N MC ∈ ℕ N_{\mathrm{MC}}\in\mathbb{N} , we consider i.i.d. copies ( Y Δ ​ t , ( i ) ) i = 1 N MC (Y^{\Delta t,(i)})_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{MC}}} of Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} and define the Monte Carlo estimator (for fixed x x and Δ ​ t \Delta t ) 

u ^ MC ​ ( x ) := 1 N MC ​ ∑ i = 1 N MC Y Δ ​ t , ( i ) . \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x):=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}Y^{\Delta t,(i)}. (C.6) 

In practice Algorithm 1 simulates trajectories until they exit Ω \Omega or until a truncation horizon T max ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ] T_{\max}\in(0,\infty] is reached. For T max = ∞ T_{\max}=\infty this reduces to the infinite horizon estimator u ^ MC ​ ( x ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x) defined above. For a finite truncation horizon we set N T := ⌊ T max / Δ ​ t ⌋ N_{T}:=\lfloor T_{\max}/\Delta t\rfloor and define the truncated discrete stopping index 

κ Δ ​ t T max := min ⁡ { N Δ ​ t , N T } , \kappa_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}:=\min\{N_{\Delta t},N_{T}\}, 

together with the truncated payoff 

Y Δ ​ t , T max := ∑ k = 0 κ Δ ​ t T max − 1 exp ⁡ ( − Δ ​ t ​ ∑ j = 0 k − 1 V ​ ( X j Δ ​ t ) ) ​ f ​ ( X k Δ ​ t ) ​ Δ ​ t + exp ⁡ ( − Δ ​ t ​ ∑ j = 0 κ Δ ​ t T max − 1 V ​ ( X j Δ ​ t ) ) ​ g ​ ( X κ Δ ​ t T max Δ ​ t ) . Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\kappa_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}-1}\exp\Bigl(-\Delta t\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}V(X_{j}^{\Delta t})\Bigr)f(X_{k}^{\Delta t})\,\Delta t\;+\;\exp\Bigl(-\Delta t\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}-1}V(X_{j}^{\Delta t})\Bigr)g(X_{\kappa_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}}^{\Delta t}). (C.7) 

We write u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) := 𝔼 x ​ [ Y Δ ​ t , T max ] u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x):=\mathbb{E}^{x}[Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}}] for the corresponding truncated value. For T max = ∞ T_{\max}=\infty we adopt the convention Y Δ ​ t , ∞ := Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t,\infty}:=Y^{\Delta t} and u Δ ​ t ∞ ​ ( x ) := u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) u_{\Delta t}^{\infty}(x):=u_{\Delta t}(x) so that the notation covers both finite and infinite horizons. Our aim is to control the tails of FK estimators of the form u ^ MC ​ ( x ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x) and of their truncated counterparts, and hence show that the resulting FK labels behave like noisy point evaluations of u ⋆ u^{\star} with sub-exponential noise. 

C.2 Exit time estimates and exponential moments 

We first establish exponential moments for the continuous exit time τ \tau and the discrete exit time τ Δ ​ t \tau_{\Delta t} . These are the key inputs for controlling the tails of the payoffs Y Y and Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} and hence the Monte Carlo error. 

C.2.1 Continuous exit time 
Theorem C.1 (Exponential moments of the continuous exit time) . 
Suppose that Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} is a bounded C 2 C^{2} domain and that the diffusion X X defined by ( C.1 ) has globally Lipschitz coefficients and is uniformly elliptic. Then there exist constants λ c > 0 \lambda_{c}>0 and C c < ∞ C_{c}<\infty such that 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ 𝔼 y ​ [ e λ c ​ τ ] ≤ C c . \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[e^{\lambda_{c}\tau}\big]\;\leq\;C_{c}. 

In particular, the law of τ \tau has exponential tails uniformly over starting points y ∈ Ω ¯ y\in\overline{\Omega} . 
Proof. 
Under these assumptions, the generator ℒ \mathcal{L} of X X with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions on ∂ Ω \partial\Omega admits a discrete spectrum with strictly positive first eigenvalue λ 1 > 0 \lambda_{1}>0 (Pinsky, 1995 ) . The killed semigroup ( P t Ω ) t ≥ 0 (P_{t}^{\Omega})_{t\geq 0} therefore satisfies 

∥ P t Ω ∥ L ∞ ​ ( Ω ) → L ∞ ​ ( Ω ) ≤ C ​ e − λ 1 ​ t , for all ​ t ≥ 0 \lVert P_{t}^{\Omega}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)\to L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq Ce^{-\lambda_{1}t},\ \text{ for all }t\geq 0 

for some constant C < ∞ C<\infty . It follows that 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ ℙ y ​ ( τ > t ) = sup y ∈ Ω ¯ P t Ω ​ 𝟏 ​ ( y ) ≤ C ​ e − λ 1 ​ t , for all ​ t ≥ 0 . \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau>t)=\sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}P_{t}^{\Omega}\mathbf{1}(y)\leq Ce^{-\lambda_{1}t},\ \text{ for all }t\geq 0. 

Fix any λ c ∈ ( 0 , λ 1 ) \lambda_{c}\in(0,\lambda_{1}) . Then, integrating the tail bound, 

𝔼 y ​ [ e λ c ​ τ ] = 1 + ∫ 0 ∞ λ c ​ e λ c ​ t ​ ℙ y ​ ( τ > t ) ​ 𝑑 t ≤ 1 + C ​ λ c ​ ∫ 0 ∞ e − ( λ 1 − λ c ) ​ t ​ 𝑑 t < ∞ , \mathbb{E}^{y}\big[e^{\lambda_{c}\tau}\big]=1+\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{c}e^{\lambda_{c}t}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau>t)\,dt\leq 1+C\lambda_{c}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{c})t}\,dt<\infty, 

with the bound uniform in y ∈ Ω ¯ y\in\overline{\Omega} . This proves the claim for a suitable constant C c C_{c} . ∎ 

C.2.2 Discrete exit time 

We next recall moment estimates for the discrete exit time τ Δ ​ t \tau_{\Delta t} . We rely on the results of Bouchard et al. ( 2017 ) . 
Theorem C.2 (Moments of continuous and discrete exit times) . 
Assume that Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} is a bounded C 2 C^{2} domain with non-characteristic boundary, and that the coefficients b , σ b,\sigma in ( C.1 ) are globally Lipschitz and of linear growth. Let τ \tau be as above and τ Δ ​ t \tau_{\Delta t} be defined by ( C.4 ). Then, for each integer p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 , there exist finite constants C p < ∞ C_{p}<\infty and Δ ​ t p > 0 \Delta t_{p}>0 such that for all 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t p 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{p} , 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ 𝔼 y ​ [ τ p ] \displaystyle\sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{E}^{y}[\tau^{p}] ≤ C p , \displaystyle\leq C_{p}, 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ 𝔼 y ​ [ τ Δ ​ t p ] \displaystyle\sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{E}^{y}[\tau_{\Delta t}^{p}] ≤ C p , \displaystyle\leq C_{p}, 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ 𝔼 y ​ [ | τ Δ ​ t − τ | p ] \displaystyle\sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[\lvert\tau_{\Delta t}-\tau\rvert^{p}\big] ≤ C p ​ Δ ​ t p / 2 . \displaystyle\leq C_{p}\,\Delta t^{p/2}. 
Proof. 
This is a specialization of the main results in Bouchard et al. ( 2017 ) . See in particular Theorems 3.1 there, specialized to globally Lipschitz coefficients and a bounded C 2 C^{2} domain. The non-characteristic boundary condition is implied by uniform ellipticity. Collecting the corresponding bounds yields the stated result. ∎ 

Combining Theorems C.1 and C.2 , one can show that τ Δ ​ t \tau_{\Delta t} admits exponential moments uniformly over Δ ​ t \Delta t small enough by a standard renewal argument. We sketch this in the next two lemmas. 
Lemma C.3 (Uniform exit in finite time) . 
Under the standing assumptions, there exist constants T ∗ > 0 T_{\ast}>0 and p 0 ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p_{0}\in(0,1) such that 

inf y ∈ Ω ¯ ℙ y ​ ( τ ≤ T ∗ ) ≥ p 0 . \inf_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau\leq T_{\ast})\;\geq\;p_{0}. 

Moreover, there exist Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 and p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p\in(0,1) such that for all 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} , 

inf y ∈ Ω ¯ ℙ y ​ ( τ Δ ​ t ≤ T ∗ ) ≥ p . \inf_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta t}\leq T_{\ast})\;\geq\;p. 
Proof sketch. 
For the continuous exit time, Theorem C.1 yields sup y ∈ Ω ¯ ℙ y ​ ( τ > t ) ≤ C c ​ e − λ c ​ t \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau>t)\leq C_{c}e^{-\lambda_{c}t} , hence, choosing T ∗ T_{\ast} large enough that C c ​ e − λ c ​ T ∗ ≤ 1 / 2 C_{c}e^{-\lambda_{c}T_{\ast}}\leq 1/2 gives 

inf y ∈ Ω ¯ ℙ y ( τ ≤ T ∗ ) ≥ 1 / 2 = : p 0 . \inf_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau\leq T_{\ast})\geq 1/2=:p_{0}. 

By Theorem C.2 , for some p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 we have sup y ∈ Ω ¯ 𝔼 y ​ [ | τ Δ ​ t − τ | p ] → 0 ​ as ​ Δ ​ t → 0 \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[|\tau_{\Delta t}-\tau|^{p}\big]\to 0\text{ as }\Delta t\to 0 , hence, for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ ℙ y ​ ( | τ Δ ​ t − τ | > ε ) ≤ ε − p ​ sup y 𝔼 y ​ [ | τ Δ ​ t − τ | p ] → 0 , \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{P}^{y}\big(|\tau_{\Delta t}-\tau|>\varepsilon\big)\leq\varepsilon^{-p}\sup_{y}\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[|\tau_{\Delta t}-\tau|^{p}\big]\to 0, 

so τ Δ ​ t → τ \tau_{\Delta t}\to\tau in probability, uniformly in y y . Since the law of τ \tau has a density (see, e.g., Pinsky ( 1995 ) ), we have ℙ y ​ ( τ = T ∗ ) = 0 \mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau=T_{\ast})=0 for all y y , and thus ℙ y ​ ( τ Δ ​ t ≤ T ∗ ) ⟶ ℙ y ​ ( τ ≤ T ∗ ) \mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta t}\leq T_{\ast})\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau\leq T_{\ast}) uniformly in y ∈ Ω ¯ y\in\overline{\Omega} as Δ ​ t → 0 \Delta t\to 0 . Hence for Δ ​ t \Delta t small enough we still have 

inf y ∈ Ω ¯ ℙ y ( τ Δ ​ t ≤ T ∗ ) ≥ p 0 / 2 = : p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) . \inf_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta t}\leq T_{\ast})\geq p_{0}/2=:p\in(0,1). 

∎ 
Proposition C.4 (Exponential moments of the discrete exit time) . 
Under the standing assumptions, there exist constants λ d > 0 \lambda_{d}>0 , C d < ∞ C_{d}<\infty and Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 such that 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ , 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 𝔼 y ​ [ e λ d ​ τ Δ ​ t ] ≤ C d . \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega},\,0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0}}\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[e^{\lambda_{d}\tau_{\Delta t}}\big]\;\leq\;C_{d}. 
Proof sketch. 
Let T ∗ > 0 T_{\ast}>0 , p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p\in(0,1) and Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 be as in Lemma C.3 . In particular, inf y ℙ y ​ ( τ Δ ​ t ≤ T ∗ ) ≥ p \inf_{y}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta}t\leq T_{\ast})\geq p , so sup y ℙ y ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > T ∗ ) ≤ 1 − p \sup_{y}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta}t>T_{\ast})\leq 1-p for all 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . 

Consider the continuous-time interpolation of the Euler scheme, which is a time-homogeneous Markov process. By the (strong) Markov property at times k ​ T ∗ kT_{\ast} , 

ℙ y ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > ( k + 1 ) ​ T ∗ ) = 𝔼 y ​ [ 𝟏 { τ Δ ​ t > k ​ T ∗ } ​ ℙ X Δ ​ t k ​ T ∗ ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > T ∗ ) ] ≤ ( 1 − p ) ​ ℙ y ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > k ​ T ∗ ) . \mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta t}>(k+1)T_{\ast})=\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{\Delta}t>kT_{\ast}\}}\mathbb{P}^{X^{\Delta}t_{kT_{\ast}}}(\tau_{\Delta}t>T_{\ast})\big]\leq(1-p)\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta}t>kT_{\ast}). 

Iterating this inequality gives sup y ∈ Ω ¯ ℙ y ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > k ​ T ∗ ) ≤ ( 1 − p ) k \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta t}>kT_{\ast})\leq(1-p)^{k} for all k ∈ ℕ , 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 k\in\mathbb{N},~0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . For any t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 with k ​ T ∗ ≤ t < ( k + 1 ) ​ T ∗ kT_{\ast}\leq t<(k+1)T_{\ast} , we have { τ Δ ​ t > t } ⊂ { τ Δ ​ t > k ​ T ∗ } \{\tau_{\Delta}t>t\}\subset\{\tau_{\Delta}t>kT_{\ast}\} , so the same bound holds with t t in place of k ​ T ∗ kT_{\ast} . 

Proceeding as in the proof of Theorem C.1 , we use the representation 𝔼 y ​ [ e λ ​ τ Δ ​ t ] = 1 + ∫ 0 ∞ λ ​ e λ ​ t ​ ℙ y ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > t ) ​ 𝑑 t \mathbb{E}^{y}[e^{\lambda\tau_{\Delta}t}]=1+\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda e^{\lambda t}\mathbb{P}^{y}(\tau_{\Delta}t>t)\,dt , and split the integral over the intervals [ k ​ T ∗ , ( k + 1 ) ​ T ∗ ) [kT_{\ast},(k+1)T_{\ast}) , yielding a geometric series upper bound by the previous paragraph. 

Choosing λ d > 0 \lambda_{d}>0 so small that e λ d ​ T ∗ ​ ( 1 − p ) < 1 e^{\lambda_{d}T_{\ast}}(1-p)<1 , the series in question converges and its sum is finite and independent of y y and Δ ​ t ∈ ( 0 , Δ ​ t 0 ] \Delta t\in(0,\Delta t_{0}] . This gives the claimed bound with some finite constant C d C_{d} . ∎ 

C.3 Exponential moments and tails for the payoff 

We now transfer the exponential-moment bounds for τ \tau and τ Δ ​ t \tau_{\Delta t} to the payoffs Y Y and Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} . 
Lemma C.5 (Deterministic bound for Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} ) . 
Assume that f f and g g are bounded and that V ≥ 0 V\geq 0 on Ω ¯ \overline{\Omega} . Then there exist constants C f , C g ≥ 0 C_{f},C_{g}\geq 0 such that respectively for all x ∈ Ω ¯ x\in\overline{\Omega} , z ∈ ∂ Ω z\in\partial\Omega , we have | f ​ ( x ) | ≤ C f \lvert f(x)\rvert\leq C_{f} , | g ​ ( z ) | ≤ C g \lvert g(z)\rvert\leq C_{g} , and 

| Y Δ ​ t | ≤ C f ​ τ Δ ​ t + C g ​ almost surely . \lvert Y^{\Delta t}\rvert\leq C_{f}\tau_{\Delta t}+C_{g}\ \text{almost surely}. 
Proof. 
By boundedness of f f and g g there exist C f , C g ≥ 0 C_{f},C_{g}\geq 0 with the stated properties. Since V ≥ 0 V\geq 0 , all exponential factors in ( C.5 ) are bounded by one. Thus 

| Y Δ ​ t | \displaystyle\lvert Y^{\Delta t}\rvert ≤ ∑ k = 0 N Δ ​ t − 1 | f ​ ( X k Δ ​ t ) | ​ Δ ​ t + | g ​ ( X N Δ ​ t Δ ​ t ) | \displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\Delta t}-1}\lvert f(X_{k}^{\Delta t})\rvert\,\Delta t+\lvert g(X_{N_{\Delta t}}^{\Delta t})\rvert 

≤ C f ​ N Δ ​ t ​ Δ ​ t + C g = C f ​ τ Δ ​ t + C g . \displaystyle\leq C_{f}N_{\Delta t}\Delta t+C_{g}=C_{f}\tau_{\Delta t}+C_{g}. 

∎ 

A similar argument yields a deterministic bound for Y Y in terms of τ \tau : 
Lemma C.6 (Deterministic bound for Y Y ) . 
Under the same assumptions as in Lemma C.5 , one has 

| Y | ≤ C f ​ τ + C g ​ almost surely . \lvert Y\rvert\leq C_{f}\tau+C_{g}\ \text{almost surely}. 

Combining these bounds with Theorem C.1 and Proposition C.4 , we obtain exponential moments for the payoffs. 
Proposition C.7 (Exponential moments for Y Y and Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} ) . 
Under the standing assumptions and assuming in addition that V ≥ 0 V\geq 0 on Ω ¯ \overline{\Omega} , there exist constants λ Y > 0 \lambda_{Y}>0 , C Y < ∞ C_{Y}<\infty and Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 such that 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ 𝔼 y ​ [ e λ Y ​ | Y | ] ≤ C Y , \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega}}\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[e^{\lambda_{Y}\lvert Y\rvert}\big]\;\leq\;C_{Y}, 

and 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ , 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 𝔼 y ​ [ e λ Y ​ | Y Δ ​ t | ] ≤ C Y . \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega},\,0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0}}\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[e^{\lambda_{Y}\lvert Y^{\Delta t}\rvert}\big]\;\leq\;C_{Y}. 
Proof. 
By Lemma C.5 , 

e λ ​ | Y Δ ​ t | ≤ e λ ​ C g ​ e λ ​ C f ​ τ Δ ​ t , e^{\lambda\lvert Y^{\Delta t}\rvert}\leq e^{\lambda C_{g}}e^{\lambda C_{f}\tau_{\Delta t}}, 

and by Lemma C.6 , 

e λ ​ | Y | ≤ e λ ​ C g ​ e λ ​ C f ​ τ . e^{\lambda\lvert Y\rvert}\leq e^{\lambda C_{g}}e^{\lambda C_{f}\tau}. 

Using Theorem C.1 and Proposition C.4 , choose λ Y > 0 \lambda_{Y}>0 small enough that λ Y ​ C f < λ c \lambda_{Y}C_{f}<\lambda_{c} and λ Y ​ C f < λ d \lambda_{Y}C_{f}<\lambda_{d} . Then the stated bounds follow immediately, with 

C Y := e λ Y ​ C g ​ max ⁡ { C c , C d } . C_{Y}:=e^{\lambda_{Y}C_{g}}\max\{C_{c},C_{d}\}. 

∎ 
Remark C.8 (Sub-exponential character of Y Y and Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} ) . 
A real-valued random variable Z Z is sub-exponential if there exists α > 0 \alpha>0 with 𝔼 ​ [ e λ ​ Z ] < ∞ \mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda Z}]<\infty for all | λ | < α |\lambda|<\alpha . Proposition C.7 shows that Y Y and Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} are sub-exponential, with sub-exponential norms bounded uniformly in Δ ​ t \Delta t for Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . 

C.4 Concentration for the Monte Carlo estimator at fixed Δ ​ t \Delta t 

We now derive non-asymptotic tail bounds for the Monte Carlo estimator u ^ MC ​ ( x ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x) at a fixed time step Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . The key tool is a Bernstein-type inequality for sub-exponential random variables. 

C.4.1 A Bernstein inequality for sub-exponential variables 
Theorem C.9 (Bernstein inequality for sub-exponential variables) . 
Let Z 1 , … , Z N Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N} be i.i.d. real-valued random variables with 𝔼 ​ [ Z i ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[Z_{i}]=0 and suppose there exist constants ν 2 , b > 0 \nu^{2},b>0 satisfying 

𝔼 ​ [ e λ ​ Z i ] ≤ exp ⁡ ( ν 2 ​ λ 2 2 ) ​ for all ​ | λ | < 1 / b . \mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda Z_{i}}\big]\leq\exp\Bigl(\frac{\nu^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\Bigr)\ \text{ for all }\lvert\lambda\rvert<1/b. 

Then there exists a universal constant c > 0 c>0 such that, for all t > 0 t>0 , 

ℙ ​ ( | 1 N ​ ∑ i = 1 N Z i | > t ) ≤ 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − c ​ N ​ min ⁡ ( t 2 ν 2 , t b ) ) . \mathbb{P}\Big(\Big\lvert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}Z_{i}\Big\rvert>t\Big)\leq 2\exp\Big(-cN\min\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{\nu^{2}},\frac{t}{b}\Big)\Big). 
Proof. 
See, for example, (Vershynin, 2018 , Theorem 2.9.1) . ∎ 

C.4.2 Application to the Euler payoff 

We apply Theorem C.9 to the centered Euler payoff Y Δ ​ t − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) Y^{\Delta t}-u_{\Delta t}(x) . 
Lemma C.10 (Sub-exponential parameters for Y Δ ​ t − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) Y^{\Delta t}-u_{\Delta t}(x) ) . 
Under the standing assumptions, there exist constants ν > 0 \nu>0 , b > 0 b>0 and Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 such that, for all Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} , the centered random variable Z Δ ​ t := Y Δ ​ t − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) Z^{\Delta t}:=Y^{\Delta t}-u_{\Delta t}(x) satisfies 

𝔼 ​ [ e λ ​ Z Δ ​ t ] ≤ exp ⁡ ( ν 2 ​ λ 2 2 ) ​ for all ​ | λ | < 1 / b . \mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda Z^{\Delta t}}\big]\leq\exp\Bigl(\frac{\nu^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\Bigr)\ \text{for all }\lvert\lambda\rvert<1/b. 

The constants ν \nu and b b depend only on the model data and can be chosen independent of Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . 
Proof. 
Proposition C.7 guarantees that there exist λ Y > 0 \lambda_{Y}>0 and C Y < ∞ C_{Y}<\infty such that 

sup 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 𝔼 ​ [ e λ Y ​ | Y Δ ​ t | ] ≤ C Y . \sup_{0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda_{Y}\lvert Y^{\Delta t}\rvert}\big]\leq C_{Y}. 

This implies that the moment generating function 𝔼 ​ [ e λ ​ Y Δ ​ t ] \mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda Y^{\Delta t}}] is finite on ( − λ Y , λ Y ) (-\lambda_{Y},\lambda_{Y}) uniformly in Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . Since Z Δ ​ t = Y Δ ​ t − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) Z^{\Delta t}=Y^{\Delta t}-u_{\Delta t}(x) differs from Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} by a constant shift, the same holds for Z Δ ​ t Z^{\Delta t} . 

A standard argument (see Vershynin ( 2018 , Section 2.7) ) shows that there exist parameters ( ν , b ) (\nu,b) depending only on the exponential-moment bounds of Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} such that 

𝔼 ​ [ e λ ​ Z Δ ​ t ] ≤ exp ⁡ ( ν 2 ​ λ 2 2 ) ​ for all ​ | λ | < 1 / b , \mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda Z^{\Delta t}}\big]\leq\exp\Bigl(\frac{\nu^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\Bigr)\ \text{for all }\lvert\lambda\rvert<1/b, 

uniformly over Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . We refer the reader to said reference for details. ∎ 

Combining Lemma C.10 with Theorem C.9 yields the following concentration inequality. 
Proposition C.11 (Concentration for the Monte Carlo error at fixed Δ ​ t \Delta t ) . 
Under the standing assumptions, there exist constants c > 0 c>0 , ν > 0 \nu>0 , b > 0 b>0 and Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 such that for all Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} , all N MC ∈ ℕ N_{\mathrm{MC}}\in\mathbb{N} and all t > 0 t>0 , 

ℙ ​ ( | u ^ MC ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) | > t ) ≤ 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − c ​ N MC ​ min ⁡ ( t 2 ν 2 , t b ) ) . \mathbb{P}\Big(\lvert\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x)-u_{\Delta t}(x)\rvert>t\Big)\leq 2\exp\Big(-cN_{\mathrm{MC}}\min\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{\nu^{2}},\frac{t}{b}\Big)\Big). 
Proof. 
For fixed x x , Δ ​ t \Delta t and N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} , define Z i Δ ​ t := Y Δ ​ t , ( i ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) Z^{\Delta t}_{i}:=Y^{\Delta t,(i)}-u_{\Delta t}(x) for i = 1 , … , N MC i=1,\dots,N_{\mathrm{MC}} . These variables are i.i.d. with zero mean, and by Lemma C.10 they satisfy the moment generating function condition of Theorem C.9 with parameters ( ν , b ) (\nu,b) independent of Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . Since 

u ^ MC ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) = 1 N MC ​ ∑ i = 1 N MC Z i Δ ​ t , \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x)-u_{\Delta t}(x)=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}Z^{\Delta t}_{i}, 

Theorem C.9 yields the stated inequality. ∎ 
Remark C.12 (sub-Gaussian vs exponential regimes) . 
The bound in Proposition C.11 has two regimes. For deviations 0 < t ≤ ν 2 / b 0<t\leq\nu^{2}/b , the quadratic term dominates and the tails behave essentially like exp ⁡ ( − c ​ N MC ​ t 2 / ν 2 ) \exp(-cN_{\mathrm{MC}}t^{2}/\nu^{2}) . For larger deviations, the linear term dominates and the decay is of order exp ⁡ ( − c ​ N MC ​ t / b ) \exp(-cN_{\mathrm{MC}}t/b) . In both cases the constants are independent of N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} and of Δ ​ t \Delta t for Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . 

C.5 Discretization bias 

We next bound the bias u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x ) u_{\Delta t}(x)-u^{\star}(x) of the time-discretized FK value. 
Theorem C.13 (Weak error for killed Euler schemes) . 
Assume that Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} is a bounded C 2 C^{2} domain with non-characteristic boundary, that the coefficients b , σ b,\sigma in ( C.1 ) are C b 4 C_{b}^{4} with bounded derivatives up to order 4 4 , and that f , g , V f,g,V are bounded with bounded derivatives up to the necessary order. Then there exist constants C bias < ∞ C_{\mathrm{bias}}<\infty and Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 such that, for all 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} , 

| u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x ) | ≤ C bias ​ Δ ​ t . \lvert u_{\Delta t}(x)-u^{\star}(x)\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{bias}}\,\sqrt{\Delta t}. 
Proof. 
This is a specialization of the weak error estimates for killed Euler schemes in (Gobet, 2000 , Theorem 2.4) and (Gobet and Menozzi, 2010 , Theorem 17) . Our assumptions on the regularity of b , σ , f , g , V b,\sigma,f,g,V and on the geometry of Ω \Omega are stronger than those in the cited papers, so their hypotheses are satisfied. The functional Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} in ( C.5 ) matches the discretization considered there. The stated bound follows, with a constant C bias C_{\mathrm{bias}} independent of Δ ​ t \Delta t for Δ ​ t \Delta t small enough. ∎ 
Lemma C.14 (Bias due to time truncation) . 
Assume the standing assumptions of this appendix and those of Lemma C.5 . Then there exist constants λ ~ > 0 \tilde{\lambda}>0 , C T < ∞ C_{T}<\infty and Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 such that, for all x ∈ Ω x\in\Omega , all 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} and all T max > 0 T_{\max}>0 , 

| u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) | ≤ C T ​ e − λ ~ ​ T max . \lvert u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x)-u_{\Delta t}(x)\rvert\leq C_{T}e^{-\tilde{\lambda}T_{\max}}. 
Proof. 
On the event { τ Δ ​ t ≤ T max } \{\tau_{\Delta t}\leq T_{\max}\} the discrete trajectory exits Ω \Omega before the truncation horizon and κ Δ ​ t T max = N Δ ​ t \kappa_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}=N_{\Delta t} , hence Y Δ ​ t , T max = Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}}=Y^{\Delta t} . On the complement { τ Δ ​ t > T max } \{\tau_{\Delta t}>T_{\max}\} the payoffs Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t} and Y Δ ​ t , T max Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}} differ only through the contribution of the running functional f f after time T max T_{\max} and through the terminal term that involves g g . Using the representation ( C.5 ) and the definition ( C.7 ), together with boundedness of f f and g g and the fact that all exponential factors are at most one, we obtain 

| Y Δ ​ t − Y Δ ​ t , T max | ≤ C f ​ τ Δ ​ t ​ 𝟏 { τ Δ ​ t > T max } + 2 ​ C g ​ 𝟏 { τ Δ ​ t > T max } , \lvert Y^{\Delta t}-Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}}\rvert\leq C_{f}\tau_{\Delta t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{\Delta t}>T_{\max}\}}+2C_{g}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{\Delta t}>T_{\max}\}}, 

with the constants C f C_{f} and C g C_{g} from Lemma C.5 . Taking expectations and using Proposition C.4 , for any λ ∈ ( 0 , λ d ) \lambda\in(0,\lambda_{d}) , 

ℙ x ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > t ) ≤ e − λ ​ t ​ 𝔼 x ​ [ e λ ​ τ Δ ​ t ] ≤ C d ​ e − λ ​ t , \mathbb{P}^{x}(\tau_{\Delta t}>t)\leq e^{-\lambda t}\mathbb{E}^{x}\big[e^{\lambda\tau_{\Delta t}}\big]\leq C_{d}e^{-\lambda t}, 

and therefore 

𝔼 x ​ [ τ Δ ​ t ​ 𝟏 { τ Δ ​ t > T max } ] = ∫ T max ∞ ℙ x ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > u ) ​ 𝑑 u ≤ C d λ ​ e − λ ​ T max , \mathbb{E}^{x}\big[\tau_{\Delta t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{\Delta t}>T_{\max}\}}\big]=\int_{T_{\max}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{x}(\tau_{\Delta t}>u)\,du\leq\frac{C_{d}}{\lambda}e^{-\lambda T_{\max}}, 

as well as 

ℙ x ​ ( τ Δ ​ t > T max ) ≤ C d ​ e − λ ​ T max . \mathbb{P}^{x}(\tau_{\Delta t}>T_{\max})\leq C_{d}e^{-\lambda T_{\max}}. 

Combining these bounds yields 

| u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) | = | 𝔼 x ​ [ Y Δ ​ t , T max − Y Δ ​ t ] | ≤ ( C f ​ C d λ + 2 ​ C g ​ C d ) ​ e − λ ​ T max . \lvert u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x)-u_{\Delta t}(x)\rvert=\lvert\mathbb{E}^{x}\big[Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}}-Y^{\Delta t}\big]\rvert\leq\Bigl(\frac{C_{f}C_{d}}{\lambda}+2C_{g}C_{d}\Bigr)e^{-\lambda T_{\max}}. 

The claim follows by setting λ ~ := λ \tilde{\lambda}:=\lambda and C T := C f ​ C d λ + 2 ​ C g ​ C d C_{T}:=\frac{C_{f}C_{d}}{\lambda}+2C_{g}C_{d} , and by choosing Δ ​ t 0 \Delta t_{0} as in Proposition C.4 . ∎ 

These bounds summarize the non-asymptotic behavior of the infinite horizon Monte Carlo FK estimator at a fixed point x x . For the learning-theoretic analysis we only need that, for each fixed x x , the FK label produced by Algorithm 1 behaves like a noisy point evaluation of u ⋆ ​ ( x ) u^{\star}(x) with sub-exponential noise whose parameters do not depend on the number of FK labels or on the Monte Carlo budget. This is precisely the content of Proposition 6.1 in the main text, whose proof we now provide. 
Proof of Proposition 6.1 . 
Fix x ∈ Ω x\in\Omega and consider the FK estimator produced at this spatial point by Algorithm 1, with time step 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} , truncation horizon T max ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ] T_{\max}\in(0,\infty] and Monte Carlo budget N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} . For each trajectory we simulate a payoff Y Δ ​ t , T max Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}} as in ( C.7 ). The FK label at x x is the empirical average of N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} independent copies of Y Δ ​ t , T max Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}} , that is 

u ^ T max ​ ( x ) := 1 N MC ​ ∑ i = 1 N MC Y Δ ​ t , T max , ( i ) . \widehat{u}^{T_{\max}}(x):=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max},(i)}. 

Step 1: sub-exponential Monte Carlo fluctuations. 

We distinguish the cases T max < ∞ T_{\max}<\infty and T max = ∞ T_{\max}=\infty . 

If T max < ∞ T_{\max}<\infty , then at most N T = ⌊ T max / Δ ​ t ⌋ N_{T}=\lfloor T_{\max}/\Delta t\rfloor time steps are accumulated before truncation. Boundedness of f f and g g and nonnegativity of V V imply (as in Lemma C.5 ) the deterministic bound 

| Y Δ ​ t , T max | ≤ C f ​ T max + C g ​ almost surely, \lvert Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}}\rvert\leq C_{f}T_{\max}+C_{g}\ \text{ almost surely,} 

uniformly in x x and 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . Hence, for each fixed x x , the centered variable 

Z Δ ​ t , T max := Y Δ ​ t , T max − u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) Z^{\Delta t,T_{\max}}:=Y^{\Delta t,T_{\max}}-u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x) 

is bounded and therefore sub-exponential, with parameters that depend only on C f C_{f} , C g C_{g} and T max T_{\max} and do not depend on N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} or on the number of FK points. Standard properties of sub-exponential variables (see, e.g., Vershynin ( 2018 , Section 2.7) ) imply that empirical averages of i.i.d. sub-exponential variables are again sub-exponential, and the sub-exponential parameters can be chosen independently of the sample size. Thus, conditional on X 0 = x X_{0}=x , 

u ^ T max ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) = 1 N MC ​ ∑ i = 1 N MC Z Δ ​ t , T max , ( i ) \widehat{u}^{T_{\max}}(x)-u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x)=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{MC}}}Z^{\Delta t,T_{\max},(i)} 

is mean-zero and sub-exponential with parameters depending only on the problem data and on T max T_{\max} , and not on N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} . 

If T max = ∞ T_{\max}=\infty , then Y Δ ​ t , ∞ = Y Δ ​ t Y^{\Delta t,\infty}=Y^{\Delta t} and u Δ ​ t ∞ ​ ( x ) = u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) u_{\Delta t}^{\infty}(x)=u_{\Delta t}(x) . Proposition C.7 yields constants λ Y > 0 \lambda_{Y}>0 , C Y < ∞ C_{Y}<\infty and Δ ​ t 0 > 0 \Delta t_{0}>0 such that 

sup y ∈ Ω ¯ , 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 𝔼 y ​ [ e λ Y ​ | Y Δ ​ t | ] ≤ C Y . \sup_{y\in\overline{\Omega},\,0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0}}\mathbb{E}^{y}\big[e^{\lambda_{Y}\lvert Y^{\Delta t}\rvert}\big]\;\leq\;C_{Y}. 

In particular, for each fixed x x and 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} , the centered variable 

Z Δ ​ t := Y Δ ​ t − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) Z^{\Delta t}:=Y^{\Delta t}-u_{\Delta t}(x) 

is sub-exponential with parameters depending only on C Y C_{Y} and λ Y \lambda_{Y} and independent of Δ ​ t \Delta t , N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} and the number of FK points, as per Lemma C.10 . As in the finite-horizon case, empirical averages of i.i.d. copies of Z Δ ​ t Z^{\Delta t} are again sub-exponential, with parameters that can be chosen independent of N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} . Thus u ^ ∞ ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) \widehat{u}^{\infty}(x)-u_{\Delta t}(x) is mean-zero and sub-exponential with parameters depending only on the model data. 

In both cases we may therefore write 

u ^ T max ​ ( x ) = u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) + ζ ​ ( x ) , \widehat{u}^{T_{\max}}(x)=u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x)+\zeta(x), 

where, conditional on X 0 = x X_{0}=x , the fluctuation ζ ​ ( x ) \zeta(x) is mean-zero and sub-exponential with parameters that do not depend on N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} or on the number of FK points (and are uniform over 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} ). 

Step 2: discretization and truncation bias. 

We now decompose the bias of the (possibly truncated) FK value. Adding and subtracting u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) u_{\Delta t}(x) and u ⋆ ​ ( x ) u^{\star}(x) yields 

u ^ T max ​ ( x ) = u ⋆ ​ ( x ) + ( u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x ) ) + ( u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) ) + ζ ​ ( x ) . \widehat{u}^{T_{\max}}(x)=u^{\star}(x)+\bigl(u_{\Delta t}(x)-u^{\star}(x)\bigr)+\bigl(u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x)-u_{\Delta t}(x)\bigr)+\zeta(x). 

By Theorem C.13 the discretization bias satisfies 

| u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x ) | ≤ C bias ​ Δ ​ t , for all ​ 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 , \lvert u_{\Delta t}(x)-u^{\star}(x)\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t},\ \text{ for all }0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0}, 

while Lemma C.14 gives 

| u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) | ≤ C T ​ e − λ ~ ​ T max , for all ​ T max > 0 , \lvert u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x)-u_{\Delta t}(x)\rvert\leq C_{T}e^{-\tilde{\lambda}T_{\max}},\ \text{for all }T_{\max}>0, 

with the convention e − λ ~ ​ T max = 0 e^{-\tilde{\lambda}T_{\max}}=0 when T max = ∞ T_{\max}=\infty . Defining 

b ​ ( x ) := ( u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x ) ) + ( u Δ ​ t T max ​ ( x ) − u Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) ) , b(x):=\bigl(u_{\Delta t}(x)-u^{\star}(x)\bigr)+\bigl(u_{\Delta t}^{T_{\max}}(x)-u_{\Delta t}(x)\bigr), 

we obtain the decomposition 

u ^ T max ​ ( x ) = u ⋆ ​ ( x ) + b ​ ( x ) + ζ ​ ( x ) , \widehat{u}^{T_{\max}}(x)=u^{\star}(x)+b(x)+\zeta(x), 

with 

| b ​ ( x ) | ≤ C bias ​ Δ ​ t + C T ​ e − λ ~ ​ T max , \lvert b(x)\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t}+C_{T}e^{-\tilde{\lambda}T_{\max}}, 

and with ζ ​ ( x ) \zeta(x) mean-zero and sub-exponential with parameters independent of N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} and of the number of FK points, uniformly over 0 < Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t 0 0<\Delta t\leq\Delta t_{0} . 

Step 3: random FK locations. In the FK-PINN construction we draw i.i.d. supervision locations X i FK ∈ Ω X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}\in\Omega , and at each such point we compute an independent estimator u ^ T max ​ ( X i FK ) \widehat{u}^{T_{\max}}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}) using a fresh set of N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} trajectories. Setting 

Y i FK := u ^ T max ​ ( X i FK ) , b ​ ( X i FK ) := b ​ ( X i FK ) , ζ i := ζ ​ ( X i FK ) , Y_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}:=\widehat{u}^{T_{\max}}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}),\quad b(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}):=b\bigl(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}\bigr),\quad\zeta_{i}:=\zeta\bigl(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}\bigr), 

the pairs { ( X i FK , Y i FK ) } i = 1 N FK \{(X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}},Y_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}})\}_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}} are i.i.d., and, conditional on X i FK = x X_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}=x , each Y i FK Y_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}} admits the decomposition 

Y i FK = u ⋆ ​ ( x ) + b ​ ( x ) + ζ i , Y_{i}^{\mathrm{FK}}=u^{\star}(x)+b(x)+\zeta_{i}, 

with | b ​ ( x ) | ≤ C bias ​ Δ ​ t + C T ​ e − λ ~ ​ T max \lvert b(x)\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t}+C_{T}e^{-\tilde{\lambda}T_{\max}} and with ζ i \zeta_{i} mean-zero and sub-exponential with parameters that do not depend on N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} or N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} . This is exactly the statement in Proposition 6.1 , thus we are done. 

Appendix D Error Analysis for FK-PINNs 

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 6.2 . To this end, we will develop a comprehensive error analysis, based on a decomposition of the L 2 ​ ( Ω ) L^{2}(\Omega) error in three terms, which we will control separately through learning-theoretic arguments. 

D.1 Preliminaries 

We begin by recalling the setting in which we will be working together with notations and technical assumptions that we will be working under for the remainder of this section. 

D.1.1 Notations 

We work on a bounded Lipschitz domain Ω ⊂ [ 0 , 1 ] d \Omega\subset[0,1]^{d} with boundary ∂ Ω \partial\Omega and outward unit normal ν \nu . Integration with respect to Lebesgue measure on Ω \Omega is written as ∫ Ω ⋅ d ​ x \int_{\Omega}\cdot\,dx , while ∫ ∂ Ω ⋅ d ​ s \int_{\partial\Omega}\cdot\,ds denotes integration with respect to surface measure on ∂ Ω \partial\Omega . 

For 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ 1\leq p\leq\infty we write L p ​ ( Ω ) L^{p}(\Omega) and L p ​ ( ∂ Ω ) L^{p}(\partial\Omega) for the usual Lebesgue spaces, with norms 

∥ u ∥ L p ​ ( Ω ) := { ( ∫ Ω | u ​ ( x ) | p ​ 𝑑 x ) 1 / p , 1 ≤ p < ∞ , ess ​ sup x ∈ Ω ⁡ | u ​ ( x ) | , p = ∞ , \lVert u\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}:=\begin{cases}\bigl(\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,dx\bigr)^{1/p},&1\leq p<\infty,\\[1.99997pt] \operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{x\in\Omega}|u(x)|,&p=\infty,\end{cases} 

and similarly on ∂ Ω \partial\Omega . We write C ​ ( Ω ¯ ) C(\overline{\Omega}) for the space of continuous functions on the closure of Ω \Omega with norm ∥ u ∥ C ​ ( Ω ¯ ) := sup x ∈ Ω ¯ | u ​ ( x ) | \lVert u\rVert_{C(\overline{\Omega})}:=\sup_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}|u(x)| . 

For s ∈ ℕ s\in\mathbb{N} and 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ 1\leq p\leq\infty , the Sobolev space W s , p ​ ( Ω ) W^{s,p}(\Omega) consists of functions in L p ​ ( Ω ) L^{p}(\Omega) whose weak derivatives up to order s s lie in L p ​ ( Ω ) L^{p}(\Omega) , endowed with norm 

∥ u ∥ W s , p ​ ( Ω ) := ( ∑ | α | ≤ s ∥ D α ​ u ∥ L p ​ ( Ω ) p ) 1 / p for ​ p < ∞ , \lVert u\rVert_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}:=\biggl(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq s}\lVert D^{\alpha}u\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}\biggr)^{1/p}\quad\text{for }p<\infty, 

and with the obvious modification for p = ∞ p=\infty . We use H s ​ ( Ω ) := W s , 2 ​ ( Ω ) H^{s}(\Omega):=W^{s,2}(\Omega) . Fractional Sobolev spaces H s ​ ( Ω ) H^{s}(\Omega) for s ∉ ℕ s\notin\mathbb{N} are defined in the standard way, for instance via real interpolation or via the Gagliardo seminorm, see Evans ( 2022 ) . We denote the associated norms by ∥ ⋅ ∥ H s ​ ( Ω ) \lVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{s}(\Omega)} . 

Throughout, ⟨ ⋅ ⟩ ⋅ \langle\cdot\rangle{\cdot} denotes the L 2 ​ ( Ω ) L^{2}(\Omega) inner product, and ∥ ⋅ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) \lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)} is abbreviated to ∥ ⋅ ∥ 2 \lVert\cdot\rVert_{2} when the domain is clear from the context. For random variables we write 𝔼 ​ [ ⋅ ] \mathbb{E}[\cdot] for expectation and ℙ ​ ( ⋅ ) \mathbb{P}(\cdot) for probabilities. 

D.1.2 Setting and Assumptions 

We recall the PDE from Section 2.1 . Throughout this appendix we work in the elliptic setting for clarity. All statements are formulated and proved for the second order elliptic problem in ( 2.1 )–( 2.2 ). The same learning theoretic arguments extend to parabolic problems that admit a Feynman–Kac representation, once the time variable has been discretized and the resulting time discretization error is controlled separately. Since this would require additional notation and is orthogonal to the main message of the paper, we do not pursue the parabolic case in detail here. 
Assumption D.1 (PDE well posedness and FK representation) . 
The operator ℒ \mathcal{L} in ( 2.1 ) is uniformly elliptic with bounded measurable coefficients and satisfies the structural conditions stated in Section 2.1 . The data f f and g g are such that ( 2.1 )–( 2.2 ) admit a unique strong solution 

u ⋆ ∈ H 2 ​ ( Ω ) ∩ C ​ ( Ω ¯ ) , u^{\star}\in H^{2}(\Omega)\cap C(\overline{\Omega}), 

with u ⋆ | ∂ Ω = g u^{\star}|_{\partial\Omega}=g . Moreover, there exists a diffusion process ( X t ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t})_{t\geq 0} and an exit time τ \tau such that u ⋆ u^{\star} admits a Feynman–Kac representation of the form 

u ⋆ ​ ( x ) = 𝔼 x ​ [ ∫ 0 τ r ​ ( X t ) ​ 𝑑 t + h ​ ( X τ ) ] , x ∈ Ω , u^{\star}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigl[\int_{0}^{\tau}r(X_{t})\,dt+h(X_{\tau})\Bigr],\quad x\in\Omega, 

for suitable running and terminal functionals r r and h h , as described in Section 3 and Appendix A . 

We follow the sampling scheme described in Algorithm 1 and Algorithm 2 . Let 

X int ∼ U ​ ( Ω ) , X ∂ Ω ∼ U ​ ( ∂ Ω ) , X^{\mathrm{int}}\sim U(\Omega),\qquad X^{\partial\Omega}\sim U(\partial\Omega), 

be interior and boundary points drawn independently from the uniform distributions on Ω \Omega and ∂ Ω \partial\Omega . The corresponding population PDE and boundary risks are 

ℛ PDE ​ ( u ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u) := | Ω | ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | ℒ ​ u ​ ( X int ) − f ​ ( X int ) | 2 ] , \displaystyle:=\lvert\Omega\rvert\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl|\mathcal{L}u(X^{\mathrm{int}})-f(X^{\mathrm{int}})\bigr|^{2}\bigr], (D.1) 

ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(u) := | ∂ Ω | ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | u ​ ( X ∂ Ω ) − g ​ ( X ∂ Ω ) | 2 ] . \displaystyle:=\lvert\partial\Omega\rvert\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl|u(X^{\partial\Omega})-g(X^{\partial\Omega})\bigr|^{2}\bigr]. (D.2) 

In the FK term we work in a data scarce regime. We sample FK points from the same underlying geometry as the interior points, but we keep their number small. Concretely, we assume 

X FK ∼ U ​ ( Ω ) , X^{\mathrm{FK}}\sim U(\Omega), 

independently of X int X^{\mathrm{int}} and X ∂ Ω X^{\partial\Omega} , while the sample size N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} is allowed to remain bounded or grow much more slowly than N int N_{\mathrm{int}} and N ∂ Ω N_{\partial\Omega} . At each such point we generate a Monte Carlo FK estimate u ^ MC ​ ( X FK ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(X^{\mathrm{FK}}) as in Algorithm 1 . Proposition 6.1 shows that these labels admit the decomposition 

u ^ MC ​ ( X FK ) = u ⋆ ​ ( X FK ) + b ​ ( X FK ) + ζ , \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(X^{\mathrm{FK}})=u^{\star}(X^{\mathrm{FK}})+b(X^{\mathrm{FK}})+\zeta, 

where the deterministic bias b : Ω → ℝ b:\Omega\to\mathbb{R} satisfies 

| b ​ ( x ) | ≤ C bias ​ Δ ​ t + C T ​ e − κ ​ T max for all ​ x ∈ Ω , \lvert b(x)\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t}+C_{T}e^{-\kappa T_{\max}}\quad\text{for all }x\in\Omega, 

for constants C bias , C T , κ > 0 C_{\mathrm{bias}},C_{T},\kappa>0 independent of N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} and of the number of FK trajectories, and where, conditional on X FK X^{\mathrm{FK}} , the fluctuation ζ \zeta is mean-zero and sub-exponential with parameters that do not depend on N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} or on the number of trajectories. The corresponding population FK risk is 

ℛ FK ​ ( u ) := 𝔼 ​ [ | u ​ ( X FK ) − u ⋆ ​ ( X FK ) | 2 ] . \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u):=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl|u(X^{\mathrm{FK}})-u^{\star}(X^{\mathrm{FK}})\bigr|^{2}\bigr]. (D.3) 

For fixed nonnegative weights λ PDE \lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}} , λ ∂ Ω \lambda_{\partial\Omega} and λ FK \lambda_{\mathrm{FK}} we define the population FK-PINN risk 

ℛ ​ ( u ) := λ PDE ​ ℛ PDE ​ ( u ) + λ ∂ Ω ​ ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) + λ FK ​ ℛ FK ​ ( u ) . \mathcal{R}(u):=\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u)+\lambda_{\partial\Omega}\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(u)+\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u). (D.4) 

In what follows we assume that the PDE and boundary weights λ PDE , λ ∂ Ω \lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}},\lambda_{\partial\Omega} , and that the FK weight is non-negative. For convenience, we will use the abbreviation 

λ min := min ⁡ { λ PDE , λ ∂ Ω } . \lambda_{\min}:=\min\{\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}},\lambda_{\partial\Omega}\}. 

Note that rescaling the three weights only changes the constants in the bounds below, so this choice entails no loss of generality. The empirical FK-PINN risk is obtained by Monte Carlo approximation of the expectations in ( D.1 )–( D.3 ). Given i.i.d. samples 

{ x i int } i = 1 N int ⊂ Ω , { x j ∂ Ω } j = 1 N ∂ Ω ⊂ ∂ Ω , { x k FK } k = 1 N FK ⊂ Ω , \{x_{i}^{\mathrm{int}}\}_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{int}}}\subset\Omega,\quad\{x_{j}^{\partial\Omega}\}_{j=1}^{N_{\partial\Omega}}\subset\partial\Omega,\quad\{x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}\}_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\subset\Omega, 

and the corresponding FK labels { u ^ MC ​ ( x k FK ) } k = 1 N FK \{\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})\}_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}} , we set 

ℛ ^ PDE ​ ( u ) \displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u) := | Ω | ​ 1 N int ​ ∑ i = 1 N int | ℒ ​ u ​ ( x i int ) − f ​ ( x i int ) | 2 , \displaystyle:=\lvert\Omega\rvert\,\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{int}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{int}}}\bigl|\mathcal{L}u(x_{i}^{\mathrm{int}})-f(x_{i}^{\mathrm{int}})\bigr|^{2}, (D.5) 

ℛ ^ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) \displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\partial\Omega}(u) := | ∂ Ω | ​ 1 N ∂ Ω ​ ∑ j = 1 N ∂ Ω | u ​ ( x j ∂ Ω ) − g ​ ( x j ∂ Ω ) | 2 , \displaystyle:=\lvert\partial\Omega\rvert\,\frac{1}{N_{\partial\Omega}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\partial\Omega}}\bigl|u(x_{j}^{\partial\Omega})-g(x_{j}^{\partial\Omega})\bigr|^{2}, (D.6) 

ℛ ^ FK ​ ( u ) \displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u) := 1 N FK ​ ∑ k = 1 N FK | u ​ ( x k FK ) − u ^ MC ​ ( x k FK ) | 2 , \displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\bigl|u(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})-\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})\bigr|^{2}, (D.7) 

and 

ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) := λ PDE ​ ℛ ^ PDE ​ ( u ) + λ ∂ Ω ​ ℛ ^ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) + λ FK ​ ℛ ^ FK ​ ( u ) . \widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u):=\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u)+\lambda_{\partial\Omega}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\partial\Omega}(u)+\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u). (D.8) 

Up to the adaptive task weights used in Equation 4.3 , the empirical loss ℛ ^ ​ ( u θ ) \widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{\theta}) coincides with ℛ FK ​ - ​ PINN ​ ( θ , φ ) \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK\text{-}PINN}}(\theta,\varphi) . 

We will make use of the following classical PDE estimate. 
Lemma D.1 (PDE stability) . 
Under Assumption D.1 there exists a constant C stab > 0 C_{\mathrm{stab}}>0 such that for every u ∈ H 2 ​ ( Ω ) ∩ C ​ ( Ω ¯ ) u\in H^{2}(\Omega)\cap C(\overline{\Omega}) with u | ∂ Ω ∈ L 2 ​ ( ∂ Ω ) u|_{\partial\Omega}\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega) , 

∥ u − u ⋆ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) 2 ≤ C stab ​ ( ℛ PDE ​ ( u ) + ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) ) . \lVert u-u^{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C_{\mathrm{stab}}\Bigl(\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u)+\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(u)\Bigr). (D.9) 

Lemma D.1 is a direct consequence of the a priori boundary estimates of Agmon et al. ( 1959 ) , combined with the embedding L 2 ​ ( Ω ) ↪ H − s ​ ( Ω ) L^{2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow H^{-s}(\Omega) for suitable s > 0 s>0 . One may also refer to Evans ( 2022 , Chapter 6) and Jiao et al. ( 2022 ); Lei et al. ( 2025 ) for formulations tailored to PINNs. 

We will work with a hypothesis class 𝒰 \mathcal{U} of candidate solutions, specified in the next subsection. For any u ∈ 𝒰 u\in\mathcal{U} we define 

ℰ app := inf v ∈ 𝒰 ∥ v − u ⋆ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) , ℰ stat : = sup v ∈ 𝒰 | ℛ ( v ) − ℛ ^ ( v ) | . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{app}}:=\inf_{v\in\mathcal{U}}\lVert v-u^{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}:=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{U}}\bigl|\mathcal{R}(v)-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(v)\bigr|. (D.10) 

The following decomposition is standard. 
Lemma D.2 (Risk decomposition) . 
Let u ^ ∈ arg ​ min u ∈ 𝒰 ⁡ ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) \widehat{u}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u) be any empirical minimizer. Then 

ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ≤ 2 ​ ℰ stat + inf u ∈ 𝒰 [ ℛ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ] . \mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\leq 2\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}+\inf_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\bigl[\mathcal{R}(u)-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\bigr]. (D.11) 
Proof. 
Fix any u ∈ 𝒰 u\in\mathcal{U} . Then 

ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star}) = ( ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ^ ​ ( u ^ ) ) + ( ℛ ^ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) ) \displaystyle=\bigl(\mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{u})\bigr)+\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{u})-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u)\bigr) 

+ ( ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ​ ( u ) ) + ( ℛ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ) . \displaystyle\quad+\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u)-\mathcal{R}(u)\bigr)+\bigl(\mathcal{R}(u)-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\bigr). 

By optimality of u ^ \widehat{u} for ℛ ^ \widehat{\mathcal{R}} we have ℛ ^ ​ ( u ^ ) ≤ ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) \widehat{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{u})\leq\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u) , so the middle term is nonpositive. Hence 

ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star}) ≤ | ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ^ ​ ( u ^ ) | + | ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ​ ( u ) | + ( ℛ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ) \displaystyle\leq\bigl|\mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{u})\bigr|+\bigl|\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u)-\mathcal{R}(u)\bigr|+\bigl(\mathcal{R}(u)-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\bigr) 

≤ 2 ​ ℰ stat + ( ℛ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ) , \displaystyle\leq 2\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}+\bigl(\mathcal{R}(u)-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\bigr), 

where we used the definition of ℰ stat \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}} in ( D.10 ). Since this bound holds for every u ∈ 𝒰 u\in\mathcal{U} , taking the infimum over u u yields 

ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ≤ 2 ​ ℰ stat + inf u ∈ 𝒰 [ ℛ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ] . ∎ \mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\leq 2\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}+\inf_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\bigl[\mathcal{R}(u)-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\bigr].\qed 

Later we also introduce an optimization error ℰ opt \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}} that measures the gap between the empirical minimizer and the iterate produced by gradient descent. These three terms will form the building blocks of the final non-asymptotic bound. 

D.1.3 Neural Network Architecture 

We consider fully connected feedforward neural networks with tanh \tanh activations in the hidden layers and a linear output. For an integer depth L ≥ 1 L\geq 1 and a fixed width vector ( m ℓ ) ℓ = 0 L (m_{\ell})_{\ell=0}^{L} with m 0 = d m_{0}=d and m L = 1 m_{L}=1 , a parameter vector
