𝜽 := { W ℓ , b ℓ } ℓ = 1 L , W ℓ ∈ ℝ m ℓ × m ℓ − 1 , b ℓ ∈ ℝ m ℓ , \bm{\theta}:=\bigl\{W_{\ell},b_{\ell}\bigr\}_{\ell=1}^{L},\quad W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{\ell}\times m_{\ell-1}},\ b_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{\ell}}, 

defines a function u 𝜽 : Ω → ℝ u_{\bm{\theta}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R} by 

z 0 ​ ( x ) \displaystyle z_{0}(x) := x ∈ ℝ d , \displaystyle:=x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, (D.12) 

z ℓ ​ ( x ) \displaystyle z_{\ell}(x) := tanh ⁡ ( W ℓ ​ z ℓ − 1 ​ ( x ) + b ℓ ) , ℓ = 1 , … , L − 1 , \displaystyle:=\tanh\bigl(W_{\ell}z_{\ell-1}(x)+b_{\ell}\bigr),\quad\ell=1,\dots,L-1, (D.13) 

u 𝜽 ​ ( x ) \displaystyle u_{\bm{\theta}}(x) := W L ​ z L − 1 ​ ( x ) + b L ∈ ℝ . \displaystyle:=W_{L}z_{L-1}(x)+b_{L}\in\mathbb{R}. (D.14) 

We write P := P ​ ( L , ( m ℓ ) ℓ ) P:=P(L,(m_{\ell})_{\ell}) for the total number of free parameters in 𝜽 \bm{\theta} and call L L the depth and m := max ℓ ⁡ m ℓ m:=\max_{\ell}m_{\ell} the width of the network. 

We will later use the following upper bound on the number of free parameters: by definition, 

P = ∑ ℓ = 1 L ( m ℓ ​ m ℓ − 1 + m ℓ ) , m 0 = d , m L = 1 ​ and ​ m ℓ ≤ m ​ for all ​ 0 ≤ ℓ ≤ L . P=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bigl(m_{\ell}m_{\ell-1}+m_{\ell}\bigr),\ \ m_{0}=d,\ \ m_{L}=1\ \text{ and }\ m_{\ell}\leq m\text{ for all }0\leq\ell\leq L. 

This implies that P ≤ C arch ​ ( d ) ​ L ​ m 2 P\leq C_{\mathrm{arch}}(d)\,L\,m^{2} for a constant C arch ​ ( d ) > 0 C_{\mathrm{arch}}(d)>0 depending only on the input dimension d d . In all subsequent bounds we may therefore replace P P by C arch ​ ( d ) ​ L ​ m 2 C_{\mathrm{arch}}(d)\,L\,m^{2} if we wish to make the dependence on the architectural hyperparameters explicit. 

For the learning-theoretic analysis we restrict attention to networks whose outputs and low order derivatives are uniformly bounded on Ω ¯ \overline{\Omega} . For fixed L L , ( m ℓ ) ℓ (m_{\ell})_{\ell} and B > 0 B>0 we first define the parameter set 

Θ L , m := { 𝜽 = ( W ℓ , b ℓ ) ℓ = 1 L : W ℓ ∈ ℝ m ℓ × m ℓ − 1 , b ℓ ∈ ℝ m ℓ } . \Theta_{L,m}:=\Bigl\{\bm{\theta}=(W_{\ell},b_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}:W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{\ell}\times m_{\ell-1}},\ b_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{\ell}}\Bigr\}. 

The associated hypothesis class is 

𝒰 L , m , B : = { u 𝜽 : 𝜽 ∈ Θ L , m , ∥ u 𝜽 ∥ C 2 ​ ( Ω ¯ ) ≤ B } , \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B}:=\bigl\{u_{\bm{\theta}}:\bm{\theta}\in\Theta_{L,m},\ \lVert u_{\bm{\theta}}\rVert_{C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})}\leq B\bigr\}, (D.15) 

where ∥ ⋅ ∥ C 2 ​ ( Ω ¯ ) \lVert\cdot\rVert_{C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})} denotes the usual C 2 C^{2} norm that controls the function and its first and second order partial derivatives on Ω ¯ \overline{\Omega} . The class 𝒰 L , m , B \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B} is nonempty for every B B large enough, since constant functions belong to it. 

To lighten notation we often write 𝒰 \mathcal{U} for 𝒰 L , m , B \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B} and denote by P P the maximal number of parameters of networks in 𝒰 \mathcal{U} . All constants in the sequel may depend on ( L , m , B , d ) (L,m,B,d) but never on the sample sizes ( N int , N ∂ Ω , N FK ) (N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega},N_{\mathrm{FK}}) . 

D.2 Approximation Error Bound 

We first bound the approximation error in ( D.10 ) in terms of the size of the network. The desired bounds will directly follow from the approximation error bounds of tanh \mathrm{tanh} networks due to De Ryck et al. ( 2021 ) . 
Theorem D.3 (Approximation by two hidden layer tanh \tanh networks, simplified) . 
Let d ∈ ℕ d\in\mathbb{N} , let s ∈ ℕ s\in\mathbb{N} with s ≥ 3 s\geq 3 and let f ∈ W s , ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ] d ) f\in W^{s,\infty}([0,1]^{d}) . There exist constants C approx , C width > 0 C_{\mathrm{approx}},C_{\mathrm{width}}>0 , depending only on ( d , s , ∥ f ∥ W s , ∞ ) (d,s,\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}}) , such that for every integer N N large enough there is a tanh \tanh network f ^ N \widehat{f}_{N} with two hidden layers whose total number of parameters satisfies 

P N ≤ C width ​ N d P_{N}\leq C_{\mathrm{width}}N^{d} 

and such that, for all multi indices α \alpha with | α | ≤ 2 |\alpha|\leq 2 , 

∥ D α ​ f − D α ​ f ^ N ∥ L ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ] d ) ≤ C approx ​ N − ( s − | α | ) . \lVert D^{\alpha}f-D^{\alpha}\widehat{f}_{N}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{d})}\leq C_{\mathrm{approx}}N^{-(s-|\alpha|)}. (D.16) 
Remark D.4 . 
Theorem D.3 is a mild simplification of De Ryck et al. ( 2021 , Theorem 5.1) . The original statement specifies exact widths for the two hidden layers and tracks additional logarithmic factors in N N , while we only retain the dominant polynomial dependence on N N , which is all that enters our rate calculations. 

We now apply this result to the hypothesis class 𝒰 \mathcal{U} defined in ( D.15 ) and to the true solution u ⋆ u^{\star} . 
Corollary D.5 (Approximation error for 𝒰 L , m , B \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B} ) . 
Suppose that Assumption D.1 holds and that u ⋆ ∈ W s , ∞ ​ ( Ω ) u^{\star}\in W^{s,\infty}(\Omega) for some integer s ≥ 3 s\geq 3 . Fix any depth L ≥ 3 L\geq 3 . Then there exist constants C app , 1 , C app , 2 > 0 C_{\mathrm{app},1},C_{\mathrm{app},2}>0 and m 0 ∈ ℕ m_{0}\in\mathbb{N} such that for every width m ≥ m 0 m\geq m_{0} the hypothesis class 𝒰 L , m , B \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B} contains a network u approx u^{\mathrm{approx}} with 

∥ u ⋆ − u approx ∥ C 2 ​ ( Ω ¯ ) ≤ C app , 1 ​ P ​ ( L , m ) − γ , γ := s − 2 d , \lVert u^{\star}-u^{\mathrm{approx}}\rVert_{C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})}\leq C_{\mathrm{app},1}\,P(L,m)^{-\gamma},\qquad\gamma:=\frac{s-2}{d}, (D.17) 

where P ​ ( L , m ) P(L,m) denotes the total number of free parameters of networks in 𝒰 L , m , B \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B} . In particular, 

ℰ app = inf u ∈ 𝒰 L , m , B ∥ u − u ⋆ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) ≤ C app , 2 ​ P ​ ( L , m ) − γ . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{app}}=\inf_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{L,m,B}}\lVert u-u^{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\mathrm{app},2}\,P(L,m)^{-\gamma}. (D.18) 
Proof. 
By the Sobolev extension theorem whose conditions are satisfied here (Evans, 2022 ) , we can extend u ⋆ u^{\star} to a function in W s , ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ] d ) W^{s,\infty}([0,1]^{d}) with comparable norm. Now we apply Theorem D.3 with this extension :for each integer N N large enough, we obtain a two-hidden-layer tanh \tanh network u ^ N \widehat{u}_{N} with depth L = 3 L=3 , widths of order N d N^{d} in each hidden layer, total number of parameters P N ≤ C width ​ N d P_{N}\leq C_{\mathrm{width}}N^{d} , and 

∥ D α ​ u ⋆ − D α ​ u ^ N ∥ L ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ] d ) ≤ C approx ​ N − ( s − | α | ) , | α | ≤ 2 . \lVert D^{\alpha}u^{\star}-D^{\alpha}\widehat{u}_{N}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{d})}\leq C_{\mathrm{approx}}N^{-(s-|\alpha|)},\qquad|\alpha|\leq 2. 

In particular, 

∥ u ⋆ − u ^ N ∥ C 2 ​ ( Ω ¯ ) ≤ C ​ N − ( s − 2 ) \lVert u^{\star}-\widehat{u}_{N}\rVert_{C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})}\leq CN^{-(s-2)} 

for a constant C C depending on ( d , s , ∥ u ⋆ ∥ W s , ∞ ) (d,s,\lVert u^{\star}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}}) . 

Any such two-hidden-layer network can be realized as a special case of our architecture with depth L ≥ 3 L\geq 3 and width m m as soon as m m is larger than the maximal width of u ^ N \widehat{u}_{N} , since the extra layers and neurons can be made inactive by setting their weights to zero or to identities. Thus, for every L ≥ 3 L\geq 3 there exists m 0 m_{0} such that for all m ≥ m 0 m\geq m_{0} the network u ^ N \widehat{u}_{N} belongs to 𝒰 L , m , B \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B} provided B B is chosen large enough. 

Let N min N_{\min} be the (fixed) threshold required in Theorem D.3 and choose 

N := max ⁡ { N min , ⌊ ( P ​ ( L , m ) / C width ) 1 / d ⌋ } . N:=\max\Bigl\{N_{\min},\,\bigl\lfloor(P(L,m)/C_{\mathrm{width}})^{1/d}\bigr\rfloor\Bigr\}. 

Then P N ≤ C width ​ N d ≤ P ​ ( L , m ) P_{N}\leq C_{\mathrm{width}}N^{d}\leq P(L,m) and therefore 

∥ u ⋆ − u ^ N ∥ C 2 ​ ( Ω ¯ ) ≤ C ​ N − ( s − 2 ) ≤ C ′ ​ P ​ ( L , m ) − γ , γ = s − 2 d , \lVert u^{\star}-\widehat{u}_{N}\rVert_{C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})}\leq CN^{-(s-2)}\leq C^{\prime}P(L,m)^{-\gamma},\qquad\gamma=\frac{s-2}{d}, 

for a constant C ′ > 0 C^{\prime}>0 . Taking u approx = u ^ N u^{\mathrm{approx}}=\widehat{u}_{N} yields ( D.17 ) with C app , 1 = C ​ C ′ C_{\mathrm{app},1}=CC^{\prime} . Since Ω \Omega is bounded, ∥ v ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) ≤ C Ω ​ ∥ v ∥ C ​ ( Ω ¯ ) \lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\Omega}\lVert v\rVert_{C(\overline{\Omega})} for every continuous v v , and ( D.18 ) follows from ( D.17 ) by absorbing C Ω C_{\Omega} into C app , 2 C_{\mathrm{app},2} . ∎ 

Corollary D.5 shows that ℰ app \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{app}} decays at least polynomially in the number of parameters whenever u ⋆ u^{\star} has Sobolev regularity of order exceeding two. Stronger, essentially exponential, rates can be obtained under analyticity assumptions on u ⋆ u^{\star} using De Ryck et al. ( 2021 , Corollary 5.5) , but we do not pursue this here. 
Lemma D.6 (Control of the population risk by C 2 C^{2} approximation) . 
Under Assumption D.1 and with the weights ( λ PDE , λ ∂ Ω , λ FK ) (\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}},\lambda_{\partial\Omega},\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}) as in ( D.4 ), there exists a constant C risk > 0 C_{\mathrm{risk}}>0 , depending only on the PDE coefficients, the domain, and the weights, such that for every u ∈ C 2 ​ ( Ω ¯ ) u\in C^{2}(\overline{\Omega}) with 

∥ u − u ⋆ ∥ C 2 ​ ( Ω ¯ ) ≤ ε , \lVert u-u^{\star}\rVert_{C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})}\leq\varepsilon, 

one has 

ℛ ​ ( u ) ≤ C risk ​ ε 2 . \mathcal{R}(u)\leq C_{\mathrm{risk}}\,\varepsilon^{2}. (D.19) 

In particular, for the approximating network u approx u^{\mathrm{approx}} from Corollary D.5 we have 

ℛ ​ ( u approx ) ≤ C ~ risk ​ P − 2 ​ γ , \mathcal{R}(u^{\mathrm{approx}})\leq\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{risk}}\,P^{-2\gamma}, (D.20) 

for a constant C ~ risk > 0 \widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{risk}}>0 independent of P P . 
Proof. 
Since ℒ \mathcal{L} is a second order operator with bounded measurable coefficients, there exists a constant C ℒ > 0 C_{\mathcal{L}}>0 such that 

sup x ∈ Ω ¯ | ℒ ​ ( u − u ⋆ ) ​ ( x ) | ≤ C ℒ ​ ∥ u − u ⋆ ∥ C 2 ​ ( Ω ¯ ) . \sup_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}\bigl|\mathcal{L}(u-u^{\star})(x)\bigr|\leq C_{\mathcal{L}}\,\lVert u-u^{\star}\rVert_{C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})}. 

Therefore 

ℛ PDE ​ ( u ) = ∫ Ω ( ℒ ​ u ​ ( x ) − f ​ ( x ) ) 2 ​ 𝑑 x = ∫ Ω ( ℒ ​ ( u − u ⋆ ) ​ ( x ) ) 2 ​ 𝑑 x ≤ | Ω | ​ C ℒ 2 ​ ε 2 . \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u)=\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\mathcal{L}u(x)-f(x)\bigr)^{2}\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\mathcal{L}(u-u^{\star})(x)\bigr)^{2}\,dx\leq\lvert\Omega\rvert\,C_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{2}. 

On the boundary we have 

sup x ∈ ∂ Ω | u ​ ( x ) − g ​ ( x ) | = sup x ∈ ∂ Ω | u ​ ( x ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x ) | ≤ ∥ u − u ⋆ ∥ C ​ ( Ω ¯ ) ≤ ε , \sup_{x\in\partial\Omega}\bigl|u(x)-g(x)\bigr|=\sup_{x\in\partial\Omega}\bigl|u(x)-u^{\star}(x)\bigr|\leq\lVert u-u^{\star}\rVert_{C(\overline{\Omega})}\leq\varepsilon, 

which gives 

ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) = ∫ ∂ Ω ( u ​ ( x ) − g ​ ( x ) ) 2 ​ 𝑑 s ≤ | ∂ Ω | ​ ε 2 . \mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(u)=\int_{\partial\Omega}\bigl(u(x)-g(x)\bigr)^{2}\,ds\leq\lvert\partial\Omega\rvert\,\varepsilon^{2}. 

Finally, for the FK term we use that u ⋆ u^{\star} is itself the target in ( D.3 ), hence 

ℛ FK ​ ( u ) = 𝔼 ​ [ | u ​ ( X FK ) − u ⋆ ​ ( X FK ) | 2 ] ≤ ∥ u − u ⋆ ∥ C ​ ( Ω ¯ ) 2 ≤ ε 2 . \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl|u(X^{\mathrm{FK}})-u^{\star}(X^{\mathrm{FK}})\bigr|^{2}\bigr]\leq\lVert u-u^{\star}\rVert_{C(\overline{\Omega})}^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{2}. 

Collecting these three bounds and inserting the weights gives ( D.19 ) with a constant C risk C_{\mathrm{risk}} that depends only on the coefficients of ℒ \mathcal{L} , the domain, and the weights. The bound ( D.20 ) then follows from ( D.17 ). ∎ 

D.3 Optimization Error Bound 

We next control the optimization error incurred by terminating gradient descent after a finite number of iterations. Throughout this subsection we fix the sample set and view the empirical FK-PINN loss 

ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ) := ℛ ^ ​ ( u 𝜽 ) \ell(\bm{\theta}):=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{\bm{\theta}}) 

as a deterministic function of the parameters. 

We rely on the Polyak–Łojasiewicz framework of Karimi et al. ( 2016 ); Liu et al. ( 2022 ) . A function ℓ : ℝ P → ℝ \ell:\mathbb{R}^{P}\to\mathbb{R} satisfies a local PL ∗ condition on a set S ⊂ ℝ P S\subset\mathbb{R}^{P} with constant μ > 0 \mu>0 if 

ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ⋆ ) ≤ 1 2 ​ μ ​ ∥ ∇ ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ) ∥ 2 2 , ∀ 𝜽 ∈ S , \ell(\bm{\theta})-\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\star})\leq\frac{1}{2\mu}\,\lVert\nabla\ell(\bm{\theta})\rVert_{2}^{2},\qquad\forall\bm{\theta}\in S, (D.21) 

for some global minimizer 𝜽 ⋆ ∈ S \bm{\theta}^{\star}\in S of ℓ \ell . If, in addition, ℓ \ell is β \beta -smooth on S S (that is, ∥ ∇ ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ∇ ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ′ ) ∥ 2 ≤ β ​ ∥ 𝜽 − 𝜽 ′ ∥ 2 \lVert\nabla\ell(\bm{\theta})-\nabla\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\prime})\rVert_{2}\leq\beta\lVert\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert_{2} for all 𝜽 , 𝜽 ′ ∈ S \bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in S ), the ratio 

κ ℓ ​ ( S ) := β μ \kappa_{\ell}(S):=\frac{\beta}{\mu} 

plays the role of a condition number. 

The following result is a special case of Liu et al. ( 2022 , Theorem 6) . 
Theorem D.7 (Gradient descent under a local PL ∗ condition) . 
Let ℓ \ell be differentiable and β \beta -smooth on a convex set S ⊂ ℝ P S\subset\mathbb{R}^{P} , and assume that ℓ \ell satisfies the PL ∗ condition ( D.21 ) on S S with constant μ > 0 \mu>0 . Let 𝛉 0 ∈ S \bm{\theta}_{0}\in S and consider gradient descent 

𝜽 t + 1 = 𝜽 t − η ​ ∇ ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 t ) , \bm{\theta}_{t+1}=\bm{\theta}_{t}-\eta\nabla\ell(\bm{\theta}_{t}), 

with a fixed step size η ∈ ( 0 , 2 / β ] \eta\in(0,2/\beta] . Suppose that all iterates remain in S S . Then for all t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 t ) − ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ⋆ ) ≤ ( 1 − η ​ μ ) t ​ ( ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 0 ) − ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ⋆ ) ) . \ell(\bm{\theta}_{t})-\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\star})\leq\bigl(1-\eta\mu\bigr)^{t}\bigl(\ell(\bm{\theta}_{0})-\ell(\bm{\theta}^{\star})\bigr). (D.22) 

In Theorem 5.4 we proved that the FK-augmented loss enjoys a PL ∗ inequality with a condition number that stays uniformly bounded as the number of collocation points increases. We translate this consequence for the empirical loss. 
Corollary D.8 (Uniform PL ∗ geometry for FK-PINNs) . 
Under the assumptions of Theorem 5.4 there exist a convex set S ⊂ ℝ P S\subset\mathbb{R}^{P} , constants μ FK > 0 \mu_{\mathrm{FK}}>0 , β FK < ∞ \beta_{\mathrm{FK}}<\infty and an initialization 𝛉 0 ∈ S {\bm{\theta}}_{0}\in S such that the empirical loss ℓ ​ ( 𝛉 ) = ℛ ^ ​ ( u 𝛉 ) \ell(\bm{\theta})=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{\bm{\theta}}) satisfies the PL ∗ condition ( D.21 ) on S S with constant μ FK \mu_{\mathrm{FK}} , is β FK \beta_{\mathrm{FK}} -smooth on S S , and the associated condition number 

κ FK := β FK μ FK \kappa_{\mathrm{FK}}:=\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{FK}}}{\mu_{\mathrm{FK}}} 

is bounded by a constant that does not depend on ( N int , N ∂ Ω , N FK ) (N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega},N_{\mathrm{FK}}) . 

Under Corollary D.8 , gradient descent with a suitable step size converges linearly to an empirical minimizer. We quantify the corresponding optimization error in terms of the number of iterations. 
Proposition D.9 (Optimization error bound) . 
Let { 𝛉 t } t ≥ 0 \{{\bm{\theta}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0} be the gradient descent iterates for ℓ ​ ( 𝛉 ) = ℛ ^ ​ ( u 𝛉 ) \ell(\bm{\theta})=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{\bm{\theta}}) with step size η ∈ ( 0 , 2 / β FK ] \eta\in(0,2/\beta_{\mathrm{FK}}] , initialized at 𝛉 0 {\bm{\theta}}_{0} as in Corollary D.8 . Let 

u ^ ∈ arg ​ min u ∈ 𝒰 ⁡ ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) \widehat{u}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u) 

be an empirical minimizer and write 𝛉 ⋆ {\bm{\theta}}^{\star} for a parameter vector with u 𝛉 ⋆ = u ^ u_{{\bm{\theta}}^{\star}}=\widehat{u} . Define the optimization error 

ℰ opt ​ ( t ) := ℛ ^ ​ ( u 𝜽 t ) − ℛ ^ ​ ( u ^ ) . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(t):=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{{\bm{\theta}}_{t}})-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{u}). (D.23) 

Then, under Corollary D.8 , there exist constants C opt , c opt > 0 C_{\mathrm{opt}},c_{\mathrm{opt}}>0 independent of the sample sizes and of t t such that 

ℰ opt ​ ( t ) ≤ C opt ​ exp ⁡ ( − c opt ​ t ) . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(t)\leq C_{\mathrm{opt}}\exp(-c_{\mathrm{opt}}t). (D.24) 

In particular, for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 the network u 𝛉 t u_{{\bm{\theta}}_{t}} is an ℰ opt ​ ( t ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(t) -approximate empirical minimizer of ℛ ^ \widehat{\mathcal{R}} in the sense that 

ℛ ^ ​ ( u 𝜽 t ) ≤ inf u ∈ 𝒰 ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) + ℰ opt ​ ( t ) . \widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{{\bm{\theta}}_{t}})\leq\inf_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u)+\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(t). 
Proof. 
Inequality ( D.24 ) follows directly from Theorem D.7 with μ = μ FK \mu=\mu_{\mathrm{FK}} and β = β FK \beta=\beta_{\mathrm{FK}} , taking 𝜽 ⋆ {\bm{\theta}}^{\star} to be any parameter vector that realizes an empirical minimizer u ^ \widehat{u} of ℛ ^ \widehat{\mathcal{R}} . ∎ 

D.4 Statistical Error Bound 

We now turn to the statistical error ℰ stat \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}} in ( D.10 ), which captures the deviation between the population risk and its empirical approximation. Our goal is to obtain non-asymptotic high probability bounds on 

ℰ stat = sup u ∈ 𝒰 | ℛ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ^ ​ ( u ) | \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\bigl|\mathcal{R}(u)-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u)\bigr| 

in terms of the number of collocation points and the complexity of the hypothesis class. The analysis follows the now standard Rademacher complexity approach in statistical learning theory, but it has two distinctive features. 

First, we operate in a data scarce regime for the FK term: N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} is allowed to remain bounded while N int N_{\mathrm{int}} and N ∂ Ω N_{\partial\Omega} grow. Second, we must control the complexity not only of the network outputs u 𝜽 u_{\bm{\theta}} but also of their first and second order derivatives, since the PDE residual involves ∂ α u 𝜽 \partial^{\alpha}u_{\bm{\theta}} for | α | ≤ 2 |\alpha|\leq 2 . To the best of our knowledge, existing results of this kind only cover piecewise polynomial activations (see e.g. Jiao et al. ( 2022 ); Lu et al. ( 2022 ); Lei et al. ( 2025 ) ), which does not include tanh \tanh . We therefore derive new pseudo-dimension bounds for tanh \tanh networks and their derivatives, building on classical results of Karpinski and Macintyre ( 1997 ) and Anthony and Bartlett ( 2009 ) . These bounds are of independent interest. 

D.4.1 Preliminary results 

We briefly recall the main tools from empirical process theory that we will use. Detailed expositions can be found, e.g., in Anthony and Bartlett ( 2009 ) and Wainwright ( 2019 ) . 
Rademacher complexity. 
Let ( Z i ) i = 1 n (Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n} be i.i.d. random variables taking values in a space 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} and let ℱ \mathcal{F} be a class of measurable functions f : 𝒵 → ℝ f:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} . The empirical Rademacher complexity of ℱ \mathcal{F} with respect to a fixed sample Z 1 , … , Z n Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n} is 

ℜ n ​ ( ℱ ∣ Z 1 : n ) := 𝔼 σ ​ [ sup f ∈ ℱ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n σ i ​ f ​ ( Z i ) ] , \mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F}\mid Z_{1:n}):=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\Biggl[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(Z_{i})\Biggr], 

where ( σ i ) i = 1 n (\sigma_{i})_{i=1}^{n} are i.i.d. Rademacher variables ( ℙ ​ ( σ i = 1 ) = ℙ ​ ( σ i = − 1 ) = 1 / 2 \mathbb{P}(\sigma_{i}=1)=\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{i}=-1)=1/2 ). The expected Rademacher complexity is ℜ n ​ ( ℱ ) := 𝔼 ​ [ ℜ n ​ ( ℱ ∣ Z 1 : n ) ] \mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F}\mid Z_{1:n})] . 

Rademacher complexity controls uniform deviations between empirical and population averages. 
Lemma D.10 (Symmetrization and contraction) . 
Let ℱ \mathcal{F} be a class of functions f : 𝒵 → ℝ f:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} with | f ​ ( z ) | ≤ B \lvert f(z)\rvert\leq B for all f ∈ ℱ f\in\mathcal{F} and z ∈ 𝒵 z\in\mathcal{Z} . Then for any n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} and any δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) , with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , 

sup f ∈ ℱ | 𝔼 ​ [ f ​ ( Z ) ] − 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n f ​ ( Z i ) | ≤ 2 ​ ℜ n ​ ( ℱ ) + 3 ​ B ​ log ⁡ ( 2 / δ ) 2 ​ n . \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}[f(Z)]-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(Z_{i})\Bigr|\leq 2\,\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F})+3B\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2n}}. 

Moreover, if ϕ : ℝ → ℝ \phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} is L L -Lipschitz and ϕ ​ ( 0 ) = 0 \phi(0)=0 , then 

ℜ n ​ ( ϕ ∘ ℱ ) ≤ L ​ ℜ n ​ ( ℱ ) , \mathfrak{R}_{n}(\phi\circ\mathcal{F})\leq L\,\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F}), 

where ϕ ∘ ℱ := { ϕ ∘ f : f ∈ ℱ } \phi\circ\mathcal{F}:=\{\phi\circ f:f\in\mathcal{F}\} . 

A proof of the deviation bound is given in (Wainwright, 2019 , Chapter 3) . The Lipschitz mapping bound follows from the contraction principle (Ledoux and Talagrand, 2013 ) . 
Covering numbers and pseudo-dimension. 
Let A ⊂ ℝ n A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} and consider the metric 

d ∞ ​ ( a , a ′ ) := max 1 ≤ i ≤ n ⁡ | a i − a i ′ | . d_{\infty}(a,a^{\prime}):=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\lvert a_{i}-a^{\prime}_{i}\rvert. 

An ε \varepsilon -cover of A A with respect to d ∞ d_{\infty} is a finite subset V ⊂ ℝ n V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} such that for every a ∈ A a\in A there exists v ∈ V v\in V with d ∞ ​ ( a , v ) ≤ ε d_{\infty}(a,v)\leq\varepsilon . The covering number 𝒞 ​ ( ε , A , d ∞ ) \mathcal{C}(\varepsilon,A,d_{\infty}) is the smallest cardinality of an ε \varepsilon -cover of A A . 

Given a function class ℱ \mathcal{F} and a sample Z 1 : n = ( Z 1 , … , Z n ) Z_{1:n}=(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}) , the evaluation set 

ℱ | Z 1 : n := { ( f ​ ( Z 1 ) , … , f ​ ( Z n ) ) : f ∈ ℱ } ⊂ ℝ n \mathcal{F}|_{Z_{1:n}}:=\bigl\{(f(Z_{1}),\dots,f(Z_{n})):f\in\mathcal{F}\bigr\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} 

induces a covering number 

𝒞 ∞ ​ ( ε , ℱ , n ) := sup Z 1 : n 𝒞 ​ ( ε , ℱ | Z 1 : n , d ∞ ) . \mathcal{C}_{\infty}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F},n):=\sup_{Z_{1:n}}\mathcal{C}\bigl(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}|_{Z_{1:n}},d_{\infty}\bigr). 

The pseudo-dimension Pdim ​ ( ℱ ) \mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F}) is the standard extension of VC dimension to real-valued function classes (Anthony and Bartlett, 2009 ) . The next two results relate Rademacher complexity, covering numbers and pseudo-dimension. 
Lemma D.11 (Dudley’s entropy integral) . 
Let ℱ \mathcal{F} be a class of functions f : 𝒵 → ℝ f:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} with | f | ≤ B \lvert f\rvert\leq B . Then for every n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} , 

ℜ n ​ ( ℱ ) ≤ inf 0 < δ < B ( 4 ​ δ + 12 n ​ ∫ δ B log ⁡ ( 2 ​ 𝒞 ∞ ​ ( ε , ℱ , n ) ) ​ 𝑑 ε ) . \mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F})\leq\inf_{0<\delta<B}\Biggl(4\delta+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\delta}^{B}\sqrt{\log\bigl(2\,\mathcal{C}_{\infty}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F},n)\bigr)}\,d\varepsilon\Biggr). 
Proposition D.12 (Covering numbers from pseudo-dimension) . 
Let ℱ \mathcal{F} be a class of functions from a domain 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} to [ 0 , B ] [0,B] with pseudo-dimension d p := Pdim ​ ( ℱ ) d_{\mathrm{p}}:=\mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F}) . Then for every n ≥ d p n\geq d_{\mathrm{p}} and every ε ∈ ( 0 , B ) \varepsilon\in(0,B) , 

𝒞 ∞ ​ ( ε , ℱ , n ) ≤ ( e ​ B ​ n ε ​ d p ) d p . \mathcal{C}_{\infty}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F},n)\leq\biggl(\frac{eBn}{\varepsilon d_{\mathrm{p}}}\biggr)^{d_{\mathrm{p}}}. 

Proposition D.12 goes back to Anthony and Bartlett ( 2009 ) . Combining Lemma D.11 with Proposition D.12 yields Rademacher complexity bounds of order Pdim ​ ( ℱ ) / n \sqrt{\mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F})/n} up to logarithmic factors. 

D.4.2 Pseudo-dimension bounds for derivatives of tanh \tanh networks 

We bound the pseudo-dimension of the classes generated by u 𝜽 u_{\bm{\theta}} and its first and second order derivatives by proceeding in two steps. First, each derivative D α ​ u 𝜽 D^{\alpha}u_{\bm{\theta}} can be realized by an analytic network that reuses the original weights and biases, at the cost of a controlled increase in the number of computation nodes. Second, we invoke the VC-dimension bound for Pfaffian networks from (Karpinski and Macintyre, 1997 ) , which applies to tanh \tanh since it is an analytic Pfaffian function. 
Lemma D.13 (Pseudo-dimension as VC dimension of subgraph thresholds) . 
Let ℱ ⊂ { f : X → ℝ } \mathcal{F}\subset\{f:X\to\mathbb{R}\} and define the associated threshold (subgraph) class 

thresh ⁡ ( ℱ ) := { ( x , t ) ↦ 𝟏 ​ { f ​ ( x ) − t > 0 } : f ∈ ℱ } ⊂ { 0 , 1 } X × ℝ . \operatorname{thresh}(\mathcal{F}):=\Bigl\{\,(x,t)\mapsto\mathbf{1}\{f(x)-t>0\}\;:\;f\in\mathcal{F}\Bigr\}\subset\{0,1\}^{X\times\mathbb{R}}. 

Then 

Pdim ​ ( ℱ ) = VCdim ​ ( thresh ⁡ ( ℱ ) ) . \mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F})=\mathrm{VCdim}\bigl(\operatorname{thresh}(\mathcal{F})\bigr). 
Proof. 
The equivalence follows directly from the definitions of pseudo-shattering and shattering after mapping f f to the subgraph threshold map ( x , t ) ↦ 𝟏 ​ { f ​ ( x ) > t } (x,t)\mapsto\mathbf{1}\{f(x)>t\} . A detailed argument is given in (Anthony and Bartlett, 2009 , Chapter 11) . ∎ 

We will use the VC-dimension upper bound proved for Pfaffian networks 2 2 2 The notions of Pfaffian networks and the associated complexity parameters ( l , N , q , D , d poly , s ) (l,N,q,D,d_{\operatorname{poly}},s) are taken from (Karpinski and Macintyre, 1997 ) . As the required background is quite involved, we refer the reader to said paper for detailed definitions and explanations. in (Karpinski and Macintyre, 1997 , Section 4.2) . We restate it in the parameterization needed below. 
Theorem D.14 (Pseudo-dimension bound via VC bounds for Pfaffian networks (Karpinski and Macintyre, 1997 ) ) . 
Consider a feedforward Pfaffian network architecture A A in the sense of Karpinski and Macintyre ( 1997 , Section 4.2) , with the following complexity parameters: 

• 
l ∈ ℕ l\in\mathbb{N} : number of trainable real parameters (weights/biases); 


• 
N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N} : number of computation nodes (non-input nodes); 


• 
d poly ∈ ℕ d_{\operatorname{poly}}\in\mathbb{N} : an upper bound on the degree of each node polynomial; 


• 
s ∈ ℕ s\in\mathbb{N} : an upper bound on the number of monomials in each node polynomial; 


• 
q ∈ ℕ q\in\mathbb{N} and D ∈ ℕ D\in\mathbb{N} : Pfaffian chain length and degree controlling all activation functions used by the architecture (as in Karpinski and Macintyre ( 1997 , Section 4.2) ). 


Let ℱ ​ ( A ) ⊂ { f : ℝ d → ℝ } \mathcal{F}(A)\subset\{f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\} be the resulting real-valued function class computed by A A (as the l l parameters vary freely in ℝ l \mathbb{R}^{l} ). 

Define 

Ψ ​ ( l , N , q , D , d poly , s ) := l ​ N ​ q ​ ( l ​ N ​ q − 1 ) + ( 16 + 2 ​ log 2 ⁡ d poly + 2 ​ log 2 ⁡ s ) ​ l + 2 ​ ( l + 2 ​ l ​ N ​ q ) ​ log 2 ⁡ l + 2 ​ ( l + 2 ​ l ​ N ​ q ) ​ log 2 ⁡ ( d poly + D ) . \Psi(l,N,q,D,d_{\operatorname{poly}},s):=lNq(lNq-1)+(16+2\log_{2}d_{\operatorname{poly}}+2\log_{2}s)\,l+2(l+2lNq)\log_{2}l+2(l+2lNq)\log_{2}(d_{\operatorname{poly}}+D). 

Then 

Pdim ​ ( ℱ ​ ( A ) ) ≤ Ψ ​ ( l , N + 1 , q , D , d poly , s + 1 ) . \mathrm{Pdim}\bigl(\mathcal{F}(A)\bigr)\;\leq\;\Psi\bigl(l,\,N+1,\,q,\,D,\,d_{\operatorname{poly}},\,s+1\bigr). 
Proof. 
By Lemma D.13 , 

Pdim ​ ( ℱ ​ ( A ) ) = VCdim ​ ( thresh ⁡ ( ℱ ​ ( A ) ) ) . \mathrm{Pdim}\bigl(\mathcal{F}(A)\bigr)=\mathrm{VCdim}\bigl(\operatorname{thresh}(\mathcal{F}(A))\bigr). 

Now consider the augmented architecture A sub A^{\mathrm{sub}} with one additional input t ∈ ℝ t\in\mathbb{R} and output gate computing the Boolean predicate 

( x , t ) ↦ 𝟏 ​ { f ​ ( x ) − t > 0 } , where ​ f ∈ ℱ ​ ( A ) . (x,t)\mapsto\mathbf{1}\{f(x)-t>0\},\ \text{ where }f\in\mathcal{F}(A). 

Concretely, A sub A^{\mathrm{sub}} is obtained by taking the original output polynomial and adding the extra term ( − t ) (-t) . This adds no trainable parameters, increases the number of computation nodes by at most 1 1 (if the subtraction is implemented as a separate final node), and increases the monomial count bound by at most 1 1 . Thus thresh ⁡ ( ℱ ​ ( A ) ) \operatorname{thresh}(\mathcal{F}(A)) is realized by a Pfaffian network with parameters bounded by ( l , N + 1 , d poly , s + 1 , q , D ) (l,\,N+1,\,d_{\operatorname{poly}},\,s+1,\,q,\,D) . 

Applying the VC-dimension upper bound for Pfaffian networks from Karpinski and Macintyre ( 1997 , Section 4.2, end of section) to A sub A^{\mathrm{sub}} yields 

VCdim ​ ( thresh ⁡ ( ℱ ​ ( A ) ) ) ≤ Ψ ​ ( l , N + 1 , q , D , d poly , s + 1 ) , \mathrm{VCdim}\bigl(\operatorname{thresh}(\mathcal{F}(A))\bigr)\leq\Psi\bigl(l,\,N+1,\,q,\,D,\,d_{\operatorname{poly}},\,s+1\bigr), 

and the result follows. ∎ 
Corollary D.15 (Implication of Theorem D.14 for fixed activations) . 
Assume q , D , d poly q,D,d_{\operatorname{poly}} are absolute constants and s ≤ poly ​ ( N ) s\leq\mathrm{poly}(N) . Then there exists a constant C > 0 C>0 such that, for all l , N ≥ 2 l,N\geq 2 , 

Pdim ​ ( ℱ ​ ( A ) ) ≤ C ​ ( l ​ N ) 2 ​ log ⁡ ( l ​ N ) . \mathrm{Pdim}\bigl(\mathcal{F}(A)\bigr)\leq C\,(lN)^{2}\log(lN). 

We next need a lemma on the representation of products with analytic networks. 
Lemma D.16 . 
Let φ sq ​ ( t ) = t 2 \varphi_{\mathrm{sq}}(t)=t^{2} . In a feedforward architecture allowing tanh \tanh and φ sq \varphi_{\mathrm{sq}} hidden units and a linear output, there exists a fixed constant-size subnetwork that maps two scalar inputs ( a , b ) ∈ ℝ 2 (a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2} to the product a ​ b ab exactly. 
Proof. 
The identity a ​ b = 1 2 ​ ( ( a + b ) 2 − a 2 − b 2 ) ab=\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl((a+b)^{2}-a^{2}-b^{2}\bigr) expresses multiplication using one addition, three squaring operations, and one final affine combination. These operations can be implemented by a constant number of units with fixed coefficients, independent of the surrounding network. ∎ 

The next lemma shows that derivatives of u 𝜽 u_{\bm{\theta}} with respect to the input variables can be represented by analytic networks with the same depth and with at most a constant factor more parameters. 
Lemma D.17 . 
Fix an architecture ( m ℓ ) ℓ = 0 L (m_{\ell})_{\ell=0}^{L} with m 0 = d m_{0}=d and m L = 1 m_{L}=1 , and write 

P := ∑ ℓ = 1 L ( m ℓ ​ m ℓ − 1 + m ℓ ) , N hid := ∑ ℓ = 1 L − 1 m ℓ . P:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(m_{\ell}m_{\ell-1}+m_{\ell}),\ \ N_{\mathrm{hid}}:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L-1}m_{\ell}. 

Let u θ u_{\theta} be the corresponding fully connected tanh \tanh network (with linear output) on Ω \Omega . 

For every multi-index α ∈ ℕ 0 d \alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d} with 1 ≤ | α | ≤ 2 1\leq|\alpha|\leq 2 , there exists a feedforward Pfaffian network architecture A α A_{\alpha} (in the sense of Karpinski and Macintyre ( 1997 , Section 4.2) ) with the following properties: 

1. 
( Parameter sharing ) The set of trainable parameters of A α A_{\alpha} is exactly the original parameter vector θ ∈ ℝ P \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{P} (no additional free parameters). 


2. 
( Exact derivative ) For every θ \theta and every x ∈ Ω x\in\Omega , 

A α ​ ( x ; θ ) = D α ​ u θ ​ ( x ) . A_{\alpha}(x;\theta)=D^{\alpha}u_{\theta}(x). 


3. 
( Computation nodes ) The number N α N_{\alpha} of computation nodes of A α A_{\alpha} satisfies 

N α ≤ C d , | α | ​ N hid + 3 , N_{\alpha}\leq C_{d,|\alpha|}\,N_{\mathrm{hid}}+3, 

where one may take 

C d , 1 := 3 + 5 ​ d , C d , 2 := 8 + 5 ​ d + 13 2 ​ d ​ ( d + 1 ) . C_{d,1}:=3+5d,\ \ C_{d,2}:=8+5d+\frac{13}{2}d(d+1). 

Proof. 
Write the base tanh \tanh network as a feedforward computation graph with affine maps, tanh \tanh nonlinearities, and a final linear output. This fits the Pfaffian network framework of (Karpinski and Macintyre, 1997 ) because affine maps are polynomials and tanh \tanh is Pfaffian. 

Fix a hidden neuron with pre-activation a a and activation z = tanh ⁡ ( a ) z=\tanh(a) . Using only tanh \tanh , squaring, affine combinations, and the exact multiplication subnetwork from Lemma D.16 , we can compute z 2 z^{2} , s := 1 − z 2 s:=1-z^{2} , and t := − 2 ​ z + 2 ​ z 3 t:=-2z+2z^{3} . The identities tanh ′ ⁡ ( a ) = s \tanh^{\prime}(a)=s and tanh ′′ ⁡ ( a ) = t \tanh^{\prime\prime}(a)=t then hold exactly. 

We construct A α A_{\alpha} by propagating first and second input derivatives through the graph using the chain rule. For each input coordinate i ∈ { 1 , … , d } i\in\{1,\dots,d\} , ∂ i a \partial_{i}a is an affine combination of derivatives from the previous layer with coefficients given by the original weights, and ∂ i z = s ​ ∂ i a \partial_{i}z=s\,\partial_{i}a is obtained using one multiplication subnetwork. Similarly, for 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ d 1\leq i\leq j\leq d , ∂ i ​ j a \partial_{ij}a is affine in previous-layer derivatives and 

∂ i ​ j z = t ​ ( ∂ i a ) ​ ( ∂ j a ) + s ​ ∂ i ​ j a \partial_{ij}z=t\,(\partial_{i}a)(\partial_{j}a)+s\,\partial_{ij}a 

is implemented using three multiplications and one affine combination. All trainable coefficients are reused from θ \theta , while any additional coefficients introduced by squaring, addition, and the product construction are fixed constants. This gives parameter sharing and the exact derivative identity. 

It remains to bound the number of computation nodes. Using the concrete multiplication construction of Lemma D.16 , one multiplication can be implemented with a constant number of computation nodes, and we may upper bound this constant by 4 4 . For each hidden neuron, computing z z , z 2 z^{2} , and s = 1 − z 2 s=1-z^{2} costs 3 3 nodes. For each i i , computing ∂ i a \partial_{i}a costs one affine node and computing ∂ i z = s ​ ∂ i a \partial_{i}z=s\,\partial_{i}a costs at most 4 4 nodes, for a total of 5 ​ d 5d nodes. For second derivatives, computing z 3 z^{3} and t = − 2 ​ z + 2 ​ z 3 t=-2z+2z^{3} costs at most 5 5 additional nodes. For each pair 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ d 1\leq i\leq j\leq d , the update for ∂ i ​ j z \partial_{ij}z uses one affine node for ∂ i ​ j a \partial_{ij}a and at most 3 3 multiplications, hence at most 1 + 3 ⋅ 4 = 13 1+3\cdot 4=13 nodes per pair. Since there are d ​ ( d + 1 ) / 2 d(d+1)/2 such pairs, the per-neuron cost is bounded by C d , 1 = 3 + 5 ​ d C_{d,1}=3+5d for first derivatives and by C d , 2 = 8 + 5 ​ d + 13 2 ​ d ​ ( d + 1 ) C_{d,2}=8+5d+\tfrac{13}{2}d(d+1) for derivatives up to order 2 2 . Multiplying by N hid N_{\mathrm{hid}} and adding a constant number of output nodes yields the claimed bound on N α N_{\alpha} . ∎ 

We now define, for k ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } k\in\{0,1,2\} , the derivative classes 

ℱ ( k ) := { D α ​ u 𝜽 : u 𝜽 ∈ 𝒰 L , m , B , α ∈ ℕ 0 d , | α | = k } , \mathcal{F}^{(k)}:=\Bigl\{D^{\alpha}u_{\bm{\theta}}:u_{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathcal{U}_{L,m,B},\ \alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\ |\alpha|=k\Bigr\}, (D.25) 

with the convention that D 0 ​ u 𝜽 = u 𝜽 D^{0}u_{\bm{\theta}}=u_{\bm{\theta}} . Lemma D.17 and Corollary D.15 together give the following quantitative bound. 
Proposition D.18 (Pseudo-dimension of tanh \tanh networks and their up-to-order-two derivatives) . 
Let 𝒰 L , m , B \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B} be as in ( D.15 ) with architecture ( m ℓ ) ℓ = 0 L (m_{\ell})_{\ell=0}^{L} , and define 

P := ∑ ℓ = 1 L ( m ℓ ​ m ℓ − 1 + m ℓ ) , N hid := ∑ ℓ = 1 L − 1 m ℓ , and ​ N k := C d , k ​ N hid + 3 , P:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(m_{\ell}m_{\ell-1}+m_{\ell}),\ \ N_{\mathrm{hid}}:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L-1}m_{\ell},\ \text{ and }N_{k}:=C_{d,k}\,N_{\mathrm{hid}}+3, 

with 

C d , 0 := 1 , C d , 1 := 3 + 5 ​ d , and ​ C d , 2 := 8 + 5 ​ d + 13 2 ​ d ​ ( d + 1 ) . C_{d,0}:=1,\ \ C_{d,1}:=3+5d,\ \text{ and }C_{d,2}:=8+5d+\frac{13}{2}d(d+1). 

Then there exists a constant C pdim > 0 C_{\mathrm{pdim}}>0 depending only on d d and on the activation family (ultimately tanh \tanh ) such that for each k ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } k\in\{0,1,2\} , 

Pdim ​ ( ℱ ( k ) ) ≤ C pdim ​ ( P ​ N k ) 2 ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N k ) . \mathrm{Pdim}\bigl(\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\bigr)\leq C_{\mathrm{pdim}}\,(PN_{k})^{2}\log(PN_{k}). 
Proof. 
Fix k ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } k\in\{0,1,2\} and a multi-index α \alpha with | α | = k |\alpha|=k . By Lemma D.17 , the class ℱ ( α ) := { D α ​ u θ : θ ∈ Θ L , m } \mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}:=\{D^{\alpha}u_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{L,m}\} is contained in the Pfaffian network class ℱ ​ ( A α ) \mathcal{F}(A_{\alpha}) of an architecture with l = P l=P trainable parameters and at most N k N_{k} computation nodes. Hence 

Pdim ​ ( ℱ ( α ) ) ≤ Pdim ​ ( ℱ ​ ( A α ) ) . \mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)})\leq\mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F}(A_{\alpha})). 

Applying Corollary D.15 (a consequence of Theorem D.14 ) gives 

Pdim ​ ( ℱ ( α ) ) ≤ C ​ ( P ​ N k ) 2 ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N k ) \mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)})\leq C(PN_{k})^{2}\log(PN_{k}) 

for a constant C C depending only on the fixed activation family. 

Finally, ℱ ( k ) = ⋃ | α | = k ℱ ( α ) \mathcal{F}^{(k)}=\bigcup_{|\alpha|=k}\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)} is a finite union over ( d + k − 1 k ) \binom{d+k-1}{k} multi-indices. Using the standard finite-union bound for pseudo-dimension (Anthony and Bartlett, 2009 , Section 11.2) and absorbing ( d + k − 1 k ) \binom{d+k-1}{k} into the constant yields the claim. ∎ 

Proposition D.18 yields, to the best of our knowledge, the first rigorous bounds for first and second order derivatives of tanh \tanh networks. Together with Lemma D.11 and Proposition D.12 , it yields the Rademacher complexity bounds for the PDE, boundary and FK losses stated later in this appendix. 

D.4.3 Finite-sample bound on the statistical error 

We now bound ℰ stat \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}} using the pseudo-dimension estimates from Proposition D.18 . Recall that 

ℛ ​ ( u ) = λ PDE ​ ℛ PDE ​ ( u ) + λ ∂ Ω ​ ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) + λ FK ​ ℛ FK ​ ( u ) , \mathcal{R}(u)=\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u)+\lambda_{\partial\Omega}\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(u)+\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u), 

and that ℛ ^ \widehat{\mathcal{R}} is defined in ( D.8 ). We first separate the contributions of the three components. 
Lemma D.19 (Decomposition of the statistical error) . 
For every hypothesis class 𝒰 \mathcal{U} , 

ℰ stat ≤ λ PDE ​ ℰ stat PDE + λ ∂ Ω ​ ℰ stat ∂ Ω + λ FK ​ ℰ stat FK , \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{PDE}}+\lambda_{\partial\Omega}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\partial\Omega}+\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{FK}}, 

where 

ℰ stat PDE \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{PDE}} := sup u ∈ 𝒰 | ℛ PDE ​ ( u ) − ℛ ^ PDE ​ ( u ) | , \displaystyle:=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\Bigl|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u)-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u)\Bigr|, 

ℰ stat ∂ Ω \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\partial\Omega} := sup u ∈ 𝒰 | ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) − ℛ ^ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) | , \displaystyle:=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\Bigl|\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(u)-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\partial\Omega}(u)\Bigr|, 

ℰ stat FK \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{FK}} := sup u ∈ 𝒰 | ℛ FK ​ ( u ) − ℛ ^ FK ​ ( u ) | . \displaystyle:=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\Bigl|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u)-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u)\Bigr|. 
Proof. 
This is an immediate consequence of the triangle inequality and the linearity of ℛ \mathcal{R} and ℛ ^ \widehat{\mathcal{R}} in their three components. ∎ 
Lemma D.20 (Pseudo-dimension and fixed multipliers / finite sums) . 
Let ℱ ⊂ { f : 𝒵 → ℝ } \mathcal{F}\subset\{f:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}\} be a function class with Pdim ​ ( ℱ ) = d < ∞ \mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F})=d<\infty and let g : 𝒵 → ℝ g:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} be a fixed (non-random) function. Then 

Pdim ​ ( g ​ ℱ ) := Pdim ​ ( { z ↦ g ​ ( z ) ​ f ​ ( z ) : f ∈ ℱ } ) ≤ d . \mathrm{Pdim}\bigl(g\mathcal{F}\bigr):=\mathrm{Pdim}\bigl(\{z\mapsto g(z)f(z):f\in\mathcal{F}\}\bigr)\leq d. 

Moreover, if ℱ 1 , … , ℱ m \mathcal{F}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{F}_{m} are function classes with pseudo-dimensions d j := Pdim ​ ( ℱ j ) d_{j}:=\mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F}_{j}) , and 

ℱ sum := { z ↦ ∑ j = 1 m f j ​ ( z ) : f j ∈ ℱ j } , \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{sum}}:=\Bigl\{\,z\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}(z)\;:\;f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{j}\,\Bigr\}, 

then there exists a constant C m > 0 C_{m}>0 , depending only on m m , such that 

Pdim ​ ( ℱ sum ) ≤ C m ​ ∑ j = 1 m d j . \mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{sum}})\leq C_{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}d_{j}. 
Proof. 
For the first claim, fix a finite sample z 1 , … , z n z_{1},\dots,z_{n} and thresholds r 1 , … , r n ∈ ℝ r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\in\mathbb{R} . If we replace the class ℱ \mathcal{F} by g ​ ℱ g\mathcal{F} , the sign of g ​ ( z i ) ​ f ​ ( z i ) − r i g(z_{i})f(z_{i})-r_{i} at each point z i z_{i} is determined by the sign of f ​ ( z i ) − r i / g ​ ( z i ) f(z_{i})-r_{i}/g(z_{i}) whenever g ​ ( z i ) ≠ 0 g(z_{i})\neq 0 , while points with g ​ ( z i ) = 0 g(z_{i})=0 contribute no additional sign variability. Thus the number of sign patterns achievable by g ​ ℱ g\mathcal{F} on any finite sample is at most the number achievable by ℱ \mathcal{F} , so Pdim ​ ( g ​ ℱ ) ≤ Pdim ​ ( ℱ ) \mathrm{Pdim}(g\mathcal{F})\leq\mathrm{Pdim}(\mathcal{F}) . 

The bound for finite sums is a special case of the general pseudo-dimension bound for linear combinations of real-valued function classes as shown by Anthony and Bartlett ( 2009 , Chapter 11) . Since the number m m of summands is fixed in our setting, its contribution can be absorbed into the constant C m C_{m} . ∎ 

We treat each term in Lemma D.19 with Lemma D.10 applied to an appropriate function class. We illustrate the argument for ℰ stat PDE \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{PDE}} ; the remaining terms follow in the same way. 

Define 

ℱ PDE := { x ↦ | Ω | ​ ( ℒ ​ u ​ ( x ) − f ​ ( x ) ) 2 : u ∈ 𝒰 } . \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{PDE}}:=\Bigl\{x\mapsto\lvert\Omega\rvert\,\bigl(\mathcal{L}u(x)-f(x)\bigr)^{2}:u\in\mathcal{U}\Bigr\}. 

Define the (unsquared) residual class 

𝒢 PDE := { x ↦ ℒ ​ u ​ ( x ) − f ​ ( x ) : u ∈ 𝒰 } . {\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{PDE}}:=\Bigl\{x\mapsto\mathcal{L}u(x)-f(x):u\in\mathcal{U}\Bigr\}. 

By the bounds above we have 

sup g ∈ 𝒢 PDE sup x ∈ Ω | g ​ ( x ) | ≤ C PDE . \sup_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{PDE}}}\sup_{x\in\Omega}\lvert g(x)\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{PDE}}. 

The mapping u ↦ ℒ ​ u u\mapsto\mathcal{L}u is linear in u u and involves only first and second order derivatives. In particular, every g ∈ 𝒢 PDE g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{PDE}} can be written as a finite linear combination (with fixed coefficient functions given by the coefficients of ℒ \mathcal{L} and by − f -f ) of functions from the derivative classes ℱ ( 0 ) , ℱ ( 1 ) , ℱ ( 2 ) \mathcal{F}^{(0)},\mathcal{F}^{(1)},\mathcal{F}^{(2)} defined in ( D.25 ). Note that for every g ∈ 𝒢 PDE g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{PDE}} the function 

x ↦ | Ω | ​ g ​ ( x ) 2 x\mapsto\lvert\Omega\rvert\,g(x)^{2} 

belongs to ℱ PDE \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{PDE}} , and the mapping t ↦ | Ω | ​ t 2 t\mapsto\lvert\Omega\rvert\,t^{2} is 2 ​ | Ω | ​ C PDE 2\lvert\Omega\rvert C_{\mathrm{PDE}} -Lipschitz on [ − C PDE , C PDE ] [-C_{\mathrm{PDE}},C_{\mathrm{PDE}}] . Hence, by the contraction inequality in Lemma D.10 , 

ℜ N int ​ ( ℱ PDE ) ≤ 2 ​ | Ω | ​ C PDE ​ ℜ N int ​ ( 𝒢 PDE ) . \mathfrak{R}_{N_{\mathrm{int}}}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{PDE}})\leq 2\lvert\Omega\rvert C_{\mathrm{PDE}}\,\mathfrak{R}_{N_{\mathrm{int}}}({\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{PDE}}). 

To bound ℜ N int ​ ( 𝒢 PDE ) \mathfrak{R}_{N_{\mathrm{int}}}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{PDE}}) it suffices to control the Rademacher complexities of the derivative classes ℱ ( 0 ) , ℱ ( 1 ) , ℱ ( 2 ) \mathcal{F}^{(0)},\mathcal{F}^{(1)},\mathcal{F}^{(2)} . Indeed, writing ℒ \mathcal{L} in coordinates as 

ℒ ​ u ​ ( x ) = ∑ | α | ≤ 2 c α ​ ( x ) ​ D α ​ u ​ ( x ) , \mathcal{L}u(x)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}c_{\alpha}(x)\,D^{\alpha}u(x), 

with bounded coefficient functions c α c_{\alpha} , we have for any fixed sample x 1 , … , x N int x_{1},\dots,x_{N_{\mathrm{int}}} , 

ℜ N int ​ ( 𝒢 PDE ∣ x 1 : N int ) ≤ ∑ | α | ≤ 2 ‖ c α ‖ L ∞ ​ ( Ω ) ​ ℜ N int ​ ( ℱ ( | α | ) ∣ x 1 : N int ) , \mathfrak{R}_{N_{\mathrm{int}}}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{PDE}}\mid x_{1:N_{\mathrm{int}}})\leq\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}\|c_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,\mathfrak{R}_{N_{\mathrm{int}}}(\mathcal{F}^{(|\alpha|)}\mid x_{1:N_{\mathrm{int}}}), 

and the constant shift − f -f does not affect Rademacher complexity. By Proposition D.18 (with k ≤ 2 k\leq 2 ) and the Dudley/covering-number bounds, this yields 

ℜ N int ​ ( 𝒢 PDE ) ≲ ( P ​ N 2 ) ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 2 ) N int , \mathfrak{R}_{N_{\mathrm{int}}}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{PDE}})\;\lesssim\;(PN_{2})\sqrt{\frac{\log(PN_{2})}{N_{\mathrm{int}}}}, 

up to logarithmic factors and constants depending only on ( d , L ) (d,L) and the coefficients of ℒ \mathcal{L} . 

Applying Lemma D.10 , Lemma D.11 and Proposition D.12 to ℱ PDE \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{PDE}} , together with the bound ℜ N int ​ ( 𝒢 PDE ) ≲ ( P ​ N 2 ) ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 2 ) / N int \mathfrak{R}_{N_{\mathrm{int}}}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{PDE}})\lesssim(PN_{2})\sqrt{\log(PN_{2})/N_{\mathrm{int}}} , yields the following bound. 
Lemma D.21 (Statistical error for the PDE residual) . 
There exist constants C PDE , 1 , C PDE , 2 > 0 C_{\mathrm{PDE},1},C_{\mathrm{PDE},2}>0 , depending only on ( d , L , m , B ) (d,L,m,B) and on the coefficients of ℒ \mathcal{L} , such that for every δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) , with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , 

ℰ stat PDE ≤ C PDE , 1 ​ | Ω | ​ C PDE 2 ​ ( P ​ N 2 ) 2 ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 2 ) + log ⁡ ( 2 / δ ) N int + C PDE , 2 ​ | Ω | ​ C PDE 2 ​ ( P ​ N 2 ) 2 ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 2 ) + log ⁡ ( 2 / δ ) N int . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{PDE}}\leq C_{\mathrm{PDE},1}\lvert\Omega\rvert C_{\mathrm{PDE}}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{(PN_{2})^{2}\log(PN_{2})+\log(2/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{int}}}}+C_{\mathrm{PDE},2}\lvert\Omega\rvert C_{\mathrm{PDE}}^{2}\frac{(PN_{2})^{2}\log(PN_{2})+\log(2/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{int}}}. 

The boundary term ℰ stat ∂ Ω \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\partial\Omega} is handled analogously, using that the boundary loss involves only u 𝜽 u_{\bm{\theta}} itself and no derivatives. In this case the relevant function class is generated by ℱ ( 0 ) \mathcal{F}^{(0)} and the same argument shows that 

ℰ stat ∂ Ω ≲ | ∂ Ω | ​ C ∂ Ω 2 ​ ( P ​ N 0 ) ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 0 ) + log ⁡ ( 1 / δ ) N ∂ Ω . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\partial\Omega}\lesssim\lvert\partial\Omega\rvert C_{\partial\Omega}^{2}(PN_{0})\sqrt{\frac{\log(PN_{0})+\log(1/\delta)}{N_{\partial\Omega}}}. (D.26) 

for a suitable constant C ∂ Ω C_{\partial\Omega} . 

The FK term requires a small modification. Recall that ℛ FK ​ ( u ) \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u) is defined using the true solution u ⋆ u^{\star} in ( D.3 ), while ℛ ^ FK ​ ( u ) \widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{FK}}(u) is defined with the noisy labels u ^ MC ​ ( x k FK ) \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}) . Writing 

u ^ MC ​ ( x k FK ) = u ⋆ ​ ( x k FK ) + b ​ ( x k FK ) + ζ k , \widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})=u^{\star}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})+b(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})+\zeta_{k}, 

with b b and ζ k \zeta_{k} as in Proposition 6.1 , we deduce 

ℰ stat FK \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{FK}} = sup u ∈ 𝒰 | 𝔼 ​ [ ( u ​ ( X FK ) − u ⋆ ​ ( X FK ) ) 2 ] − 1 N FK ​ ∑ k = 1 N FK ( u ​ ( x k FK ) − u ^ MC ​ ( x k FK ) ) 2 | \displaystyle=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\Biggl|\mathbb{E}\bigl[(u(X^{\mathrm{FK}})-u^{\star}(X^{\mathrm{FK}}))^{2}\bigr]-\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\bigl(u(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})-\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})\bigr)^{2}\Biggr| 

≤ sup u ∈ 𝒰 | 𝔼 ​ [ ( u ​ ( X FK ) − u ⋆ ​ ( X FK ) ) 2 ] − 1 N FK ​ ∑ k = 1 N FK ( u ​ ( x k FK ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x k FK ) ) 2 | \displaystyle\leq\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\Biggl|\mathbb{E}\bigl[(u(X^{\mathrm{FK}})-u^{\star}(X^{\mathrm{FK}}))^{2}\bigr]-\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\bigl(u(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})-u^{\star}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})\bigr)^{2}\Biggr| 

+ sup u ∈ 𝒰 | 1 N FK ​ ∑ k = 1 N FK [ ( u ​ ( x k FK ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x k FK ) ) 2 − ( u ​ ( x k FK ) − u ^ MC ​ ( x k FK ) ) 2 ] | \displaystyle\quad+\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\Biggl|\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\bigl[(u(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})-u^{\star}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}))^{2}-(u(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})-\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}))^{2}\bigr]\Biggr| 

= : A FK + B FK . \displaystyle=:A_{\mathrm{FK}}+B_{\mathrm{FK}}. 

The term A FK A_{\mathrm{FK}} can be bounded exactly as in Lemma D.21 , now using only the class ℱ ( 0 ) \mathcal{F}^{(0)} on Ω \Omega , since it is an empirical-process deviation for the squared error with respect to u ⋆ u^{\star} . For B FK B_{\mathrm{FK}} we expand the difference of squares and use that 

( u − u ⋆ ) 2 − ( u − u ^ MC ) 2 = 2 ​ ( u − u ⋆ ) ​ ( b ​ ( x k FK ) + ζ k ) − ( b ​ ( x k FK ) + ζ k ) 2 . (u-u^{\star})^{2}-(u-\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{MC}})^{2}=2\,(u-u^{\star})\bigl(b(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})+\zeta_{k}\bigr)-\bigl(b(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})+\zeta_{k}\bigr)^{2}. 

Using ∥ u − u ⋆ ∥ C ​ ( Ω ¯ ) ≤ 2 ​ B \lVert u-u^{\star}\rVert_{C(\overline{\Omega})}\leq 2B , the bias bound in Proposition 6.1 , and the inequality | 1 N FK ​ ∑ k = 1 N FK a k | ≤ sup 1 ≤ k ≤ N FK | a k | \bigl|\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}a_{k}\bigr|\leq\sup_{1\leq k\leq N_{\mathrm{FK}}}|a_{k}| , we obtain the deterministic estimate 

sup u ∈ 𝒰 | 1 N FK ​ ∑ k = 1 N FK ( 2 ​ ( u ​ ( x k FK ) − u ⋆ ​ ( x k FK ) ) ​ b ​ ( x k FK ) − b ​ ( x k FK ) 2 ) | ≤ 4 ​ B ​ ∥ b ∥ L ∞ ​ ( Ω ) + ∥ b ∥ L ∞ ​ ( Ω ) 2 . \sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\Biggl|\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\Bigl(2\,(u(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})-u^{\star}(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}}))\,b(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})-b(x_{k}^{\mathrm{FK}})^{2}\Bigr)\Biggr|\leq 4B\,\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{2}. 

Define the Monte Carlo bias level 

ε bias := C bias ​ Δ ​ t + C T ​ e − κ ​ T max , \varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}:=C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t}+C_{T}e^{-\kappa T_{\max}}, 

so that ∥ b ∥ L ∞ ​ ( Ω ) ≤ ε bias \lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}} . We keep the contribution 4 ​ B ​ ε bias + ε bias 2 4B\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}^{2} explicit in the final bound. 

The remaining stochastic contribution in B FK B_{\mathrm{FK}} involves the mean-zero sub-exponential fluctuations ζ k \zeta_{k} . A Bernstein-type bound yields a term of order N FK − 1 / 2 N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-1/2} up to logarithmic factors, as shown in Appendix C . The genuinely stochastic part, involving the ζ k \zeta_{k} , is mean-zero and sub-exponential, and a standard Bernstein-type argument yields a contribution of order 𝒪 ​ ( N FK − 1 / 2 ) \mathcal{O}(N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-1/2}) (up to logarithmic factors), as we show in Appendix C . 

Combining these ingredients we obtain the following finite-sample bound on the full statistical error. 
Proposition D.22 (Finite-sample statistical error bound) . 
Under Assumption D.1 and the standing assumptions on 𝒰 \mathcal{U} , there exist constants C stat , C FK > 0 C_{\mathrm{stat}},C_{\mathrm{FK}}>0 such that for every δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) , with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , 

ℰ stat \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}} ≤ C stat ​ [ ( P ​ N 2 ) ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 2 ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N int + ( P ​ N 0 ) ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 0 ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N ∂ Ω ] \displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{stat}}\Biggl[(PN_{2})\sqrt{\frac{\log(PN_{2})+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{int}}}}+(PN_{0})\sqrt{\frac{\log(PN_{0})+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\partial\Omega}}}\Biggr] 

+ C FK ​ [ ( P ​ N 0 ) ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 0 ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N FK + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N FK + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N FK ] + C bias ​ ( ε bias + ε bias 2 ) , \displaystyle\quad+C_{\mathrm{FK}}\Biggl[(PN_{0})\sqrt{\frac{\log(PN_{0})+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}}+\frac{\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\Biggr]+C_{\mathrm{bias}}\bigl(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}^{2}\bigr), (D.27) 

where ε bias := C bias ​ Δ ​ t + C T ​ e − κ ​ T max \varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}:=C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t}+C_{T}e^{-\kappa T_{\max}} is the FK Monte Carlo bias level from Proposition 6.1 . In particular, for fixed network size P P and fixed confidence level, the first bracket in ( D.27 ) tends to zero as N int , N ∂ Ω → ∞ N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega}\to\infty , while the second bracket decays only as N FK − 1 / 2 N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-1/2} (up to logarithmic factors). Thus, when N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} is small or remains bounded, the FK contribution controls the asymptotic order of ℰ stat \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}} and induces a nonvanishing statistical floor of order 

ℰ stat ≳ ( P ​ N 2 ) ​ log ⁡ ( P ​ N 2 ) N FK . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}\gtrsim(PN_{2})\sqrt{\frac{\log(PN_{2})}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}}. 

The dependence on log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) \log(6/\delta) in ( D.27 ) comes from a simple union bound. We first apply Lemma D.21 to control ℰ stat PDE \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{PDE}} with failure probability at most δ / 3 \delta/3 by replacing log ⁡ ( 2 / δ ) \log(2/\delta) there with log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) \log(6/\delta) . The same argument applied to the boundary term ℰ stat ∂ Ω \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\partial\Omega} yields an analogous bound, again with failure probability at most δ / 3 \delta/3 . Finally, the FK contribution ℰ stat FK \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{FK}} is controlled in the same way, with its own failure probability bounded by δ / 3 \delta/3 and with the same replacement of log ⁡ ( 2 / δ ) \log(2/\delta) by log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) \log(6/\delta) . A union bound over these three events shows that, with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , all three component bounds hold simultaneously, which implies ( D.27 ) via Lemma D.19 . 

D.5 Putting it all together: non-asymptotic error bounds for FK-PINNs 

We are now ready to combine the approximation, statistical and optimization bounds to prove the non-asymptotic error estimate announced in Theorem 6.2 . 

Let u ^ ∈ 𝒰 \widehat{u}\in\mathcal{U} be an empirical minimizer of ℛ ^ \widehat{\mathcal{R}} and let u 𝜽 T u_{{\bm{\theta}}_{T}} be the output of T T steps of gradient descent on ℓ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = ℛ ^ ​ ( u 𝜽 ) \ell(\bm{\theta})=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{\bm{\theta}}) starting from 𝜽 0 \bm{\theta}_{0} as in Corollary D.8 . We write 

ℰ tot ​ ( T ) := ∥ u 𝜽 T − u ⋆ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{tot}}(T):=\lVert u_{\bm{\theta}_{T}}-u^{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}. 
Theorem D.23 (Non-asymptotic error bound for FK-PINNs) . 
Suppose that Assumption D.1 holds and that the assumptions of Corollary D.8 are satisfied, and that u ⋆ ∈ W s , ∞ ​ ( Ω ) u^{\star}\in W^{s,\infty}(\Omega) for some integer s ≥ 3 s\geq 3 . Fix confidence level δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) . Then there exist constants C app , C stat , C FK , C opt , c opt > 0 C_{\mathrm{app}},C_{\mathrm{stat}},C_{\mathrm{FK}},C_{\mathrm{opt}},c_{\mathrm{opt}}>0 , independent of ( N int , N ∂ Ω , N FK ) (N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega},N_{\mathrm{FK}}) and of T T , such that with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta over the sampling of the collocation and FK points, 

ℰ tot ​ ( T ) \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{tot}}(T) ≤ C app ​ P − γ + C stat ​ [ ( P ​ N 2 ) 1 / 2 ​ ( log ⁡ ( P ​ N 2 ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N int ) 1 / 4 + ( P ​ N 0 ) 1 / 2 ​ ( log ⁡ ( P ​ N 0 ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N ∂ Ω ) 1 / 4 ] \displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{app}}P^{-\gamma}+C_{\mathrm{stat}}\Biggl[(PN_{2})^{1/2}\Biggl(\frac{\log(PN_{2})+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{int}}}\Biggr)^{1/4}+(PN_{0})^{1/2}\Biggl(\frac{\log(PN_{0})+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\partial\Omega}}\Biggr)^{1/4}\Biggr] (D.28) 

+ C FK ​ [ ( P ​ N 0 ) 1 / 2 ​ ( log ⁡ ( P ​ N 0 ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N FK ) 1 / 4 + ( log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N FK ) 1 / 4 + ( log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N FK ) 1 / 2 ] \displaystyle\quad+C_{\mathrm{FK}}\Biggl[(PN_{0})^{1/2}\Biggl(\frac{\log(PN_{0})+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\Biggr)^{1/4}+\Biggl(\frac{\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\Biggr)^{1/4}+\Biggl(\frac{\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}\Biggr)^{1/2}\Biggr] 

+ C bias ​ ( ε bias + ε bias 2 ) 1 / 2 + C opt ​ exp ⁡ ( − c opt ​ T / 2 ) . \displaystyle\quad+C_{\mathrm{bias}}\bigl(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}+C_{\mathrm{opt}}\exp(-c_{\mathrm{opt}}T/2). 

where γ = ( s − 2 ) / d \gamma=(s-2)/d and P P is the maximal number of parameters of networks in 𝒰 \mathcal{U} . The constants C app , C stat , C FK , C opt , c opt C_{\mathrm{app}},C_{\mathrm{stat}},C_{\mathrm{FK}},C_{\mathrm{opt}},c_{\mathrm{opt}} may depend on the task weights ( λ PDE , λ ∂ Ω , λ FK ) (\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}},\lambda_{\partial\Omega},\lambda_{\mathrm{FK}}) and on the PDE data, but not on the sample sizes ( N int , N ∂ Ω , N FK ) (N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega},N_{\mathrm{FK}}) nor on T T . 
Proof. 
Fix δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) and work on the event where the statistical bound ( D.27 ) of Proposition D.22 holds. Set 

u T := u 𝜽 T , u_{T}:=u_{{\bm{\theta}}_{T}}, 

let u ^ \widehat{u} be an empirical minimizer of ℛ ^ \widehat{\mathcal{R}} as in Lemma D.2 , and let u approx ∈ 𝒰 u^{\mathrm{approx}}\in\mathcal{U} be the approximating network from Corollary D.5 . 

First we control the population risk of u T u_{T} . Adding and subtracting empirical risks gives 

ℛ ​ ( u T ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{R}(u_{T})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star}) = [ ℛ ​ ( u T ) − ℛ ^ ​ ( u T ) ] + [ ℛ ^ ​ ( u T ) − ℛ ^ ​ ( u ^ ) ] \displaystyle=\bigl[\mathcal{R}(u_{T})-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{T})\bigr]+\bigl[\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(u_{T})-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{u})\bigr] 

+ [ ℛ ^ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) ] + [ ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ] . \displaystyle\quad+\bigl[\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{u})-\mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})\bigr]+\bigl[\mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\bigr]. 

On the high probability event, the first and third brackets are bounded in absolute value by ℰ stat \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}} . The second bracket is exactly ℰ opt ​ ( T ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(T) by ( D.23 ). For the last bracket we apply Lemma D.2 with comparison function u approx u^{\mathrm{approx}} , which yields 

ℛ ​ ( u ^ ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ≤ 2 ​ ℰ stat + 2 ​ [ ℛ ​ ( u approx ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ] . \mathcal{R}(\widehat{u})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\leq 2\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}+2\,\bigl[\mathcal{R}(u^{\mathrm{approx}})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\bigr]. 

Since ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) = 0 \mathcal{R}(u^{\star})=0 and ℛ \mathcal{R} is nonnegative, this last term is at most 2 ​ ℛ ​ ( u approx ) 2\mathcal{R}(u^{\mathrm{approx}}) . Combining these bounds we arrive at 

ℛ ​ ( u T ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ≤ 4 ​ ℰ stat + ℰ opt ​ ( T ) + 2 ​ ℛ ​ ( u approx ) . \mathcal{R}(u_{T})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\leq 4\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}+\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(T)+2\,\mathcal{R}(u^{\mathrm{approx}}). (D.29) 

By Lemma D.6 we have ℛ ​ ( u approx ) ≤ C ~ risk ​ P − 2 ​ γ \mathcal{R}(u^{\mathrm{approx}})\leq\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{risk}}P^{-2\gamma} , so 

ℛ ​ ( u T ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ≤ 4 ​ ℰ stat + ℰ opt ​ ( T ) + C 1 ​ P − 2 ​ γ \mathcal{R}(u_{T})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\leq 4\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}+\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(T)+C_{1}P^{-2\gamma} (D.30) 

for a constant C 1 > 0 C_{1}>0 independent of the sample sizes and of P P . 

Next we use PDE stability. From ( D.4 ) and the nonnegativity of ℛ FK \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{FK}} we have, for every u u , 

λ min ​ ( ℛ PDE ​ ( u ) + ℛ ∂ Ω ​ ( u ) ) ≤ ℛ ​ ( u ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) , \lambda_{\min}\bigl(\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(u)+\mathcal{R}_{\partial\Omega}(u)\bigr)\leq\mathcal{R}(u)-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star}), 

with λ min = min ⁡ { λ PDE , λ ∂ Ω } \lambda_{\min}=\min\{\lambda_{\mathrm{PDE}},\lambda_{\partial\Omega}\} . Applying this with u = u T u=u_{T} in ( D.9 ) gives 

∥ u T − u ⋆ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) 2 ≤ C stab λ min ​ ( ℛ ​ ( u T ) − ℛ ​ ( u ⋆ ) ) . \lVert u_{T}-u^{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{stab}}}{\lambda_{\min}}\,\bigl(\mathcal{R}(u_{T})-\mathcal{R}(u^{\star})\bigr). (D.31) 

Substituting ( D.30 ) into ( D.31 ) we obtain 

∥ u T − u ⋆ ∥ L 2 ​ ( Ω ) 2 ≤ C 2 ​ ( ℰ stat + P − 2 ​ γ + ℰ opt ​ ( T ) ) , \lVert u_{T}-u^{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C_{2}\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}+P^{-2\gamma}+\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(T)\bigr), 

for a constant C 2 > 0 C_{2}>0 independent of ( N int , N ∂ Ω , N FK ) (N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega},N_{\mathrm{FK}}) and of T T . Taking square roots and using a + b + c ≤ a + b + c \sqrt{a+b+c}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}+\sqrt{c} gives 

ℰ tot ​ ( T ) ≤ C 3 ​ ( ℰ stat + P − γ + ℰ opt ​ ( T ) ) . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{tot}}(T)\leq C_{3}\Bigl(\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{stat}}}+P^{-\gamma}+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(T)}\Bigr). 

We now insert the statistical bound ( D.27 ) and the optimization bound ( D.24 ), which yields ℰ opt ​ ( T ) ≤ C opt ​ exp ⁡ ( − c opt ​ T / 2 ) \sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{opt}}(T)}\leq\sqrt{C_{\mathrm{opt}}}\,\exp(-c_{\mathrm{opt}}T/2) , and then absorb constants into C app , C stat , C FK , C opt , c opt C_{\mathrm{app}},C_{\mathrm{stat}},C_{\mathrm{FK}},C_{\mathrm{opt}},c_{\mathrm{opt}} . ∎ 
Remark D.24 (Error bound in terms of depth and width) . 
Recall that for networks in 𝒰 L , m , B \mathcal{U}_{L,m,B} the number of parameters satisfies 

P ​ ( L , m ) ≤ C arch ​ ( d ) ​ L ​ m 2 . P(L,m)\leq C_{\mathrm{arch}}(d)\,L\,m^{2}. 

Writing P ~ := C arch ​ ( d ) ​ L ​ m 2 \widetilde{P}:=C_{\mathrm{arch}}(d)\,L\,m^{2} and using log ⁡ P ≲ log ⁡ P ~ ≲ log ⁡ ( L ​ m ) \log P\lesssim\log\widetilde{P}\lesssim\log(Lm) , the bound ( D.28 ) can be rewritten (up to a change of the constants) as 

ℰ tot ​ ( T ) ≲ \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{tot}}(T)\;\lesssim\; ( L ​ m 2 ) − γ + L 2 ​ m 3 ​ log ⁡ ( L ​ m ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N int + L 2 ​ m 3 ​ log ⁡ ( L ​ m ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N ∂ Ω \displaystyle(Lm^{2})^{-\gamma}+L^{2}m^{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(Lm)+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{int}}}}+L^{2}m^{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(Lm)+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\partial\Omega}}} 

+ L 2 ​ m 3 ​ log ⁡ ( L ​ m ) + log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) N FK + exp ⁡ ( − c opt ​ T ) , \displaystyle+L^{2}m^{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(Lm)+\log(6/\delta)}{N_{\mathrm{FK}}}}+\exp(-c_{\mathrm{opt}}T), 

with γ = ( s − 2 ) / d \gamma=(s-2)/d . Moreover N hid = ∑ ℓ = 1 L − 1 m ℓ ≤ ( L − 1 ) ​ m N_{\mathrm{hid}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L-1}m_{\ell}\leq(L-1)m , hence N 2 ≤ ( C d , 2 ​ ( L − 1 ) ​ m + 3 ) ≲ d L ​ m N_{2}\leq(C_{d,2}(L-1)m+3)\lesssim_{d}Lm . Therefore ( P ​ N 2 ) ≲ d L 2 ​ m 3 (PN_{2})\lesssim_{d}L^{2}m^{3} . 
Corollary D.25 (Depth- 3 3 FK-PINN: optimal width and rate) . 
Assume the setting of Theorem D.23 and Corollary D.5 , with s ≥ 3 s\geq 3 and γ = ( s − 2 ) / d \gamma=(s-2)/d . Fix the depth to L = 3 L=3 . Using P ​ ( L , m ) ≲ m 2 P(L,m)\lesssim m^{2} in the master bound and suppressing logarithmic factors in the sample sizes and in m m , we may write, for any training time T ≥ 0 T\geq 0 , 

ℰ tot ​ ( T ) ≲ m − 2 ​ γ + m 3 / 2 ​ N int − 1 / 4 + m ​ N ∂ Ω − 1 / 4 + m ​ N FK − 1 / 4 + exp ⁡ ( − c opt ​ T / 2 ) + ( ε bias + ε bias 2 ) 1 / 2 , \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{tot}}(T)\;\lesssim\;m^{-2\gamma}+m^{3/2}N_{\mathrm{int}}^{-1/4}+m\,N_{\partial\Omega}^{-1/4}+m\,N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-1/4}+\exp(-c_{\mathrm{opt}}T/2)+\bigl(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}, 

where ε bias := C bias ​ Δ ​ t + C T ​ e − κ ​ T max \varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}:=C_{\mathrm{bias}}\sqrt{\Delta t}+C_{T}e^{-\kappa T_{\mathrm{max}}} . In the data-scarce FK regime (with N int N_{\mathrm{int}} and N ∂ Ω N_{\partial\Omega} large compared to N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} ), balance the approximation term m − 2 ​ γ m^{-2\gamma} with the FK term m ​ N FK − 1 / 4 m\,N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-1/4} . This gives the width 

m ≍ N FK 1 4 ​ ( 1 + 2 ​ γ ) = N FK d 4 ​ d + 8 ​ ( s − 2 ) . m\;\asymp\;N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{\frac{1}{4(1+2\gamma)}}=N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{\frac{d}{4d+8(s-2)}}. 

For this architecture, apart from logarithmic factors and the optimization and bias terms, the bound yields the rate 

ℰ tot ​ ( T ) = 𝒪 ​ ( N FK − γ 2 + 4 ​ γ ) = 𝒪 ​ ( N FK − s − 2 2 ​ d + 4 ​ ( s − 2 ) ) ​ (up to logs) . \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{tot}}(T)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-\frac{\gamma}{2+4\gamma}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-\frac{s-2}{2d+4(s-2)}}\right)\text{ (up to logs)}. 

once N int N_{\mathrm{int}} and N ∂ Ω N_{\partial\Omega} are large enough for the terms m 3 / 2 ​ N int − 1 / 4 m^{3/2}N_{\mathrm{int}}^{-1/4} and m ​ N ∂ Ω − 1 / 4 mN_{\partial\Omega}^{-1/4} to be negligible. 

Corollary D.25 shows that, if the number of FK trajectories N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} were allowed to grow, a depth- 3 3 FK-PINN with optimally chosen width would achieve the rate N FK − ( s − 2 ) / ( 2 ​ d + 4 ​ ( s − 2 ) ) N_{\mathrm{FK}}^{-(s-2)/(2d+4(s-2))} up to logarithmic factors. This matches the asymptotic behaviour proved by Doumèche et al. ( 2025 ) for PINNs with dense physics sampling: in that ideal regime, FK supervision does not worsen the leading statistical exponent. 

Our focus, however, is the genuinely sparse FK regime, where N FK N_{\mathrm{FK}} is fixed while N int N_{\mathrm{int}} and N ∂ Ω N_{\partial\Omega} may grow. In this setting the FK term behaves as an irreducible noise floor: even as N int , N ∂ Ω → ∞ N_{\mathrm{int}},N_{\partial\Omega}\to\infty and T → ∞ T\to\infty , the excess risk cannot be made uniformly small, because it is pinned down by the few noisy FK labels. This loss of uniform guarantees for the excess risk is the real “price” of FK supervision. We accept it in exchange for the PL ∗ geometry and uniform conditioning provided by the FK term (Corollary D.8 ), which yield fast optimization (Proposition D.9 ). How to exploit a fixed FK budget (placement of FK points, choice of λ FK \lambda_{\mathrm{FK}} , etc.) so as to mitigate this statistical saturation while retaining the optimization benefits is an interesting avenue for future work. 

Appendix E Numerical Experiments 

We now illustrate our theoretical results by a series of numerical experiments. 

E.1 Equations Considered 

We begin by specifying the concrete equations used to benchmark the proposed FK-PINNs framework. As discussed earlier, our focus is on regimes where traditional PINNs often become inaccurate or unstable, due to difficulties such multiscale coupling, singular perturbations, high frequencies etc. in the PDE data, leading to extremely stiff loss landscapes. Besides the Schrödinger equation introduced in the main text, we also consider the three following representative classes of problems: 

E.1.1 Poisson Equation 

We consider a prototypical elliptic bounded value problem on a bounded domain Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} with boundary ∂ Ω \partial\Omega : 

− Δ ​ u ​ ( x ) = f ​ ( x ) x ∈ Ω , -\Delta u(x)=f(x)\qquad x\in\Omega, (E.1) 

with Dirichlet boundary conditions 

u ​ ( x ) = g ​ ( x ) x ∈ ∂ Ω . u(x)=g(x)\qquad x\in\partial\Omega. 

Here u ​ ( x ) u(x) denotes the stationary potential or steady-state field generated by a source term f ​ ( x ) f(x) . In multiscale settings, f ​ ( x ) f(x) and/or the solution u ​ ( x ) u(x) can exhibit sharp layers and small-scale structures, which pose challenges for standard PINNs due to spectral bias. 

E.1.2 Mean Escape Time 

Consider an overdamped diffusion process in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , 

d ​ X t = b ​ ( X t ) ​ d ​ t + σ ​ ( X t ) ​ d ​ W t , \mathrm{d}X_{t}=b(X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}, (E.2) 

and a bounded domain Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} with boundary ∂ Ω \partial\Omega . The mean first exit time (or mean escape time) τ ​ ( x ) \tau(x) from Ω \Omega solves the elliptic PDE 

ℒ ​ τ ​ ( x ) = − 1 , x ∈ Ω , \mathcal{L}\,\tau(x)=-1,\qquad x\in\Omega, (E.3) 

with boundary condition 

τ ​ ( x ) = 0 , x ∈ ∂ Ω , \tau(x)=0,\qquad x\in\partial\Omega, 

where ℒ \mathcal{L} is the infinitesimal generator 

ℒ ​ ϕ = − ∇ V ⋅ ∇ ϕ + β − 1 ​ Δ ​ ϕ \mathcal{L}\phi=-\nabla V\cdot\nabla\phi+\beta^{-1}\Delta\phi (E.4) 

where β \beta is inverse temperature. The function τ ​ ( x ) \tau(x) quantifies how long, on average, trajectories starting from x x remain inside Ω \Omega . In metastable or small-noise regimes, τ ​ ( x ) \tau(x) can vary over exponentially large scales, leading to stiff elliptic problems characterized by sharp boundary layers and high-contrast magnitudes that are challenging for standard PINNs. 

E.1.3 Committor Function 

For the same diffusion process 

d ​ X t = b ​ ( X t ) ​ d ​ t + σ ​ ( X t ) ​ d ​ W t , \mathrm{d}X_{t}=b(X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}, (E.5) 

let A , B ⊂ ℝ d A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} be two disjoint sets, and define Ω = ℝ d ∖ ( A ∪ B ) \Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(A\cup B) . The committor function q ​ ( x ) q(x) is the probability that a trajectory starting at x x reaches B B before A A . It is characterized as the solution of the elliptic boundary value problem 

ℒ ​ q ​ ( x ) = 0 x ∈ Ω , \mathcal{L}\,q(x)=0\ \ \ \ \ x\in\Omega, (E.6) 

with boundary conditions 

q ​ ( x ) = 0 x ∈ A , q ​ ( x ) = 1 x ∈ B , q(x)=0\ \ \ \ x\in A,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ q(x)=1\ \ \ \ x\in B, 

where ℒ \mathcal{L} is the same generator as above. The committor encodes the effective reaction coordinate in transition path theory and large deviation theory, typically exhibiting sharp interfaces between metastable basins. Such boundary layers and rare-event structures are difficult to resolve with conventional PINNs, especially under small-noise conditions. 

E.2 Evaluation of FK-PINNs against baseline PINNs 

In this section, we evaluate our proposed FK-PINNs by testing them on the aforementioned PDEs, and comparing how they perform against standard PINNs as baseline. This choice is to isolate the effect of the Feynman-Kac supervision terms on the training dynamics. 

E.2.1 Implementation details 

Neural Network Architectures. All neural networks (baseline and FK-PINNs) used for these experiments are fully connected tanh \tanh networks with 4 hidden layers, and 128 neurons per layer. 

Training Schedule. All neural networks (baseline and FK-PINNs) used for these experiments are trained using the Adam optimizer for 30 , 000 30,000 iterations, followed by 15 , 000 15,000 iterations of L-BFGS to fine-tune the solution. For the Adam phase, we implement a step-based exponential decay schedule, where the initial learning rate of 10 − 3 10^{-3} is decayed every k k iterations by a factor of γ = exp ⁡ ( ln ⁡ ( 10 − 3 ) / ( N / k ) ) \gamma=\exp(\ln(10^{-3})/(N/k)) , reaching a final learning rate of 10 − 6 10^{-6} . 

Feynman-Kac data supervision. To generate the FK “data points”, we use Algorithm 1 and set Δ ​ t = 10 − 3 \Delta t=10^{-3} , N MC = 500 N_{\mathrm{MC}}=500 , and T max = ∞ T_{\max}=\infty (we simulate the trajectories until escape). As for the number of FK points, we first choose a number N coll N_{\mathrm{coll}} of collocation points, and fix a proportion p data ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p_{\mathrm{data}}\in(0,1) of randomly selected points for which we compute the FK label. We thus define 

N FK := ⌊ p data ​ N coll ⌋ , N int := N coll − N FK . N_{\mathrm{FK}}:=\big\lfloor p_{\mathrm{data}}\,N_{\mathrm{coll}}\big\rfloor,\quad N_{\mathrm{int}}:=N_{\mathrm{coll}}-N_{\mathrm{FK}}. (E.7) 

We fix p data = 0.02 p_{\mathrm{data}}=0.02 for all experiments in E.2 . We will further explore the effects of different N MC N_{\mathrm{MC}} , Δ ​ t \Delta t , and p data p_{\mathrm{data}} on the experimental results in E.5 . 

Metrics. To evaluate the performance of the models, we use the L 2 L^{2} and H 1 H^{1} error metrics in both absolute and relative forms. 

L 2 L^{2} absolute error: L abs 2 ​ ( f θ ) = 1 N ​ ∑ n = 1 N | f θ ​ ( x n ) − u ref ​ ( x n ) | 2 \displaystyle L_{\text{abs}}^{2}(f_{\theta})=\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left|f_{\theta}(x_{n})-u_{\text{ref}}(x_{n})\right|^{2}} (E.8) 

L 2 L^{2} relative error: L rel 2 ​ ( f θ ) = 1 N ​ ∑ n = 1 N | f θ ​ ( x n ) − u ref ​ ( x n ) | 2 1 N ​ ∑ n = 1 N | u ref ​ ( x n ) | 2 \displaystyle L_{\text{rel}}^{2}(f_{\theta})=\frac{\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left|f_{\theta}(x_{n})-u_{\text{ref}}(x_{n})\right|^{2}}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left|u_{\text{ref}}(x_{n})\right|^{2}}} (E.9) 

H 1 H^{1} absolute error: H abs 1 ​ ( f θ ) = 1 N ​ ∑ n = 1 N | f θ ​ ( x n ) − u ref ​ ( x n ) | 2 + ‖ ∇ f θ ​ ( x ) − ∇ u ref ​ ( x ) ‖ 2 \displaystyle H_{\text{abs}}^{1}(f_{\theta})=\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left|f_{\theta}(x_{n})-u_{\text{ref}}(x_{n})\right|^{2}+\left\|\nabla f_{\theta}(x)-\nabla u_{\text{ref}}(x)\right\|^{2}} (E.10)

E.2.2 Poisson Equation 

In this example, we solve the Poisson equation on the square Ω = [ 0 , 1 ] × [ 0 , 1 ] \Omega=[0,1]\times[0,1] , with source term given by 

f ​ ( x 1 , x 2 ) = π 2 ​ [ 51 ​ sin ⁡ ( π ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( 4 ​ π ​ x 2 ) + 156 ​ sin ⁡ ( 5 ​ π ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( π ​ x 2 ) + 24 ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ x 2 ) + 244 ​ sin ⁡ ( 5 ​ π ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( 6 ​ π ​ x 2 ) ] , f(x_{1},x_{2})=\pi^{2}[51\sin(\pi x_{1})\sin(4\pi x_{2})+156\sin(5\pi x_{1})\sin(\pi x_{2})+24\sin(2\pi x_{1})\sin(2\pi x_{2})+244\sin(5\pi x_{1})\sin(6\pi x_{2})], 

And exact solution u ∗ u^{*} : 

u ∗ ​ ( x 1 , x 2 ) = 3 ​ sin ⁡ ( π ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( 4 ​ π ​ x 2 ) + 6 ​ sin ⁡ ( 5 ​ π ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( π ​ x 2 ) + 3 ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ x 2 ) + 4 ​ sin ⁡ ( 5 ​ π ​ x 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( 6 ​ π ​ x 2 ) . u^{*}(x_{1},x_{2})=3\sin(\pi x_{1})\sin(4\pi x_{2})+6\sin(5\pi x_{1})\sin(\pi x_{2})+3\sin(2\pi x_{1})\sin(2\pi x_{2})+4\sin(5\pi x_{1})\sin(6\pi x_{2}). (E.12) 

We set N coll = 10000 N_{\mathrm{coll}}=10000 interior collocation points, and ( N bc = 400 N_{\mathrm{bc}}=400 ) boundary collocation points. 

Figure 4 : The ground truth solution ( C ​ o ​ l ​ .1 Col.1 ), predicted 2D,3D results by PINNs ( C ​ o ​ l ​ .2 Col.2 ), 2D,3D absolute error by PINNs ( C ​ o ​ l ​ .3 Col.3 ), predicted 2D,3D results by FK-PINNs ( C ​ o ​ l ​ .4 Col.4 ), 2D,3D absolute error by FK-PINNs ( C ​ o ​ l ​ .5 Col.5 ) on Poisson equations. 

Figure 4 compares the performance of PINNs and FK-PINNs on this Poisson problem with high-frequency oscillations. While both models capture the global solution profile, PINNs exhibit significant absolute errors (up to 9.0), particularly near the oscillatory peaks and boundaries. In contrast, by leveraging the “extra data”, FK-PINNs effectively mitigate spectral bias, achieving a much finer reconstruction with absolute errors nearly an order of magnitude lower. 

Figure 5 likewise demonstrates that FK-PINNs achieve superior convergence stability and a significantly lower PDE loss compared to standard PINNs, effectively mitigating the optimization stagnation observed in the vanilla baseline. 

Figure 5 : Comparison of PDE loss, BC loss of PINNs versus PDE loss, BC loss, Data loss of FK-PINNs for Poisson equation 

E.2.3 Mean Escape Time 

We set the domain Ω \Omega as the regular hexagon centered at the origin with circumradius R = 2 R=2 . We set V V as a double-well potential function: 

V ​ ( x 1 , x 2 ) = 1 4 ​ ( x 1 2 − 1 ) 2 + α 2 ​ x 2 2 , V\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)=\frac{1}{4}(x_{1}^{2}-1)^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}x_{2}^{2}, (E.13) 

where α = 1 \alpha=1 . The corresponding Mean Escape Time PDE is then given by: 

− ∇ V ⋅ ∇ τ + β − 1 ​ Δ ​ τ = − 1 , -\nabla V\cdot\nabla\tau+\beta^{-1}\Delta\tau=-1, (E.14) 

with inverse temperature β = 5 \beta=5 and zero Dirichlet boundary condition. 

Figure 6 : Comparison of Mean Escape Time PDE solutions learned by PINNs (left) and FK-PINNs(right) 

Figure 6 shows the solutions learned both by PINNs and FK-PINNs. We see that PINNs (Cols. 2–3) exhibit catastrophic failure on this task, and completely fail to produce a meaningful solution. FK-PINNs (Cols. 4–5), on the other hand, successfully learn the solution and manage to capture the transition layers near the boundary, despite the corner singularities. 

Figure 7 : Comparing the evolution of loss components for solving the Mean Escape Time problem between PINNs and FK-PINNs 

The training loss evolutions in Figure 7 reveal that standard PINNs encounter significant optimization stagnation after the initial phase, seemingly getting stuck around a spurious local minimum for the PDE loss. We see on the other hand that FK-PINNs manage to break through the poorly conditioned loss landscape and converge to the true solution. 

E.2.4 Committor Function 

We now report the performance of FK-PINNs on the committor function problem on the domain Ω = [ − 1.5 , 1.2 ] × [ − 0.2 , 2 ] \Omega=[-1.5,1.2]\times[-0.2,2] with Müller-Brown potential, which is a standard benchmark in transition path studies (E and Vanden-Eijnden, 2006 ; Vanden-Eijnden and others, 2010 ) . It is defined as the sum of four exponentials: 

V ​ ( x , y ) = ∑ k = 1 4 A k ​ exp ⁡ [ a k ​ ( x − x k 0 ) 2 + b k ​ ( x − x k 0 ) ​ ( y − y k 0 ) + c k ​ ( y − y k 0 ) 2 ] V(x,y)=\sum_{k=1}^{4}A_{k}\exp\left[a_{k}(x-x_{k}^{0})^{2}+b_{k}(x-x_{k}^{0})(y-y_{k}^{0})+c_{k}(y-y_{k}^{0})^{2}\right] (E.15) 

The specific coefficients used to define the potential energy surface in this work are provided in Table 3 . These parameters ensure the presence of the characteristic “three-well” structure often used to benchmark transition path sampling and committor function approximations. 

We further define two metastable states, A A and B B , which correspond to the reactant and product regions, respectively. These states are represented as circular boundary regions Ω ¯ A \bar{\Omega}_{A} and Ω ¯ B \bar{\Omega}_{B} with a fixed radius of r = 0.2 r=0.2 . The centers of these disks, 𝐜 A \mathbf{c}_{A} and 𝐜 B \mathbf{c}_{B} , are positioned at the primary local minima of the potential: 

• 
State A A (Reactant): 𝐜 A = ( − 0.5582 , 1.4417 ) \mathbf{c}_{A}=(-0.5582,1.4417) , satisfying the boundary condition q ​ ( 𝐱 ) = 0 q(\mathbf{x})=0 . 


• 
State B B (Product): 𝐜 B = ( 0.6235 , 0.0281 ) \mathbf{c}_{B}=(0.6235,0.0281) , satisfying the boundary condition q ​ ( 𝐱 ) = 1 q(\mathbf{x})=1 . 


The domain for the committor equation is thus defined as Ω ′ := Ω ∖ ( Ω ¯ A ∪ Ω ¯ B ) \Omega^{\prime}:=\Omega\setminus(\bar{\Omega}_{A}\cup\bar{\Omega}_{B})

Figure 9 : Comparison of the convergence behavior of individual loss components in PINNs and FK-PINNs trained with Adam optimizer only 

As highlighted in Figure 8 , the PINN solution fails to meaningfully capture the transition regions and the dynamics induced by the loss landscape, unlike FK-PINNs which recover a fully satisfactory solution. The improved training dynamics are also apparent in Figure 9 .
